                - The foundation of Mysore power were laid by Hyder Ali, which was later consolidated by his able son, Tipu Sultan

    - Hyder Ali
        - Rise to Power
            - He began his career as a petty officer in the Mysore Army.
            - Though Uneducated, he was a man of great energy and intellect.
            - He gradually rose in the Mysore Army to be a brilliant commander.
            - In 1761, Hyder overthrew Nanjaraja and established authority over Mysore.
            - He established his control over rebellious poligars (Zamindars) and conquered Bindur, Malabar etc.
        - Military and Expansionist Ambitions
            - He was an expansionist at heart and naturally clashed with other regional powers - Maratha, Nizam and the English.
            - Faced Maratha raids in 1764, 1766, and again in 1771 as the Marathas recovered fast under Peshwa Madhav Rao.
            - He also inflicted a heavy defeat on the English near Madras in 1769.
            - His most remarkable achievement was his vision to modernise his army along the European lines and this way he had the best standing army among the regional powers of India.
                - He inducted French Experts and established a modern Arsenal at Dindigul.
                - Formed the first Indian controlled corps of sepoys armed with firelocks, bayonets, and backed by artillery served by Europeans
            - Hyder died during the course of the second Anglo Mysore War (1782) and was succeeded by his son, Tipu Sultan.

    - Tipu Sultan
        - Assertion of Autonomy
            - Afer assuming the throne, he strongly asserted his autonomy.
                - In 1787, he assumed the title of Padshah, issued coins in his own name with any reference to the Mughal Emperor.
                - He even issued a new Calendar.
                - He also sought a Sanad (Royal order) from the Ottoman Khalif to legitimize his rule.
            - Being a realist, he recognised Mughal authority when it suited him and defied it when it did not.
        - Administration and Welfare
            - For the General Public - he took great care for the welfare and happiness of his people.
            - More than any other 18th century Indian ruler, Tipu thoroughly recognised the threat that the English posed to South India and thus raised an efficient military force, employed French officers to train his troops and even raised a French corps, Made efforts to build modern Navy in 1796.
            - Economically - He was very sound and understood the importance of modern trade and Industry.
            - Hence promoted both Inland and Foreign Trade but imposed tight government regulations and control over it.
                - Set up a number of Factories in Mysore Territory such as those of sugar, silk fabrics etc.
                - Sent Emissaries to France, Turkey, Iran, Myanmar etc.
                - Tried to set up trading companies on the European Lines - which did not fructify,
                    - Pioneered iron cased rocket technology,
                - Worked for modernisation of Agriculture and introduced sericulture in Mysore.
            - Under his Administration, He acted like a despotic one - Himself being the civil. Political and military authority of the State.
            - There was no office of Wazir in his office but there were separate departments each under a different officer such as Military department, Finance department etc.
        - Personal Interests and Legacy
            - He was an avid learner and had a library with books on different subjects.
            - Tipu showed keen interest in the French Revolution and is believed to have planted the ʻTree of Libertyʼ at Seringapatam, became a member of the Jacobian Club and allowed himself to be addressed as Citizen Tipu.
                - His famous Line - “Better to live a day as a lion than a lifetime as a Sheep”.
            - He ruled Mysore till his death at the hands of the British in 1799 - where he died defending his capital during the course of Fourth Anglo Mysore War.

    - British Expansion in South India
        - Threats to British Interests
            - Hyder and Tipuʼs dominance over the rich trade of Malabar Coast was seen as the threat to the English Companyʼs Trade in pepper and cardamom.
                - In 1785, Tipu declared an embargo on export of pepper and cardamom through the ports within his kingdom.
                - In 1788, he clearly forbade dealings with English traders.
            - Thus Mysore was also seen as a threat to English control over Madras.
            - Moreover, The French Alliance with Mysore was seen as a threat to English dominance.
            - The British home government was in need of finances for growing expenditure in Napoleonic wars, and so it encouraged an aggressive expansionist policy of the Companyʼs merchants.

    - Anglo Mysore Wars (1767 – 1799)
        - Overview
            - The rise of Mysore under Hyder Ali was viewed as a threat by the neighbouring states - the Marathas - The Nawabs of Carnatic and The Nizam of Hyderabad.
            - The Marathas and the Nizam formed an alliance against Mysore and also co-operated with the English Company to curb Mysore.
            - But the Nizam was equally apprehensive of the Marathas and therefore cooperated with the English company against the Marathas also.
        - First Anglo Mysore War (1767 – 1769)
            - Hyder Aliʼs friendship with French alongside the Mysore border dispute with Nawab of Carnatic is considered as one of the main reasons for this war.
            - The immediate context of war was provided by the rivalry over control of Northern Circars.
            - This part was under the Nizam of Hyderabad and The English Company was seeking an overland connection between its territories in Madras and Bengal, and the Northern Circars was a series of coastal territories that lay in between.
            - The Nizam of Hyderabad initially rejected the British demand, however later signed a treaty with the Company in view of his poor financial condition.
            - As per this treaty, the Company got the Northern Circars and in return promised to give military support to the Nizam in his endeavours against Hyder Ali.
            - At the same time, Nizam had formed an alliance with the Marathas as both felt threatened by the expansionist attitude of Hyder Ali.
            - Hyder himself was annoyed with the English as they had set up a fortified outpost at Vellore.
            - In this way, The Marathas, The Nizam and the English entered into a tripartite treaty against Hyder Ali and attacked Mysore in 1766.
            - Hyder successfully persuaded the Nizam and the Marathas to join hands with him against the English.
            - He then launched an offensive attack against the Company and reached upto the gates of Madras, He forced the Madras Council to sign peace on his terms in 1769, known as the Treaty of Madras.
            - As per the treaty, both the powers agreed to help each other in case of an attack by a third party.
        - Second Anglo Mysore War (1780 – 1784)
            - The British had no intention of abiding by the Madras treaty.
            - When the Marathas invaded Hyderʼs territory in 1771, the English Company did not come to his help.
            - So this feeling of being betrayed laid the foundation of the Second Mysore War, On the other hand the French proved more helpful to Hyder with his military demands.
            - Further, The American War of Independence had broken out in 1775 and the French help to American colonists made Governor General Warren Hastings extremely anxious of Hyderʼs reaction with the French.
            - Under these circumstances, The Company attempted to capture Mahe (A French settlement on the Malabar Coast within Hyderʼs Jurisdiction), which was a direct challenge to Hyder Ali.
                - The English had also offended Hyder Ali when an English force trespassed on Hyderʼs territory in order to take over Guntur in Northern Circars.
            - The English capture of Mahe provided the immediate context for the second Mysore War, The English won over the Marathas and the Nizam (Bribery).
            - In July 1781, The English Army under Sir Eyre Coote defeated Hyder Ali at Porto-Novo.
            - Skirmishes continued for the time being.
            - But later Hyder Ali died of cancer in December 1782 during the course of the second Mysore War.
            - Tipu, son and successor continued the war.
            - However in 1783, The American War of Independence ended, and the French sailed back to Europe leaving Tipu to fight his own battles.
            - The Madras Government also desired peace due to lack of resources and uncertainty of the Maratha attitude.
            - Tipu also needed time to strengthen his own administration.
            - Thus the war ended with the Treaty of Mangalore (March 1784) signed on the basis of mutual restitution of each otherʼs territories.
            - This was the last British treaty with an Indian ruler on an equal footing.
        - Third Anglo Mysore War (1790 – 1792)
            - The above treaty was only a temporary respite before a final showdown.
            - Lord Cornwallis had become the Governor General in 1786 and through his diplomacy he brought the Marathas and The Nizam to the English side.
            - Convinced of the inevitability of the war, Tipu also sought help from the Turks and the French by sending embassies to Constantinople and France during 1784-1787.
            - However, Tipuʼs attack on Travancore became the immediate cause of the Third-Mysore War as the English were already itching for a war and decided to side with the ruler of Travancore.
            - The War started in 1790, supported by the Marathas and the Nizamʼs troops, Cornwallis himself headed a large army and approached Seringapatanam.
            - Tipu suffered a serious setback and showed initiative for peace.
            - Thus, the Treaty of Srirangapatnam was signed in 1792 under which -
                - Tipu had to surrender half of his territory to the Company and his allies.
            - While the Company acquired Baramahal, Dindigul and Malabar
            - The Marathas gained territory on the Tungabhadra side.
            - Nizam acquired territories form the Krishna to beyond the Pennar.
                - Tipu also had to pay war indemnity with Rs. 3 crore, but as he was unable to pay this sum indemnity, his two sons were kept as prisoners with Cornwallis.
            - Afer Cornwallis, Sir John Shore became the Governor General but he adopted the Policy of non-intervention towards Mysore.
            - For this reason, John Shore is known as the Father of policy of non-intervention.
        - Fourth Anglo Mysore War (1799)
            - Lord Wellesly sailed for India in 1797, this was a period when English were struggling against the French all over the world.
            - Napoleon Bonaparte had captured Egypt and Syria and he now turned towards India.
                - In 1801, he made an alliance with Czar Paul of Russia and drew up a serious plan to invade India via Herat and Kandahar.
            - The English realised that the consequences of a defeat at the hands of Napoleon would mean an end to its commerce that had brought immense financial and political gains.
            - In Napoleonʼs plan, Tipu Sultan of Mysore saw the right opportunity to oust the English from India.
            - He entered into negotiations with the French and started making elaborate preparations for war with the Company.
            - He planted a ʻFlag of Libertyʼ at Srirangapatna, styled himself ʻCitizen Tipuʼ.
            - He employed the French Commandant Monsieur Raymond and raised a corps of 14000 men with his help.
            - Wellesly alarmed by this, Tried to convert as many as Indian states into the subsidiary allies of the Company, either by persuasion or by force.
            - War began in February 1799 and was over by May, The English Company won a decisive victory at the Battle of Serinagapatnam.
            - Tipu was killed while defending the city before French help could reach him.
            - He died a heroes death defending the capital.
            - The Company, The Nizam and The Marathas annexed much of the remaining Mysorean territory.
            - The remaining core around Mysore and Serinagapatnam, was restored to the minor Indian prince, two year old Krishnaraj belonging to the Wadiyar Dynasty, whose forefathers had been the actual rulers before Hyder Ali became the defacto ruler.
            - A special Treaty of Subsidiary Alliance was imposed on the Wadiyar King and Mysore virtually became a dependance of the British.
            - Tipuʼs family was imprisoned in Vellore and his two sons pensioned.
            - Afer the success , Wellesley arrogantly pronounced “Now the Kingdom of the East is under our feet”

- Rise of British Supremacy (4) - Anglo Maratha Wars (1777 - 1818)
    - Overview
        - From the ashes of the Mughal Empire had emerged the Marathas and the English East India Company.
        - Both had emerged victorious in their own spheres - The Marathas among other Indian states, The English among other European contenders in India.
        - But now, Towards the last quarter of the 18th century, the two came face to face and indulged in direct clash and conflict.
        - The 1st, 2nd and 3rd Anglo Maratha Wars were fought between the army of the English East India Company, which afer the 1757 was de facto ruler of Bengal, and the Maratha Empire, in the Deccan.
        - Maratha Empire comprised of five major chiefs
            - The Peshwas of Poona
            - Bhonsle of Nagpur
            - Scindia at Gwalior
            - Holkar at Indore
            - Gaekwad at Baroda.
        - Peshwa was the nominal head of this Maratha confederacy and there existed bitter mutual rivalry among all of them, Particularly Scindia and Holkar.
        - Their strength had already been weakened by their defeat in the Third battle of Panipat (1761), in which the Marathas were defeated by the Ahmed Shah Abdali.
        - The wars started in 1777 and ended with British victory in 1818.
        - This lef the British in control, directly or indirectly via treaties of Princely states, of a vast proportion of India, making India the jewel in the crown of the British Empire.
    - First Anglo-Maratha War (1775 - 1782)
        - Background
            - It was a result of the excessive ambition of the English, accentuated by the internal strife of the Marathas.
            - The Bombay Government under the British had its longing eyes upon the Maratha territories, particularly the ports of Salsette and Bassein and hoped to gain political influence at the Poona Court.
            - The dispute over succession to the Peshwaship afer the death of fourth Peshwa Madhav Rao gave the English the much needed opportunity to intervene in the Maratha affairs.
            - Story - Afer Madhav Rao's death in 1772, his brother Narayan Rao succeeded him as the fifh Peshwa.
            - However, Narayan Raoʼs uncle Raghunath Rao got him assassinated and named himself as the next Peshwa, although he was not a legal heir.
                - Narayan Raoʼs widow Gangabai then gave birth to a son afer her Husbands death (Swaii) and he was legally the next Peshwa.
                - Then happened the Barbhai conspiracy under Nana Phadnavis which removed Raghunath Rao from the seat.
        - Treaty of Surat
            - Thus out of frustration, Raghunath Rao went over to the British and tried to capture power with their help.
            - This was the immediate background of the Anglo-Maratha War.
            - The war began with the Treaty of Surat and ended with the Treaty of Salbai.
            - In Raghunath Rao, the Bombay government saw a plaint tool and hoped to set up in Maharashtra the type of Dual Government as Clive had done in Bengal.
            - This treaty was signed between Raghunath Rao and the British Government at Bombay on 7 March 1775.
            - As per the treaty it was decided that the British would help Raghunath Rao in becoming the Peshwa by sending 2500 soldiers.
            - In return, Raghoba would cede territories of Salsette and Bassein and revenues from Surat and Bharauch to the British.
        - Treaty of Surat and Battle of Adas
            - In accordance with the Treaty of Surat, A British Army reached Surat led by Colonel Keating.
            - On 18 May 1775, at Adas (Battle of Adas) there took place a terrible battle between the English and the Maratha Armies.
            - In this battle, though the Marathas were defeated, however they retained control over Poona.
        - The Reversal with Treaty of Purandar
            - When a copy of Treaty of Surat reached the superior British Government in Bengal, it condemned the treaty as unjust and unauthorised.
            - Further, Warren Hastings sent Colonel Upton to Poona Darbar and signed the Treaty of Purandar.
            - Thus the Treaty of Purandar was signed between the British Government at Calcutta and Poona Court and it stated the following.
                - The Treaty of Surat was annulled.
                - The British lef the side of Raghunath and it was decided that Raghunath will be given a monthly pension of Rs 25,000 by Peshwa Government and he will reside in Gujarat.
        - Plot Twist - Treaty of Purandar Not Implemented
            - However, The Treaty of Purandar was not implemented due to a sudden turn of events.
                - The American War of Independence had erupted.
                - In 1775 and 1778, France joined on the American side against England.
            - At the same time, A French adventurer, Chevailer de St. Lubin, reached Poona.
            - This greatly alarmed Warren Hastings, who immediately scrapped the Treaty of Purandar, sent a large force to reinforce Bombay Government and sanctioned operations against the Marathas.
        - Movie Begins - First Anglo-Maratha War
            - Thus the Bombay Government resumed the war and sent an army towards Poona under Colonel Egerton (Later replaced by Colonel Cockburn).
            - The English and Maratha forces met on the outskirts of Poona.
            - The Maratha Army was led by the brilliant General Mahadji Schinde.
            - He lured the English Army into valleys of the Western Ghats near Talegaon (Battle of Talegaon) and trapped them from all side.
            - The Marathas also used the ʻScorched Earth Policyʼ, burning farmlands and poisoning wells.
        - Treaty of Wadgaon
            - The English were forced to retreat to villages named Wadgaon.
            - Finally by mid January 1779, The English surrendered and the Bombay Government was forced to sign a humiliating treaty with Poona Darbar called as Treaty of Wadgaon.
            - A per this treaty, it was decided that Bombay Government would return all territory conquered by it afer 1773 and the Scindias would get a part of revenue from Bharuch.
        - Humiliation Too Much for Hastings
            - Hastings refused to accept this humiliating treaty of Wadgaon.
                - He quickly sent an army from Bengal which soon captured Ahmedabad and Vasai.
                - But the British Army got defeated by Marathas in poona.
                - But meanwhile Gwalior was captured by British and eventually Scndia was seated in the Battle of Sipri.
            - Finally the Treaty of Salbai was signed between the British and the Poona Government.
        - Treaty of Salbai
            - This treaty between the British and The Poona Government was signed with the mediation by the Mahdji Scindia.
            - The treaty was signed on the basis of mutual restitution of each otherʼs territories or ʻstatus quo ante bellumʼ.
            - The main provision of this territory were -
                - Madhav Narayan Rao will be accepted as Peshwa.
                - British will give up the cause of Raghunath Rao and he be given an yearly pension of Rs 3.5 Lakhs.
                - The Company gave up Bassein and other territories captured since the Treaty of Purandar , but retained Salsette and The Elephanta Island.
                - Scinida got back the entire territory on the west of Yamuna.
        - Treaty of Salbai - First War Ends in Draw
            - In this way, the British attempt to gain supremacy in the Marahta state proved premature.
            - The First Anglo Maratha war was drawn.
            - Both sides had a taste of each otherʼs strength which ensured mutual respect and peace for the next 20 years.
            - It also gave British the required time to concentrate on other fronts especially Mysore.
    - Second Anglo-Maratha War (1803 - 1805)
        - Prelude to War
            - Lord Wellesly came to India in 1798 and aggressively followed his policy his subsidiary alliance.
            - The Marathas earlier refused all offers to accept subsidiary alliance, however their internal differences ultimately drove them into Wellesleyʼs trap.
            - Story -
                - Due to internal ongoing politics among the Marathasm Peshwa Baji Rao II murdered the brother of Jaswant Rao Holkar.
                - It led to Holkar forces defeating Sindhia and the Peshwa and installing an adopted brother on the throne of Pune.
                - The Peshwa of Poona sought assistance from Wellesley, resulting in Wellesley singing a treaty on December 31, 1802 known as the Treaty of Bassein.
                - This treaty, essentially a subsidiary alliance with the Peshwa, would ultimately mark the downfall of the Maratha Empire.l)
            - Imp Point - Subsidiary Alliance - Hyderabad (1798), Mysore (1799), Tanjore (1799), Awadh (1801), Peshwa (1802), Scindia (1803), Gaekwad (1803)
        - Treaty of Bassein (Subsidiary Alliance, 31 December 1802)
            - This treaty of ʻperpetual and general allianceʼ was signed between Peshwa Bajirao II and the English Company.
            - It was a treaty of the Subsidiary Alliance.
            - The main provisions of this treaty were -
                - Peshwa accepted the Companyʼs protection and had to pay a large annual subsidy.
                - Peshwa accepted that an English army and Resident will be stationed at Poona and no European enemy of the English will be kept in the Maratha Army.
                - Peshwa accepted that his foreign relations with other states will go under under British control.
                - Also -
                    - To surrender the city of Surat
                    - To give up all claims of Chauth on the Nizamʼs dominion
            - In this way, The Peshwa lost his independent authority and eventually the other Maratha chiefs were reduced to a similar position of subordination to the Company.
                - Company received a native infantry regiment permanently stationed at Poona.
                - City of Surat was surrounded by the British along with other territories.
                - Peshwa gave up all the claims on Chauth on the Nizamʼs dominion.\
                - Peshwa also agreed not to sign any future negotiations with any other power without the consent of East India Company.
                - This treaty, sometimes described as granting the English - “Key to India”
                - According to Sir Thomas Munro, as a result of the Second Anglo Maratha War, the British became the “complete masters of India and nothing can shake our power, if we take proper measure to confirm it”
            - This affected the patriotic feeling of other Maratha chiefs and provoked the Second Anglo - Maratha War that began the breakup of Maratha confederacy.
                - Daulat Rau Sindhia and Raghuji Bhonsle immediately formed an alliance against the English.
                - They tried to include Yashwant Rao Holkar but could not success.
                - Gaekwad remained neutral.
                - In this way, even in times of national danger, the Maratha chiefs did not unite.
        - Course of the War
            - The English decided to attack all Maratha Regions.
            - The War was fought in 2 main centres - in Deccan under Arthur Wellesley and in north India under General Lake.
            - The Maratha Forces due to disunity had to face bitter losses on both sides.
            - It is noteworthy that now the control of Delhi passed from the Marathas into British hands and once again the blind Mughal ruler, Shah Alam II, became a pensioner of the Company (He had first became a pensioner of the Company afer his defeat in the Battle of Buxar in 1764).
            - In this way, Scindia and Bhonsle faced total defeat within five months and had to sign two separate treaties with the English Company.
            - Alarmed by the defeat of Bhonsle and Scinida, This Time Holkars made an attempt to form a coalition of Indian rulers to fight against the British, but all his attempts proved to be unsuccessful, thus the Marathas were defeated, reduced to British vassalage and isolated from one another.
            - Defeat of Bhonsle led to - Treaty of Deogaon
            - Defeat of Scindia led to - Treaty of Surji-Anjangaon
            - Defeat of Holkar - Led to Treaty of Rajpurghat
    - Third Anglo Maratha War (1817 - 1819)
        - Background
            - The third and final phase of the Anglo-Maratha war started with the arrival of British Governor Lord Hastings.
                - He tried to establish the supremacy of English East India Company in India and resumed the threads of aggressive policy abandoned in 1805 with the recall of Wellesley.
            - The breathing space that the Marathas had got since 1805 was not utilised by consolidating their power but wasted in mutual conflicts.
            - Hastingsʼ main objective now was to destroy the independence of the three Maratha rulers - The Bhonsle, The Scindia and the Holkar.
        - Course of the War
            - Peshwa Baji Rao II who had earlier signed the Treaty of Bassein with time, began to feel strangulated and began to show signs of Independence.
            - Also the Treaty of Bassein, described as a Treaty with the Cipher, wounded the feelings of other Maratha Leaders, and they saw this treaty as absolute surrender of Independence.
            - Immediate Cause - Lord Hastingsʼ actions against the Pindaris were seen as a transgression of the sovereignty of the Marathas, there served to once again unite the Maratha confederacy.
                - A repentant Bajirao II made a last bid in 1817 by rallying together the Maratha Chiefs against the English in the course of the Third Anglo Maratha War.
            - But Marathas were now too weak to win against British
            - Different Battles were fought at different end by the British and the various powers of Marathas, however they all were defeated and forced to sign isolated treaties all together (Peshwa was defeated at Khirki, Bhonsle at Sitabuldi and Holkar at Mahidpur)
                - June 1817 - Treaty of Poona with Peshwa
                - November 1817 - Treaty of Gwalior with Scinida
                - January 1818 - Treat of Mandsaur with Holkar
        - Aftermath of Anglo Maratha War
            - In this way, The entire Maratha force was routed by superior military power of EIC.
                - Peshwa was forced to breakup the Maratha confederacy
            - Hastings decided to get rid of both - Peshwa and the Peshwaship.
            - Hence the Peshwa was dethroned, pensioned off and ordered to spend last days of his life in Bithoor near Kanpur.
            - His kingdom was brought under British Control and the enlarged Presidency of Bomday was brought into existence.
            - To satisfy the Maratha pride, a small kingdom of Satara was carved out of Peshwaʼs kingdom and given to Partap Singh, a direct descendant of Shivaji.
            - Thus by 1818, The entire Indian subcontinent except Sindh and Punjab had been brought under British Control, directly or indirectly.
        - Why Marathas Lost?
            - Inept Leadership
                - (Maratha leaders like Baji Rao II lacked the strategic vision and unity of earlier figures like Shivaji, often prioritizing personal gain over collective resistance, weakening their response to British aggression.)
            - Defective Nature of Maratha State
                - (The Maratha Confederacy’s decentralized structure, with autonomous chiefs like Scindia and Holkar, fostered rivalry rather than cooperation, undermining a unified front against the British.)
            - Loose Political Setup
                - (The lack of a strong central authority after the Third Battle of Panipat left the Marathas politically fragmented, making it easier for the British to exploit divisions through diplomacy and treaties.)
            - Inferior Military System
                - (Despite early adoption of European tactics, the Marathas failed to modernize consistently, relying on outdated cavalry against the British’s disciplined infantry and artillery.)
            - Unstable Economic Policy
                - (Frequent internal conflicts and reliance on plunder rather than a stable revenue system drained Maratha resources, leaving them financially unable to sustain prolonged wars against the well-funded EIC.)
            - Superior English Diplomacy and Espionage
                - (The British, under leaders like Wellesley and Hastings, masterfully used treaties like Bassein and espionage to turn Maratha factions against each other, securing allies like the Nizam and isolating opponents.)
            - Progressive English Outlook
                - (The British leveraged industrial advancements and organizational efficiency, contrasting with the Marathas’ traditional feudal approach, giving them an edge in logistics, communication, and sustained warfare.)
    - War Years Summary
        - First Anglo-Maratha War 1775-1782
            - A conflict over the Maratha succession following the death of the Maratha Peshwa in 1772.
            - The British East India Company initially supported Raghunath Rao's claim to the throne, leading to war with the Maratha Empire.
            - The conflict ended with the Treaty of Salbai, maintaining the status quo.
        - Second Anglo-Maratha War 1803-1805
            - This war was triggered by the expansionist policies of the British East India Company and territorial disputes.
            - The British achieved significant victories and imposed the Treaty of Bassein, greatly increasing their influence over the Maratha states.
        - Third Anglo-Maratha War 1817-1819
            - The final war led to the end of the Maratha Empire and the British East India Company taking control of most of India.
            - Triggered by the Maratha Peshwa's defiance of the British, the war concluded with the British victory and the absorption of Maratha territories into the British realm.

- Rise of British Supremacy (5) - Annexation of Punjab and Sindh
    - Conquest of Sindh (1843)
        - Background
            - In the 18th century, Sindh was ruled by Kallora chiefs, in 1758, an English Factory was built at Thatta, owing to a royal order given by the chief.
                - This advantage was taken by the English up to 1775 when a not-too-friendly ruler, Sarfaraz Khan, made the English close their factory.
            - In 1780’s, Sindh was captured by Talpuras, a Baloch Tribe, led by Mir Fateh Ali Khan.
            - After his death, it came to be shared by his 4 brothers, Known as ‘Char Yar’ who called themselves the Amirs of Sind.
        - Context of Anglo-French Rivalry
            - After 1807, The Anglo French rivalry shifted to North West.
            - After the French defeat in naval conflict, Napoleon planned to invade India via land route.
            - Alarmed by this, various diplomatic missions were sent to the frontier states and likewise Selton mission was sent to Sindh by Lord Minto, with the help of these diplomatic missions, friendly treaties were concluded with the respective governments to counter French Challenge.
            - Thus, in 1809, The Treaty of Eternal Friendship was signed with the Amirs, who promised not to allow the French to settle in Sindh.
            - In 1820, the treaty was renewed further to exclude Americans also from Sindh.
            - But soon the commercial and navigational value of the Indus began to attract the attention of the Company’s authorities, therefore a new treaty was signed in 1832 between the Sindh and the English (Lord Bentick) according to which -
                - Amirs allowed a free passage to English travellers and merchants thorough Sindh and also allowed the use of Indus for commercial pursuits.
                - No English merchant would settle down in Sindh, and passports would be needed for travellers.
            - During the Great Game, Sindh was seen from a larger perspective of defence for Indian from the Russian threat.
            - Therefore, Sindh was a necessary prelude to annexation of Afghanistan.
            - Thus Sindh was conquered to counter Russia.
            - Soon Ranjit Singh captured Rojhan, a town on the Sindh frontier giving the Company a chance to offer protection to the Amirs.
            - The Amirs had neither desired nor asked for foreign help, yet they were forced to sign a Subsidiary Treaty in 1839 and accept a British Resident at Hyderabad.
            - Therefore the Amirs had virtually passed under British protection.
        - Context of Afghan War
            - During the Afghan War, Amirs found themselves saddled with the responsibility of helping the British forces, Parts of their territory were taken up for this and also they had to donate large amounts of money in order to maintain the troops which in reality they did not want.
            - In 1842, when Ellenborough succeeded Auckland, he was eager to find a pretext for the annexation of Sindh and in doing so, regain the English prestige which had suffered during the first Afghan War.
            - In September 1842, Chalres Napier was appointed as British Resident in Sindh.
            - He charged the Amirs for hostility and disaffection towards the British Government and therefore Sindh was annexed.
            - After all this he said - ‘We have no right to seize Sindh, but we shall do so and a very advantageous, useful, humane, piece of rascality it will be’.
            - Sindh was finally annexed in 1843 after a brief campaign (Battle of Miani and Dabo) led by Sir Charles Napier.
            - Napier called the Sindh war as ‘The tail of the Afghan Storm’.
            - In 1847, Sindh was made part of British India’s Bombay Presidency.
            - Please note - British annexation of Sindh in the 19th century was also intricately linked to the control of opium trade.

    - Conquest of Punjab (Anglo Sikh Wars)
        - Background - Punjab under Ranjit Singh
            - In 1799, Ranjit Singh was recognised as the Governor of Lahore by Zaman Shah, the ruler of Afghanistan.
            - In 1801, He proclaimed himself as the Maharaja of Punjab and by 1805, Ranjit Singh had acquired Jammu and Amritsar, making him the ruler of Punjab.
            - Due to the Russian French Threat, Metcalfe was sent to Punjab to conclude a favourable treaty with Ranjit Singh which was done on the condition that English would remain neutral in case of Sikh Afghan War and would consider Ranjit Singh the sovereign of the entire Punjab.
            - However the negotiations failed, but with time he agreed to sign the Treaty of Amritsar (April 25,1809) with the Company.
                - Sutlej as the boundary line for his dominions.
            - In 1838, Ranjit Singh was coerced into singing the Tripartiate Treaty with the English and Shah Shuja in oder to invade Afghanistan and install Shah Shuja as the ruler.
            - However, Ranjit Singh declined to allow British Troops.
        - Punjab after Ranjit Singh
            - Maharaja Ranjit Singh died in June 1839, His death was followed by political instability. (Sukerchakia Misl)
            - He left behind a military state which was guarded by 40,000 soldiers and the strength of this army increased three fold within 5 years of his death which proved to be a great burden on the shrinking resources of the state.
            - When the soldiers could not be paid properly, they went out of control and interfered in politics.
            - Alongside, death of his successors one after the another proved to be another big issue which deteriorated the affairs of the state, and the incompetent heirs were unable to check the forces of disorder.
            - In 1843, Dalip Singh, a minor son of Maharaja, was proclaimed king under the guardianship of Rani Jindan.
            - English had long been casting their eyes over the waters and the rich fertile lands of Punjab and waited for the right opportunity to interfere and moreover only the Punjab was the only dominion left in the entire India subcontinent which was outside their domination.
            - In 1843, Major Broadfoot was appointed as Company’s political agent in Punjab.
            - He stressed on the disorder in Punjab and recounted every tale of corrupt behaviour at the court.
            - In 1844, Lord Ellenborough was succeeded by Lord Hardinge as Governor-General, he undertook various rigorous measures to strengthen the Company’s military position.
            - The Company took a hypocritical stand and contended that the preparation were only defensive in nature and calculated to meet a possible attack from the Sikhs so that they could shift the responsibility of war on the shoulders of the Sikhs.
            - Rani Jindan dreaded the absolute power of the Khalsa army and found her only hope of security in urging it on to challenge British supremacy.
            - In 1845, Lal Singh, a lover of Rani Jindan, had won over the army on his side and became the wazir and made Teja Singh the new commander of the Sikh forces.
            - To the Sikhs, The British moves appeared more as an act of aggression rather than defence.
            - Therefore the Sikh troops led by Lal Singh, crossed the Satluj and took offensive actin against the English troops commanded by Sir Hugh Gough.
            - On 13 December, Hardinge, waiting for the opportunity, made his declaration of war.
        - First Anglo Sikh War (1845-46)
            - Five successive battles were fought between the British and the Sikh Army.
            - These were the battles of Mudki, Ferozshah, Baddowal and Aliwal and Sabroan
            - The fifth and the most decisive battle - Battle of Sobraon was fought on 10 February.
            - Due to treachery by Lal Singh and Teja Singh, ,who gave critical information to the British, the battle resulted in defeat of Sikhs, after which the British occupied Lahore and forced the Sikhs to sign the Treaty of Lahore which meant -
            - Maharaja gave up all these territories south of River Satluj.
            - To pay Rs. 1 crore war indemnity, and upon its failure in order to compensate British sold Kashmir to Gulab Singh for Rs 75 Lakhs.
            - Minor Dalip Singh was accepted as king with Queen Jindan as guardian and Lal Singh was Wazir.
            - Sir Henry Lawrence became the British Resident at Lahore.
            - Sikhs disliked Kashmir begin sold to Gulab Singh and once again revolted under the leadership of Lal Singh, the revolt was suppressed by the British and once again Treaty of Bharioval was signed which included -
                - Rani Jindan’s guardianship was cancelled and she was pensioned off.
                - A Council of 8 Sikh Chiefs under the Chairmanship of British Resident was appointed for governance work.
                - It was decided that a permanent British Army will be placed at Lahore until Dalip Singh became a major.
        - Second Anglo Sikh War (1848 - 1849)
            - After the Treaty of Bhairowal, The British Resident became the virtual ruler of Punjab with unlimited civil and military powers.
            - When Rani Jindan showed her resentment towards such usurpation of powers, her jewellery was seized and she was sent to Sheikhpura.
            - Her pension was also arbitrarily reduced.
            - The New Governor General Dalhousie soon got his opportunity for annexation when 2 British officers, Vans Angew and Lt. Anderson were murdered and other Sikh chiefs joined hands together in order to refer against the British.
            - Thus Dalhousie declared war and three battles were fought
                - Battle of Ramnagar - Indecisive
                - Battle of Chillianwala - Indecisive
                - Battle of Gujarat - Sikh Army was defeated due to treachery by its officials - Battle of Guns.
        - Conquest of Punjab
            - Once the Sikh army collapsed, Dalhousie decided in favour of annexation declaring that “There never can be now any guarantee for the tranquility of India, until we shall have affected the entire subjugation of the Sikh People and destroyed its power as an Independent Nation”
            - Hence on 29 March, 1849, Dalhousie annexed Punjab.
            - Maharaja Dalip Singh was pensioned off and sent to England.
            - Kohinoor Diamond was taken away from Dalip Singh and surrendered to the British Crown.
                - For Administration, a 3 member board was nullified and Punjab was placed under a chief commissioner.
                - In 1853, The board was nullified and Punjab was placed under a chief commissioner.
                    - John Lawrence became the first chief Commissioner.


- British Expansion Beyond Borders
    - Anglo Nepal War
        - Overview
            - The British wanted protection from the Chinese in the North, therefore they looked at Nepal as a stable buffer state between India and Tibet or China.
            - However, the Gorkhas also wanted to expand their territories in the south (As they were unable to do it in the Chinese territory of the North), so in May 1814, they attacked three police stations at Butwal, then under the EICʼs authority.
            - Governor General Hasting took it as a challenge to Companyʼs authority and decided to launch an offensive campaign against them.
            - This led to the Nepal War in October 1814, finally the Nepalis were defeated and forced to sign the Treaty of Sugauli (March 1816).
                - Nepal accepted a British Resident.
                - Nepal ceded the territories of Garhwal, Kumaon and abandoned claims to Terai region.
                - Nepal also withdrew from Sikkim
                - The British gained the districts of Garhwal and Kumaon and a resident was placed at Kathmandu and even they allowed no diplomatic mission, other than that of Britain into Nepal , but in return they treated the Nepalese ruler as sovereign.

    - British Expansion beyond Borders - Burma
        - First Anglo Burmese War (1824 - 1826)
            - Immediate Cause
                - In 1823-24, The Burmese Military, in its pursuit for rebels, entered and occupied Assam and Manipur and prepared to attack Chittagong.
                - These territories were, however, claimed by the English in India.
                - The British Indian Army attacked Burma in retaliation, which came to be known as the First Anglo Burmese War.
            - Course and Outcome
                - In 1824, The British Indian army attacked Burma.
                - The Burmese forces led by Maha Bandula were easily defeated and the King was forced to sign a peace treaty, Treaty of Yandabo.
                - As per the Treaty, The English regained control over Assam, Manipur and occupied Arakan and Tenasserim region, Thus the King lost most of his coast.
                - The King was forced to accept a British resident at his capital and pay war indemnity
        - Second Anglo Burmese War (1852)
            - Background
                - In 1839, an English resident claimed that the Burmese Officials did not treat him well and lef.
                - Meanwhile, Burma emerged as a major supplier of timber and other costly items like tin, pepper and ivory.
                    - The delta of Irrawaddy river also emerged as a major supplier of rice to Bengal and to the growing settlements in south-east Asia.
                - These developments tempted the British to look for an opportunity to annex Burma.
            - Immediate Context
                - Two British captains were heavily fined by Burmese Government on certain charges brought against them.
                - Following the incident, the British merchants sent a petition to then Governor General Lord Dalhousie, providing the desired opportunity.
                - Thus in 1852, Dalhousie decided to attack Burma on the pretext of restoring the dignity of the English.
                - Finally the Burmese were defeated, territories annexed gaining the entire coast of Bay of Bengal and the Burmese King was forced to shif his capital to Mandalay.
        - Third Anglo Burmese War (1885)
            - It took place in 1885 when the English attacked Burma on the pretext that the King was intriguing with the French in Vietnam to weaken English control over Asia.
            - The war ended in English victory and annexation of Upper Burma, thus Burma was turned into a province of British India (1937 onwards, Burma was governed as a separate colony)

    - British Expansion beyond Borders - Afghanistan
        - Afghanistan - The Russian Threat
            - The 19th century was a period of diplomatic competition between the British and Russian empires for spheres of influence in Asia known as ʻthe Great Gameʼ to the British and the ʻTournament of Shadowsʼ to the Russians.
            - Thus, any Russian advance in Central Asia was always assumed in London to be directed towards the conquest of India.
            - In 1832, British had expanded its franchise which was condemned by Emperor Nicolas I of Russia.
            - This set the stage for ʻcold war between autocratic Russia and democratic Britainʼ.
            - In 1837, British fearing the instability of Afghanistan, The Sindh and the increasing power of the Sikh Kingdom in the northwest, raised the spectre of a possible Russian invasion of British India through Afghanistan, the only entrance to India from Central Asia.
        - Afghanistan - British Reaction to Russian Threat
            - Forward Policy - One reaction was to secure commanding influence in Afghanistan, either by Annexation or by creating it as a buffer stage. (View held by conservatives)
            - Policy of Masterly Inactivity (Also known as ʻStationary Schoolʼ) - Another reaction was to reach a diplomatic understanding with Russia and follow as policy of non-intervention as far as Afghanistan was concerned. (View held by Liberals).
        - First Anglo Afghan War (1838 - 1842)
            - British Reaction to Russian Threat
                - In 1830s, The British became over anxious to protect Afghanistan leading the the First Anglo Afghan War which proved to be a great disaster for the British.
                - A direct consequence of this was the annexation of Sindh (1843) and Punjab (1849) and made the British Indiaʼs frontier directly co-terminus with that of Afghanistan.
                - The concern for imperial defence and search for a scientific border in the north-west brought the English in contact with the Afghans.
                - Afghanistan was considered as a buffer state against the Napoleonic France and Czarist Russia.
                - It was felt that Afghanistan should be under the control of a friendly prince.
                - Initially, the Relations with Dost Mohammad (Amir of Afghanistan) was good but with time he began showing signs of friendship with Russia.
                - The concern for imperial defence and search for a scientific border in the north-west brought the English in contact with the Afghans.
                - Afghanistan was considered as a buffer state against the Napoleonic France and Czarist Russia.
                - It was felt that Afghanistan should be under the control of a friendly prince.
                - Initially, the Relations with Dost Mohammad (Amir of Afghanistan) was good but with time he began showing signs of friendship with Russia.
                - In September 1837, British sent a commercial mission to Afghanistan (Burnes Mission), but in reality it was to judge the political situation there.
                - During the rounds of negotiation which could not lead to any fruitful result, Dost Mohammad received the Russian ambassador and with much warmth.
                - Afer the failure of this Burns Mission, Governor General Auckland decided to follow a ʻForward Policyʼ, he planned to depose Dost Muhammad and entrust the country in the hands of some Friendly prince.
            - Course of the War
                - Tripartite Treaty - Between English, Shah Shuja (Ex-ruler of Afghanistan) and Ranjit Singh - combined forces of the three parties, known as ʻArmy of the Indusʼ launched an attack on Kabul.
                - The combined forces won but there were glitches in this plan as Shah Shuja (New Amir of Afghanistan) was unacceptable to the people of Afghanistan and his position could be maintained only with the help of British Army.
                - Thus a small garrison was placed at Kabul under the command of General Elphinstone, but the English continued to face retaliation.
                - Thereafer, numerous battles were fought and in the end - disastrous retreat began from Kabul in 1842.
                - Elphinstone along with his garrison were annihilated in the snow covered passes and Shah Shuja was murdered.
                - Aucklandʼs grandiose plan had exploded like ballon and he was soon replaced by Lord Ellenborough, He ordered the retreat of forces and securing the release of prisoners and subsequently Dost Mohammad was re-established his authority in Kabul.
        - Second Anglo Afghan War (1878 - 1880)
            - The Second Afghan war (1878 -1880) occurred at the time of Governor-General Lytton and ended with the Treaty of Gandamak.
            - As per this treaty, the Amir Yakub Khan Agreed to station a permanent British Resident at Kabul and conduct his foreign policy on advise of the Government of India.
            - Thus, Afghanistan was created as a buffer state between the British Indian and Russian Empire.

    - Annexation of Awadh
        - Early Confrontation
            - The state of Awadh came into direct confrontation with the British during the Battle of Buxar in 1964 when Shuja-ud-Daulha (Nawab of Awadh joined the opposite forces).
            - Awadh then lay at the Mercy of the British but Clive decided not to annex it.
            - At that time Awadh was restored to Nawab afer signing the Treaty of Alliance.
        - The Treaty of 1801 (Subsidiary Alliance)
            - When Governor General Lord Wellesly came to India, He forced the new nawab Saadat Ali khan, to sign a new treaty in the year 1801.
            - As per this treaty, Nawab had to cede half of his territory.
            - He was also required to act as per the advice of the Council of officers of Company.
            - This treaty of 1801 formed the primary basis for the Annexation of Awadh in 1801 as there were rampant cases of corruption and misgovernance in the Awadh state because of the interference of British Council but the whole responsibility was laid on the Saadat Ali khan.
        - Final Annexation
            - By the mid 19 century, British opinion was ripe in favour of annexation of Awadh.
            - In fact, the British imperialists during the time had come to believe that Britain alone had the capacity for good governance.
            - However, Dalhousieʼs greed had chiefly been surrounded by the immense potential of Awadh as a market for Manchester Goods.
            - Accordingly in 1848, when Lord Dalhousie became Governor General, he skilfully planned the annexation of Awadh.
            - He appointed Colonel Sleeman as resident to Lucknow who wanted to go to lengths to prove the misgovernance in the Awadh court.
            - Finally in 1856, Awadh was annexed by Lord Dalhousie on the pretext of maladministration based on Outramʼs report (he succeeded Sleeman).
            - The Nawab, Wajid Ali shah, was kept under house arrest in Calcutta.
            - This incident particularly hurled sepoys of the British Army as most of them came from Awadh.
            - The Land revenue system introduced afer the annexation further intensified the people's discontent with the cumulative result that nearly 3/4th of the adult population of Awadh, including landholders, peasants and sepoys, participated in the Revolt of 1857.

    - British Expansion Beyond Indian Frontiers
        - Reasons for Expansion beyond Indian Frontiers
            - Shifting Financial Base of the East India Company (From Trade to Land Revenue)
                - (As trade profits declined due to European competition and monopolies eroded, the EIC increasingly relied on land revenue from conquered territories to sustain its operations and fund further expansion.)
            - Need for New Markets and Supplies
                - (The Industrial Revolution in Britain demanded raw materials like cotton, timber, and opium, and new markets for manufactured goods, driving the EIC to secure regions like Burma and Awadh.)
            - Need to Safeguard Indian Empire
                - (The perceived threats from Russia, France, and local powers like the Gorkhas necessitated buffer states—Nepal, Afghanistan, Burma—to protect British India’s frontiers and trade routes.)
        - Tibet & History with British
            - Treaty of Lhasa (1904) - with Tibet
                - Tibet would respect the frontier to Sikkim
                - Tibet would not grant any concessions to any foreign power, but would give some control over the foreign affairs of Tibet.
                - Thus reducing Tibet to the status of a protectorate of the British.
                - Afer the Chinese Revolution of 1911, the British accepted Chinaʼs suzerainty over eastern Tibet, and the western zone was declared autonomous.
                - It was decided to draw a boundary line between Tibet and British India known as the Manmohan Line
        - Other Important Points
            - Duran Line - British India and Afghanistan
                - (Established in 1893 by Sir Mortimer Durand, this line demarcated the border between British India and Afghanistan, aimed at securing a scientific frontier against Russian influence, though it remains contested to this day.)
            - Treaty of Punakha (1910) - recognising the Bhutanese ruler as sovereign in all matters expect foreign relations, which were placed under British control.


            Revolt of 1857
            Political Causes
            ● Discontent among ruling chiefs and royal families: Broken pledges and oaths resulted
            in loss of political prestige for some, while policies such as policies of ‘Subsidiary Alliance’
            and 'Doctrine of Lapse’ caused alarm and suspicion in the minds of others.
            ● The collapse of rulers—the erstwhile aristocracy—also adversely affected those
            sections of the Indian society which derived their sustenance from cultural and religious
            pursuits.
            ● The Alien rule of the British - The practice of ‘Absentee sovereignty’ embittered the
            Indians against the British.
            ● Anti-British feelings were particularly strong in those regions like Burma, Assam,
            Coorg, Sind, and the Punjab which were unjustly annexed to the British Empire.
            ● Abolition of Regal Titles - The Mughal Emperor Bahadur Shah II had grown old and
            weak. Lord Dalhousie recognised the succession of Mughal Prince Faqir-Ud-Din, but he
            was not in favour of retaining an imperium in imperio and imposed strict conditions on
            him.
            ○ In 1856, Faqir-ud-din died and Lord Canning declared that the prince next in
            succession would have to renounce the regal title and the ancestral Mughal
            palaces. In addition, the British also abolished the regal titles of the Nawabs of
            Carnatic and Tanjore.
            Economics Causes
            Exploitative Land Revenue Settlements introduced by British -
            ● The huge drain of wealth, The destruction of its industry and increasing land revenue
            (Introduction of Permanent, Ryotwari and Mahalwari settlement) had become the
            common features of the latter half of the eighteenth century.
            ● The Summary Settlement of Awadh proceeded to remove the taluqdars wherever
            possible.
            ○ In pre-British times, taluqdars had held 67% of the total number of villages in Awadh;
            by the Summary Settlement this number had come down to 38 per cent.
            ○ The taluqdars of southern Awadh were the hardest hit and some lost more than half
            of the total number of villages they had previously held.
            
            ○ British land revenue officers believed that by removing taluqdars they would be able
            to settle the land with the actual owners of the soil and thus reduce the level of
            exploitation of peasants while increasing the revenue returns for the state.
            ○ The East India Company, after attaining political power, used it to fund the growth of
            British trade and commerce at the cost of Indians.
            ● A new plantation system introduced in the year 1833: It resulted in incalculable misery
            for the Indian peasants.
            ○ This was the result of permitting Englishmen to acquire land plantations in
            India. The hard hit were the peasants on the indigo plantations in Bengal and
            Bihar. (Teen Kathiya System)
            ○ Sometimes, zamindars, the traditional landed aristocracy, often saw their land rights
            forfeited by the administration. This resulted in a loss of status for them in the
            villages. These dispossessed taluqdars seized the opportunity presented by the
            sepoy revolt, particularly in Awadh.
            Destruction of Indian Trade, Handicraft and Industry
            ● High tariff in Britain against Indian goods: It damaged the Indian trade and manufacture;
            the British also encouraged the import of British goods to India.
            ○ In India the ruin of the millions of artisans and craftsmen was not accompanied by
            any alternative growth of new industrial forms.
            ○ Annexation of Indian states by the Company cut off their major source of patronage.
            Moneylenders and traders emerged as the new landlords
            ● Compelled by this new form of revenue settlement, the peasants resorted to loans from
            moneylenders/traders at usurious rates, the latter often evicting the former on
            nonpayment of debt dues.
            ○ The ruination of Indian industry increased the pressure on agriculture and land.
            Social Causes
            Arrogant Attitude of the British and the Racial Discrimination
            ● Racial overtones and a superiority complex characterized Indiscrimination and assaults
            on Indians by Englishmen became quite common.
            British Interference in Religion and Culture
            ● A general alarm was raised among the Hindus and Muslims by the activities of the
            Christian missionaries. (Charter Act of 1813)
            
            ○ The educational institutions established by the missionaries inculcated western
            education and culture in the place of oriental learning.
            ● Legislations were passed regarding prohibition of female infanticide, sati and slavery and
            passing of legislations regarding widow marriage.
            ○ In 1850, the Religious Disabilities Act was passed which modified certain Hindu
            customs, for example, it stated that a change of religion would not debar a son from
            inheriting the property of his heather father.
            Military Causes
            Low Salary and Poor Prospects of Promotion
            ● Alongside, The conditions of service in the Company's Army and cantonments
            increasingly came into conflict with the religious beliefs and prejudices of the sepoys.
            ○ Restrictions on wearing caste and sectarian marks and secret rumours of
            proselytising activities of chaplains (often maintained on the Company's expenses)
            were interpreted by Indian sepoys, who were generally conservative by nature, as
            interference in their religious affairs.
            ● To the religious Hindu of the time, crossing the seas meant loss of caste.
            ● In 1856 Lord Canning's Government passed the General Service Enlistment Act which
            decreed that all future recruits to the Bengal Army would have to give an undertaking to
            serve anywhere their services might be required by the Government.
            ● The Vellore mutiny of 1806, a precursor to the 1857 Great Revolt, was the outcome of
            such tendencies on the part of the military authorities.
            ● The Indian sepoy was equally unhappy with his emoluments compared to his British
            counterpart.
            ● A more immediate cause of the sepoys' dissatisfaction was the order that they would not
            be given the foreign service allowance (batta) when serving in Sindh or in Punjab.
            ● The Indian sepoy was made to feel a subordinate at every step and was discriminated
            against racially and in matters of promotion and privileges.
            ● The sepoy, in fact, was a 'peasant in uniform' whose consciousness was not divorced
            from that of the rural population.
            ● In 1854, The Post Office Act was passed which withdrew the free postage privilege of the
            Sepoys.
            ● Close to 1857, there were reports of bone dust in flour rations.
            
            Invincibility of the British Rule Shattered
            ● Immediately before this revolt, British suffered major losses such as that in -
            ○ Afghan Wars (1838 - 1842), Punjab Wars (1845 -1849), Crimea War (1854 - 1856)
            ● All these events largely emboldened the spirits of the Sepoys and now they were waiting for
            the event to trigger their anger which was supplied by the greased cartridge event.
            ● This enhanced the sepoys' growing disaffection with the Government.
            ● The cartridge of the new rifle had to be bitten off before loading and the grease was
            reportedly made of beef and pig fat and the administration did nothing to allay these fears,
            and the sepoys felt their religion was in grave danger.
            ● There had been a long history of revolts in the British Indian Army—in Bengal (1764),
            Vellore (1806), Barrackpore (1825) and during the Afghan Wars (1838-42) to mention
            just a few.
            Events Leading up to the Revolt
            ● The 19th Native Infantry at Berhampur, which refused to use the newly introduced
            Enfield rifle and broke out in mutiny in February 1857 was disbanded in March 1857.
            ○ A young sepoy of the 34th Native Infantry, Mangal Pande, went a step further and
            fired at the sergeant major of his unit at Barrackpore. He was overpowered and
            executed on April 6 while his regiment was disbanded in May.
            ○ The 7th Awadh Regiment which defied its officers on May 3 met with a similar fate.
            ● On April 24, ninety men of 3rd Native Cavalry refused to accept the greased
            cartridges.
            ○ On May 9, eighty five of them were dismissed, sentenced to 10 years'
            imprisonment and put in fetters.
            ○ This sparked off a general mutiny among the Indian soldiers stationed at Meerut.
            ○ The very next day, on May 10, they released their imprisoned comrades, killed
            their officers and unfurled the banner of revolt.
            ○ They set off for Delhi after sunset.
            The Great Revolt
            ● The revolt began at Meerut, 58 km from Delhi, on May 10, 1857 and then, gathering force
            rapidly, soon embraced a vast area from the Punjab in the north and the Narmada in the
            south to Bihar in the east and Rajputana in the west.
            ● In Delhi, the local infantry joined them, killed their own European officers including Simon
            
            Fraser, the political agent, and seized the city.
            ● The aged and powerless Bahadur Shah Zafar was proclaimed the emperor of India. The
            long reign of Mughals had made them the traditional symbol of India's political unity.
            ● All the Indian chiefs who took part in the revolt hastened to proclaim their loyalty to the
            Mughal emperor.
            ● On 12 May 1857, Delhi was seized by rebelling sepoys after they overcame Lt. Willoughby,
            the commanding officer at Delhi. The loss of Delhi was a serious blow to the prestige of the
            British.
            ● Bahadur Shah, after initial vacillation, wrote letters to all the chiefs and rulers of India
            urging them to organise a confederacy of Indian states to fight and replace the British
            regime.
            ● The entire Bengal Army soon rose in revolt which spread quickly. Awadh, Rohilkhand,
            the Doab, Bundelkhand, central India, large parts of Bihar and East Punjab shook off
            British authority.
            ● The revolt of the sepoys was accompanied by a rebellion of the civil population,
            particularly in the north-western provinces and Awadh.
            ● Their accumulated grievances found immediate expression and they raised support to give
            vent to their opposition to British rule.
            ● It is the widespread participation in the revolt by the peasantry, the artisans, shopkeepers,
            day labourers, zamindars, religious mendicants, priests and 'civil servants which
            gave it real strength as well as the character of a popular revolt.
            ● Peasants and petty zamindars gave free expression to their grievances by attacking the
            moneylenders and zamindars that had displaced them from the land.
            ● They took advantage of the revolt to destroy the moneylenders’ account books and debt
            records.
            ● They also attacked the British-established law courts, revenue offices (tehsils),
            revenue records and police stations.
            ● Although the Revolt spread to major parts of North India, but practically India, north of
            Narmada remained undisturbed.
            ● One Important thing was the Hindu Muslim unity that was witnessed during the revolt.
            Storm Centres And Leaders Of The Revolt
            ● At Delhi the nominal and symbolic leadership belonged to the Mughal emperor, Bahadur
            Shah, but the real command lay with a court of soldiers headed by General Bakht Khan
            
            who had led the revolt of Bareilly troops and brought them to Delhi.
            ● At Kanpur, Nana Saheb, the adopted son of the last Peshwa, Baji Rao II. He was
            refused the family title and, banished from Poona, was living near Kanpur. Nana Saheb
            expelled the English from Kanpur, proclaimed himself the Peshwa, acknowledged Bahadur
            Shah as the emperor of India and declared himself to be his governor. (Tantya Tope)
            ● Begum Hazrat Mahal took over the reins at Lucknow where the rebellion broke out on
            June 4, 1857 and popular sympathy was overwhelmingly in favour of the deposed Nawab.
            ● Jhansi - Doctrine of Lapse - Rejecting the claim of Damodar Rao (adopted son of Rani and
            Gangadhar Rao)
            ● Brailley - Khan Bahadur Khan
            ● In Bihar, the revolt was led by Kunwar Singh, the zamindar of Jagdishpur . An old man
            in his seventies, he nursed a grudge against the British who had deprived him of his
            estates. He unhesitatingly joined the sepoys when they reached Arrah from Danapore .
            ● Maulvi Ahmadullah of Faizabad was another outstanding leader of the revolt. He was a
            native of Madras and had moved to Faizabad in the north where he fought a stiff battle
            against the British troops. He emerged as one of the revolts' acknowledged leaders once it
            broke out in Awadh in May 1857.
            ● Gonoo was a tribal cultivator of Singhbhum in Chotanagpur, became a rebel leader of the
            Kol tribals of the region.
            ● Shah Mal mobilised the headmen and cultivators of chaurasee des, moving at night
            from village to village, urging people to rebel against the British.
            ○ Locally acknowledged as the Raja, Shah Mal took over the bungalow of an English
            officer, and turned it into a “hall of justice”, settling disputes and dispensing
            judgments.
            ○ He also set up an amazingly effective network of intelligence. For a period the
            people of the area felt that firangi raj was over, and their raj had come.
            ○ Shah Mal was killed in battle in July 1857
            ● Important centres of revolt and the lines of British attack against the rebels
            
            Suppression Of Revolt
            ● The British captured Delhi on September 20, 1857 after prolonged and bitter fighting.
            ● Bahadur Shah was taken prisoner. The royal princes were captured and butchered on
            the spot, publicly shot at point blank range
            ○ The emperor was exiled to Rangoon where he died in 1862.
            ● Thus the great House of Mughals was finally and completely extinguished.
            ● With the fall of Delhi the focal point of the revolt disappeared.
            ● Nana Saheb, defeated at Kanpur, escaped to Nepal in early 1859, never to be heard of
            again.
            ○ His close associate Tantia Tope escaped into the jungles of central India, was
            captured after being betrayed by his friend while asleep in April 1859 and put to
            death.
            ● The Rani of Jhansi had died on the battlefield earlier in June 1858.
            ● By 1859, Kunwar Singh, Bakht Khan, Khan Bahadur Khan of Bareilly, Rao Sahib
            (brother of Nana Saheb) and Maulvi Ahmadullah were all dead, while the Begum of
            Awadh was compelled to hide in Nepal.
            ● By the end of 1859, British authority over India was fully reestablished.
            
            British Officers who surpassed the Revolt
            ● Delhi and Lucknow - Sir Colin Campbell
            ● Jhansi - Sir Hugh Rose
            ● Banaras - Colonel Neil
            Reasons for Failure
            ● Limited territorial spread was one factor; there was no all-India veneer about the revolt.
            ○ The eastern, southern and western parts of India remained more or less
            unaffected.
            ○ Certain classes and groups did not join and, in fact, worked against the revolt.
            (Scindia Ruler of Gwalior)
            ● Big zamindars acted as "breakwaters to storm"; even Awadh tehsildars backed off once
            promises of land restitution were spelt out.
            ○ Moneylenders and merchants suffered the wrath of the mutineers badly and anyway
            saw their class interests better protected under British patronage.
            ● Support by Educated Indians -
            ○ Modern educated Indians viewed this revolt as backward looking, and
            mistakenly hoped the British would usher in an era of modernization
            ○ Most Indian rulers refused to join and often gave active help to the British.
            ● European Modern War Machinery -
            ○ The Indian soldiers were poorly equipped materially, fighting generally with swords
            and spears and very few guns and muskets.
            ○ On the other hand, the European soldiers were equipped with the latest weapons of
            war like the Enfield rifle.
            ○ The electric telegraph kept the commander-in-chief informed about the
            movements and strategy of the rebels.
            ● The revolt was poorly organized with no coordination or central leadership.
            ○ The principal rebel leaders—Nana Saheb, Tantia Tope, Kunwar Singh,
            Laxmibai—were no match to their British opponents in generalship.
            ● The mutineers did not lack a clear understanding of colonial rule; nor did they have a
            forward looking programme, a coherent ideology, a political perspective or a societal
            alternative.
            ● The rebels represented diverse elements with differing grievances and concepts of
            current politics.
            
            Consequences
            ● The direct responsibility for the administration of the country was assumed by the
            British Crown and Company rule was abolished.
            ○ The assumption of the Government of India by the sovereign of Great Britain was
            announced by Lord Canning at a durbar at Allahabad in the 'Queen's
            Proclamation' issued on November 1, 1858.
            ● The 1857 Revolt, however, sowed the seeds of Indian nationalism
            
            Post 1857 Scenario
            Aftermath of Revolt of 1857
            The Act for Better Government of India, 1858
            ● Even before the Revolt could be suppressed fully, The British Parliament, on August 2,
            1858, passed an Act for the Better Government of India.
            ● The 1857 revolt had exposed the Company’s limitations in administering under a
            complex situation. The 1858 Act sought to rectify this -
            ○ India was to be governed by and in the name of the Crown through a secretary of
            state and a council of fifteen members.
            ○ The initiative and the final decision were to be with the secretary of state and the
            Council was to be just advisory in nature. Thus, the dual system introduced by
            the Pitt’s India Act came to an end.
            ○ Governor-general became the Viceroy (his prestige, if not authority, increased).
            ○ The assumption of power by the Crown was one of formality rather than substance.
            It gave a decent burial to an already dead horse—the Company’s administration.
            Queen’s Proclamation,1858
            ● The assumption of the Government of India by the sovereign of Great Britain was
            announced by Lord Canning at a durbar at Allahabad in the ‘Queen’s Proclamation’
            issued on November 1, 1858. It was by this proclamation that the governor-general
            acquired the additional title of ‘Viceroy’.
            ● As per the Queen’s proclamation,
            ○ The era of annexations and expansion had ended and the British promised to
            respect the dignity and rights of the native princes.
            ○ The Indian states were henceforth to recognise the paramountcy of the British
            Crown.
            ○ The people of India were promised freedom of religion without interference from
            British officials.
            ○ It promised equal and impartial protection under law to all Indians, besides equal
            opportunities in government services irrespective of race or creed. It was also
            promised that old Indian rights, customs and practices would be given due regard
            while framing and administering the law.
            
            Aftermath of Revolt of 1857 (Changes in the Army)
            ● The Army, which was at the forefront of the outbreak, was thoroughly re-organized and
            British military policy came to be dominated by the idea of “division and counterpoise”. The
            British could no longer depend on Indian loyalty, so the number of Indian soldiers
            was drastically reduced even as the number of European soldiers was increased.
            ● Recruits were to be drawn from the ‘martial’ races of Punjab, Nepal, and north-western
            frontier that had proved loyal to the British during the Revolt. Efforts were made to keep the
            army away from the civilian population.
            ● All Indian artillery units, except a few mountain batteries, were made defunct. All higher
            posts in the army and the artillery departments were reserved for the Europeans.
            ● Change in Military Policy -
            ○ The number of European Soldiers was increased.
            ■ In the Bengal army, the ratio was fixed at one European soldier for two
            European soldiers.
            ■ In the Bombayr and Madras armies, the ratio was fixed 1:3 or 2:5.
            ○ All big positions in the Army and artillery were reserved for Europeans. (Artillery for
            now was specifically reserved for Europeans).
            ○ Soldiers from Awadh, Bihar and Central India were declared as non-marital while
            Punjabis, Gorkhas and Pathans (who had fought for the British) were declared
            as martial and recruited in large numbers. By 1875, half of the British Indian Army
            was recruited from Punjab.
            Aftermath of Revolt of 1857 (Changes in the Approach)
            ● The earlier reformist zeal evaporated as many liberals in Britain began to believe that
            Indians were beyond reform. Thus the era of reforms came to an end. The conservative
            reaction in England made the British Empire in India more autocratic; it began to deny
            the aspirations of the educated Indians for sharing power.
            ● In the long term, this new British attitude proved counter-productive for the Empire, as this
            caused frustrations.
            ● The policy of divide and rule started in earnest after the Revolt of 1857. The British used
            one class/ community against another unscrupulously.
            ● While British territorial conquest was at an end, a period of systematic economic loot by
            the British began. The Indian economy was fully exploited without fear.
            ● The complete structure of the Indian government was remodeled and based on the notion
            
            of a master race—justifying the philosophy of the ‘Whiteman’s burden’. This widened
            the gulf between the rulers and the ruled.
            Aftermath of Revolt of 1857 Other Changes
            Change in Policy towards the Upper Class (Princes and Landlords)
            ● Policy of Annexation abandoned.
            ● Princes and Landlords rewarded and befriended.
            ● Britain as a paramount power - Britain was acknowledged as a paramount power.
