            ○ In 1876, Queen Victoria assumed the title of ‘Empress of India’.
            ● Hostility towards Higher Education - The British now became hostile to Higher Education
            in India and even took active steps to curtail after the formation of Indian National Congress
            in 1885.
            ● Indian Civil Services Act of 1861 - In order to show off the notion of equality and
            transparency, this act was passed which provided for an annual competitive exam to be
            held in London.
            ○ However, the detailed rules framed were such that they had the effect of keeping the
            civil services a close preserve of the Englismen.
            Change in Attitude towards Educated Indians
            ● Indian Councils Act of 1861
            ○ Sir Bartle Frere, in his famous Minute of 1860, pushed for representation of
            Indians in the Legislative Council - As the British now realised that a major cause
            of the Revolt was the miscommunication between the ruler and the ruled.
            ○ Thus this Act, marked the starting of the active representation of Indian in these
            Councils.
            ● Indian Civil Services Act of 1861
            ○ In order to show off the notion of equality and transparency, this act was passed
            which provided for an annual competitive exam to be held in London.
            ○ However, the detailed rules framed were such that they had the effect of keeping the
            civil services a close preserve of the Englismen.
            The Azamgarh Proclamation of 1857
            ● It was authored mostly by Firoz Shah, the grandson of Mughal Emperor Bahadur
            Shah II.
            ● Major Highlights of the proclamation
            ● The declaration outlines the various complaints of various social groups against the
            
            unfair colonial authority in great detail and proclaims the Mughals' capacity to
            establish their suzerainty over India.
            ● The declaration advises the rulers and chieftains to accept the throne of Delhi and
            defend their people in terrible times.
            ● "Both Hindoos and Mohammedans are being ruined under the tyranny and
            oppression of the infidel and treacherous English," the author claims.
            ● Assuring a brighter economic, social, and cultural future following the defeat of the
            English, the manifesto urges landlords, merchants, public servants, artisans, and
            people of other religions to accept the Badshahi Government.
            British portray of the Revolt
            The Clemency of Canning
            ● When Governor-General Canning declared that a gesture of leniency and a show of
            mercy would help in winning back the loyalty of the sepoys, he was mocked in the
            British press.
            ● In one of the cartoons published in the pages of Punch, a British journal of comic
            satire, Canning is shown as a looming father figure, with his protective hand over the
            head of a sepoy who still holds an unsheathed sword in one hand and a dagger in
            the other, both dripping with blood. It was called ‘The Clemency of Canning.
            
            Relevant PYQ’s for Practice -
            
            Political Associations before 1885 & Formation of Indian National Congress
            Political Association before the Indian National Congress
            ● Most of the political associations in the early half of the nineteenth century were
            dominated by wealthy and aristocratic elements.
            ● They were local or regional in character.
            Through long petitions to the British Parliament most of them demanded
            ● Administrative reforms.
            ● Association of Indians with the administration.
            ● Spread of education.
            ● The political associations of the second half of the nineteenth century came to be
            increasingly dominated by the educated middle class—the lawyers, journalists,
            doctors, teachers, etc.,—and they had a wider perspective and a larger agenda.
            Political Associations in Bengal
            ● Raja Ram Mohan Roy - Morning Star of Reform Movement in the country, was the first
            great leader of modern India.
            ● He was also the pioneer of the political movement in India, Well read and deeply
            
            influenced by Western Ideas, considered to be the first to focus the attention of the
            Englishmen on the grievances of Indian people.
            ● The Bangabhasha Prakasika Sabha was formed in 1836 by associates of Raja
            Rammohan Roy with the aim of discussing Government Policy and seeking redressal
            through petitions and memorandums.
            Landholders Society/Zamindari Association (1838)
            ● Founded by Dwarka Nath Tagore, RadhaKanta Deb and others to safeguard the
            interests of Bengal, Bihar and Orissa.
            ● This organization is credited with launching Organized political activity in India and the
            use of constitutional methods for grievance redressal.
            ● It marked the beginning of an organised Political Activity in India and the use of
            methods of constitutional agitation for the redressal of grievances.
            Bengal British India Society (1843)
            ● The Bengal British India Society was founded in 1843 with the object of ―
            ○ The collection and dissemination of information relating to the actual condition of
            the people of British India
            ○ And to employ such other means of peaceful and lawful character as may appear
            calculated to secure the welfare, extend the just rights and advance the
            interests of all classes of our fellow subjects.
            British Indian Association (1851)
            ● In 1851, both the Landholders‘ Society and the Bengal British India Society merged
            into the British Indian Association.
            ● They sent some suggestions to the British Parliament to be included in the Company
            Charter such as -
            ○ Establishment of a separate legislature of a popular character
            ○ Separation of executive from judicial functions
            ○ Reduction in salaries of higher officers
            ○ Abolition of salt duty, abkari and stamp duties
            ● The prayers of the Association were partially met and the Charter Act of 1853 provided
            for the addition of 6 members in the Governor General’s Council for legislative purpose.
            ● This association continued to function even after the formation of the Indian National
            
            Congress.
            ● In 1852, its branches were also established in Bombay and Madras and were known as
            the Bombay Association and the Madras Native Association respectively
            Political Associations in Bombay
            Poona Sarvajanik Sabha (1870)
            ● The Poona Sarvajanik Sabha was founded in 1867 by Mahadeo Govind Ranade and
            others, with the objective of serving as a bridge between the government and the
            people.
            ● The sabha provided a platform for voicing political concerns. One of its demands was
            Indian representation in Parliament. It publicly voiced its protest against the Vernacular
            Press Act. The Sabha was also involved in getting agrarian disputes settled.
            ● This body is considered as a precursor to the Indian National Congress which was
            also formed in Maharashtra.
            Bombay Presidency Association (1885)
            ● The Bombay Presidency Association was started by Badruddin Tyabji, Pherozshah
            Mehta and K.T. Telang in 1885.
            ● It championed Indian interests and hosted the first meeting of the Indian National
            Congress in Bombay at the end of 1885.
            Political Association in Madras
            Madras Native Association (1852)
            ● It was a branch of the British Indian Association of Calcutta by Gazulu
            Lakshminarasu Chetty.
            Madras Mahajan Sabha (1884)
            ● The Madras Mahajan Sabha was founded in 1884 by M. Viraraghavachari, B.
            Subramanya Aiyar and P. Anandacharlu to coordinate the activities of the Local
            Associations.
            Political Associations in England
            ● The East India Association was organised by Dadabhai Naoroji in 1866 in London to
            
            discuss the Indian question and influence public men in England to promote
            Indian welfare.
            ● Later, branches of the association were started in prominent Indian cities.
            Other National Associations
            India League (1875)
            ● The Indian League was started in 1875 by Sisir Kumar Ghosh with the objective of
            ―stimulating the sense of nationalism amongst the people and of encouraging
            political education.
            ● Ghosh himself was a noted journalist and a founder of Amrita Bazar Patrika. (A noted
            Bengali Newspaper).
            Indian Association (1876)
            ● Most Important of the Pre Congress Organisations.
            ● The Indian Association of Calcutta (also known as the Indian National Association)
            superseded the Indian League and was founded in 1876 by younger nationalists of
            Bengal led by Surendra Nath Banerjee to -
            ○ Create a strong public opinion on political questions
            ○ Unify Indian people in a common political programmed.
            ○ It demanded simultaneous holding of civil service examination in England
            and India and Indianization of higher administrative posts.
            ○ It led a campaign against the repressive arms act and the vernacular press act.
            ● It sponsored an all India conference which first took place in Calcutta on December 28
            to 30, 1883. More than hundred delegates from different parts of the country attended.
            ● The Annual subscription rate was Rs 5 per annum, and it showed all signs of a broad
            based nationalist movement.
            ● Soon the Indian Association became the leading representative of the educated
            community of Bengal as well as a major force in Indian politics.
            ● When in 1876, Lytton reduced the age for appearing in the ICS to 19 years from 21
            Years, The Indian Association took up this issue and organized a pan India agitation,
            known as the Indian Civil Service Agitation.
            
            Relevant PYQ’s for practice -
            
            Formation of Indian National Congress
            Factors Promoting the Growth of Nationalism in India
            Political Unity
            ● For the first time, most of the regions in India were united politically and administratively
            under a single power (the British rule).
            ● It introduced a uniform system of law and government.
            Development of Communication and Transport
            ● The introduction of railways, telegraphs and postal services and the construction of
            roads and canals facilitated communication among the people.
            ● All these brought Indians nearer to each other and provided the facility to organise the
            national movement on an all India basis.
            English Language and Western Education
            ● The English-educated Indians, who led the national movement, developed Indian
            nationalism and organised it.
            ● Western education facilitated the spread of the concepts of liberty, equality, freedom
            and nationalism and sowed the seeds of nationalism.
            The Role of the Press
            ● The Indian Press, both English and vernacular, had also aroused the national
            consciousness.
            Social and Religious Movements of the Nineteenth Century
            ● The leaders of various organizations like the Brahmo Samaj, Ramakrishna Mission, Arya
            Samaj, and Theosophical Society generated a feeling of regard for and pride in the
            motherland.
            Economic Exploitation by the British
            ● The English systematically ruined the Indian trade and native industries.
            Racial Discrimination
            ● The Revolt of 1857 created a kind of permanent bitterness and suspicion between the
            British and the Indians. India as a nation and Indians as individuals were subjected to
            insults, humiliation and contemptuous treatment.
            Administration of Lytton
            ● Lord Lytton arranged the Delhi Durbar at a time when the larger part of India was in the
            grip of famine.
            ● He passed the Vernacular Press Act which curbed the liberty of the Indian Press.
            
            ● His Arms Act was a means to prevent the Indians from keeping arms. All these measures
            created widespread discontent among the Indians.
            The Ilbert Bill controversy
            ● The Ilbert Bill was presented in the Central Legislature during the Viceroyalty of Lord
            Ripon.
            ● The Bill tried to remove racial inequality between Indian and European judges in
            courts. This Bill was opposed by the British residents in India. Ultimately the Bill was
            modified.
            Rise of Middle-Class Intelligentsia
            ● Understanding of Contradictions in Indian and Colonial Interests.
            ● Impact of Contemporary Movements in the World
            ● Two sessions of the Indian National Conference had been held in 1883 and 1885, which
            had representatives drawn from all major towns of India. (Indian Association).
            ○ Surendranath Banerjee and Ananda Mohan Bose were the main architects of the
            Indian National Conference.
            ● Now it was only a matter of time before a national body was created and the stage was set
            for the birth of the INC.
            ● Role of Nationalist art and Literature.
            ○ In the 1860s, a Bengali play called Neel Darpan (Dinbandhu Mitra) became
            popular which reflected the plight of Indigo Farmers.
            ○ Bankim Chandra Chaterjee wrote Anand Math which contains the immoral song
            ‘Bande Mataram’.
            ○ Bhartendu Harishchandra, the father of Modern Hindi, also urged his countrymen
            in his plays and poems to buy Swadeshi things.
            ● Role of Political Associations prior to INC
            ● The final shape to the idea of an all-India organisation was given by a retired English civil
            servant, A.O. Hume, who mobilised leading intellectuals of the time and, with their
            cooperation,
            ○ Organized the first session of the Indian National Congress at Gokuldas Tejpal
            Sanskrit College in Bombay in December 1885. (GG at this time - Lord Dufferin)
            (1885 to 1906, Hume was the General Secretary of the Congress).
            ○ Hume and Lord Rippon were of the view that educated middle class in India is here
            to stay and its legit imitate aspirations should be met and grievances addressed.
            ● Earlier the INC was to be known by the name of ‘Indian National Union’ whose first
            
            session was to be convened in Poona but due to outbreak of Cholera, the venue was
            shifted to Bombay.
            ● First Phase (1885 - 1905) - This phase is known as the Moderate Phase. During this
            period, the vision and the activities of the Congress were dim and vague.
            ● Second Phase (1905 - 1918) - This phase is known as the Extremist phase. Swaraj or Self
            Government was adopted as the political Goal.
            ● Third Phase (1919 - 1947) - This phase is known as the phase of Mass Mobilisation under
            Mahatma Gandhi. Purna Swaraj or Complete Independence was the main Goal.
            1st Congress Session
            ● The first session of the Indian National Congress was attended by 72 delegates and
            presided over by Womesh Chandra Bonnerjee. (He was a Barrister and also the first
            Indian to contest the election for the British House of Commons, although he lost the
            elections)
            ○ In his first speech, he declared that Congress had immense faith in the British
            sense of Justice, And the Congress was a loyal well wisher of the Government and
            in return they demanded greater representation of Indians in Government and Higher
            Posts.
            ○ This session was attended by 72 delegates of which 39 were lawyers.
            ○ During this session, the proceedings were conducted in strict parliamentary
            fashion. In all nine sessions were adopted.
            ● Historian Bipan Chandra writes that, ‘If Hume wanted to use the Congress as a safety
            Valve, the early Congress leaders hoped to use him as a ‘Lightening Conductor’’
            ● Controversies regarding the Origin of the Indian National Congress -
            ○ Safety Valve Theory (Official Conspiracy Theory)
            ● Hereafter, the Congress met every year in December, in a different part of the country each
            time.
            ● In 1890, Kadambini Ganguly, the first woman graduate of Calcutta University,
            addressed the Congress session, which symbolized the commitment of the freedom
            struggle to give the women of India their due status in national life.
            
            Consolidation of British Empire In India
            Evolution of the East India Company (EIC)
            Transition from Trading to Political Power
            ● Initially established as a trading enterprise.
            ● Gradually expanded its control over India through conquests.
            ● Evolved into a formidable political force.
            Shift from Commercial to Territorial Entity
            ● The company transformed from being primarily commercial to predominantly territorial in
            nature.
            Issues with Company Administration
            ● Misuse of Power:
            ○ Widespread abuse of authority by company officials.
            ○ This led to growing concerns in Britain about governance in Indian territories.
            ● Financial Crisis:
            ○ Continuous wars and administrative mismanagement caused severe financial
            distress.
            ● Revenue Maximisation:
            ○ The British Government sought to maximise revenue from EIC territories.
            British Parliament's Intervention
            ● Concerns about the Company's administration led to pressure on the British Parliament.
            ● The Parliament introduced various constitutional laws to regulate and control the
            Company's operations.
            
            Let’s Understand Company Structure -
            Important Learning from the past
            ● Dual Government of Bengal -
            ○ The failed dual government in Bengal, coupled with the
            company's abuse of power and corruption, became one of the
            primary reasons for the British parliament to regulate the EIC
            activities in India.
            ○ This dual system was eventually abolished by Warren Hastings in
            1772.
            ● Additional Fact - Bengal Famine of 1770, commonly referred to as Chhiattorer Monnontor,
            estimated to have claimed the lives of around 10 million people.
            ● The primary cause of famine was the company’s mismanagement and harsh land tax
            collection, particularly in cash.
            
            Regulating Act of 1773
            Background
            ● By this time, Company had already acquired political power and power-Yielding
            territories and became an empire in itself, therefore the British Company soon realised that
            Company could no longer be allowed to remain outside the ambit of the State.
            ● Therefore the British Parliament subjected the company to control through various
            constitutional laws and Acts.
            Regulating Act of 1773 (Factors leading to the Act)
            ● The Dual Govt of Bengal led to rampant corruption in the whole system and there
            seemed to be a fatal divorce of power from law and order in Bengal.
            ○ The abuses of private trade reached a greater height than ever before. Clive
            himself returned to England at the age of 34, with wealth and property in India
            yielding 40,000 pounds a year.
            ○ This wealth attracted the interest of British Parliamentarians and suggested that
            the Crown should take over the Company.
            ○ The first intervention of the British Parliament in the Company’s affairs in 1767
            merely took the form of a demand for share of Bengal’s plunder to the tune of
            400,000 pound per annum.
            ● There was jealousy among Englishmen in Britain and the Company’s officials began to be
            looked down upon in England and were given the deserved title of ‘Nabobs’, it symbolised
            the corrupt form of Nawab and was used for exporters of India.
            ● Military setbacks, particularly the defeat against Hyder Ali of Mysore in 1769.
            ● Financial Crisis in East India Company - By 1770, the continuous war and expansion
            policy of the Company had pushed it on the brinks of bankruptcy, and a committee was set
            up.
            ○ Based on the Committee’s report, Lord North (British PM) presented his famous
            bill on 18 May 1773, which later came to be known as the Regulating Act.
            ● By a separate Act, the Company was granted a loan of Rs. 1,40,000 pounds at 4%
            interest.
            Regulating Act of 1773 (Main Provision)
            ● Main aim was to regulate the activities of Company in both India as well as England & led to
            ○ Control by British Government - Act allowed the company to retain its territories in
            India but regulated its activities.
            
            ● Restructuring of Company -
            ○ The Act also provided for direct Parliamentary control over the CoD for the first time.
            ■ The Regulating Act raised the issue of raising the qualification for both CoP
            and CoD. (No Re-election, no private trade)
            ■ The Court of directors had to report all civil, military, revenue matters of
            Bengal to the British Government.
            ○ The Act provided for payment of liberal salaries and prohibited immoral
            activities such as private trade, receiving or giving bribes, money lending etc.
            ● In India -
            ○ Since there was no supreme Authority in India, This Act tried to establish an
            empowered central authority.
            ○ To check the rampant corruption in Bengal, The Act provided for a new presidency
            of the Bengal,
            ■ It designated the Governor of Bengal as the ‘Governor General of
            Bengal’ and created an Executive Council with 4 councillors. (Decision
            was taken by majority, binding on GG. GG has both an ordinary and a casting
            
            vote, but no veto.)
            ■ Warren Hastings was the first Governor General.
            ○ Another defect in the system was that there was no independent judicial body to
            keep check over the executive.
            ■ The Act provided for the establishment of the Supreme Court of Justice at
            Calcutta.
            ■ It is composed of Chief Justice and three puisne Judges. (Sir Elijah
            Imphey)
            Regulating Act of 1773 (Limitations)
            ● It was intentionally vague in many of its provisions - It had neither given the state a
            definite control over the Company, nor the Company a definite control over the Servants.
            ● When put to practice - it broke down under the weight of its own defects .
            Amending Act (Act of Settlement 1781)
            Objective
            ● Passed to resolve conflicts regarding the jurisdiction of the Supreme Court which was left
            vague in the Regulating Act of 1773.
            Key Provisions
            ● Jurisdiction Exemptions
            ○ Governor-General (GG) and Council, along with Company servants, were exempted
            from the Supreme Court’s jurisdiction for official actions.
            ● Exclusion from Revenue Matters
            ○ The Supreme Court’s jurisdiction did not cover revenue matters or issues related to
            revenue collection.
            ● Calcutta Jurisdiction
            ○ The Supreme Court’s authority extended over all residents of Calcutta.
            ○ Hindus and Muslims were to be tried under their respective personal laws.
            ● Regulatory Authority
            ○ The Governor-General and Council were empowered to establish rules and
            regulations for Provincial Courts and Councils.
            ○ This Act marked the first effort to delineate the executive and judiciary
            
            functions in India, clearly defining their separate jurisdictions.
            ○ Calcutta Jurisdiction
            ■ The Supreme Court’s authority extended over all residents of Calcutta.
            ■ Hindus and Muslims were to be tried under their respective personal laws.
            ○ Regulatory Authority
            ■ The Governor-General and Council were empowered to establish rules and
            regulations for Provincial Courts and Councils.
            Pitt’s India Act of 1784
            ● This bill was introduced by William Pitt which later came to be known as Pitt’s India Act.
            ○ Its aim was to remove the weakness of the Regulating Act of 1773 and bring
            Company’s administration in India under the supreme control of British
            Parliament.
            ○ A similar bill was introduced by Fox-North Coalition but the Bill was defeated
            and the Ministry had to resign. This was the first and the last time a British
            Ministry was wrecked on an Indian issue.
            ● Increased control of British Government - it was the first time that areas in India
            controlled by Company were officially referred to as “British Possessions in India”
            Pitt’s India Act of 1784 (Main Provisions)
            ● Allowed the Court of Directors to manage the commercial affairs of the Company.
            ○ But for Political Affairs, the Act appointed a Board of six Commissioners, known as
            Board of Control.
            ● In this way, a form of Double Government was introduced. The aim was to allow the
            Company to rule, and the Board to control.`
            ● The Act thus placed the civil and military government of the Company in due
            subordination to the Government in England and this continued until 1858.
            ● The Act mandated East India Company officials to declare their assets and introduced
            stringent penalties for corruption, forming a regulatory framework that lasted until 1858
            ● Allowed the Court of Directors to manage the commercial affairs of the Company.
            ○ But for Political Affairs, the Act appointed a Board of six Commissioners, known as
            Board of Control.
            ● In this way, a form of Double Government was introduced. The aim was to allow the
            Company to rule, and the Board to control.`
            ● The Act thus placed the civil and military government of the Company in due
            
            subordination to the Government in England and this continued until 1858.
            ● The Act mandated East India Company officials to declare their assets and introduced
            stringent penalties for corruption, forming a regulatory framework that lasted until 1858.
            Changes with respect to Company’s Governance in India
            ● Changes within Governor General and his council -
            ○ The number of members in the Governor-General’s Council was reduced to 3. The
            Commander-in-Chief of the British Army in India would be one of the three.
            ○ Madras and Bombay presidencies became subordinate to the Bengal
            presidency. Calcutta effectively became capital of British Indian Colonies.
            ○ The GG and council were made subordinate to the British Government, they
            were forbidden to declare war without prior sanction of the Directors.
            ● The Act also introduced the principle of non-intervention in the affairs of Indian Princely
            states to end further war and conquests.
            Significance
            ● Increased British Control - It established a Board of Control, shifting power from East
            India Company to British Govt, thereby increasing direct British oversight in Indian
            affairs.
            ● Ambiguity in Governance - Act created confusion in the division of power between
            EIC and British Gov.
            ○ Lack of clear responsibilities between the Board of Control and the Court of
            Directors.
            ● Allegation of Favouritism and Anti Corruption Measures
            Act of 1786
            ● In 1786, Pitt moved this bill in order to make Cornwallis accept the
            Governor-Generalship of India.
            ○ Cornwallis wanted to assume the power of both the GG and
            Commander-in-Chief.
            ● This Act joined both the offices of GG and the CnC in the same person.
            ● The GG was further empowered and allowed to override the majority of his Council in
            matters of War and peace on his own responsibility.
            
            Charter Act of 1793
            ● This act was passed in response to concerns about the following -
            ○ The concentration of power in the hands of GG.
            ○ The need to regulate the Company’s financial and commercial activities.
            ○ To codify Laws to ensure that there was an impression of fairness and
            transparency in governance.
            Provisions
            ● Extension of Company’s Charter -
            ○ It extended the Company’s exclusive privilege of trade for a further period of 20
            Years, maintaining its trade monopoly and political control in India.
            ● Increased power of Governor General
            ○ GG had the power to bypass the council's decisions.
            ● Revenue Collection and Economic Reforms -
            ○ The company was empowered to give licences to individuals as well as the
            Company’s employees to trade in India.
            ○ This Act separated the Company’s revenue collection and legal duties, Leading to
            the end of Maal Adalats, which were revenue courts.
            ● Codification of Laws -
            ○ This act laid the foundation of Governance based on written laws in British India in
            place of personal rules of past rulers. (Started with Bengal)
            ○ The Act required all the laws to be printed and translated in India Languages “so that
            the people should become familiar with their rights, privileges and immunities.”
            Charter Act of 1813
            Background
            ● Britain in the midst of the Industrial Revolution, sought new markets for its manufactured
            goods and sources of raw material.
            ● There was growing public and paramilitary opposition to the EIC monopoly, with demands
            for free trade and Laissez faire policies.
            ○ Moreover, By 1813, The Company's territories in India had greatly expanded and it
            was considered nearly possible for the Company to function both as a
            commercial as well as political entity.
            
            ● The Napoleonic Wars had greatly impacted European trade, prompting Britain to look
            beyond Europe, particularly towards India.
            ● Additionally, Christian missionaries were advocating for access to India against the
            Company’s restrictive policies.
            Charter Act of 1813 (Main Provisions)
            ● Retention of Territories and Revenue -
            ○ The Act of 1813 renewed the Company’s Charter for next 20 years and allowed it to
            possess territories and collect revenue for next 20 years.
            ○ The Act explicitly declared the Sovereignty of Crown over the territories acquired by
            the Company.
            ● Ending Company’s monopoly -
            ○ Company’s monopoly on trade was broken.
            ○ Exception - Trade in tea, opium and goods with China.
            ● Expanded Jurisdiction of Indian Courts -
            ○ Indian Courts were granted greater Jurisdiction over British and other European
            subjects.
            ● Christian Missionaries allowed -
            ○ It allowed Missionaries to go to India and allowed them to settle there under a
            license. (Facilitated the appointment of Bishop in Calcutta)
            ● Funding for Education -
            ○ It provided a yearly fund of Rs. 1 Lakh for promotion of literature and sciences
            among Indian Subjects.
            ● Civil Services Reforms -
            ○ Act proposed open competition for civil service positions and states that Indian
            should be eligible for these roles, However this reform faced opposition and was not
            implemented.
            Charter Act of 1813 (Assessment of the Act)
            ● Economic Liberalisation - The act encouraged competition and economic growth by
            allowing Indian merchants and other Europeans to trade.
            ● Social and Religious Changes - Act facilitated the spread of Christianity and social
            reforms, influencing religious and societal dynamics.
            ● Educational Advancement - The Act funded Indian Education, recognising its importance
            for social progress and intellectual development.
            
            Charter Act of 1833
            ● This act is also known as Saint Helena Act of 1833 because it transferred ownership of the
            island of Saint Helena (Located in South Atlantic) from the EIC to the British Crown.
            Background
            ● EIC trade monopoly was viewed as harmful to both the British administration and the Indian
            populace.
            ● This is a period of Industrial Revolution and the Englishmen are looking for new export
            markets.
            ● In 1830, The Whigs (later known as Liberal Party) came into power.
            ○ Two Liberals - Macauly and James Mill, had a visible impact on the Charter of 1833.
            Charter Act of 1833 (Main Provisions)
            ● Legal British Colony -
            ○ India was made a British colony and for the first time, GOI was established with
            authority over the entire territory possessed by the British in India.
            ○ All the restrictions imposed on the entry of Europeans and British were abolished.
            ○ Europeans and British could now freely acquire, hold or dispose of any property in
            India.
            Office of GG
            Important Points - Executive Powers
            ● GG of Bengal made GG of India and his council known as Indian Council.
            ● William Bentick - First GG of India.
            ● Civil - Military and Revenue matters were controlled by GG.
            ● Financial Centralisation - The GG’s council took over all the Financial matters.
            ● For the first time, The Government GG ran was called as Government of India
            Office of GG
            Important Points - Legislative Powers
            GG in Council were given exclusive legislative powers such as -
            ● Power to Repeal, alter or amend any laws or regulations for all persons in the British
            territory or India.
            
            ● Act deprived the Governors of Bombay and Madras of their legislative powers (Councils
            Act of 1861 will restore them back)
            ● From now on, the rules made by company were called ‘Acts’ (earlier called regulations)
            ● Act provided for appointment of the 4th member of GG’s Council, He had no voting rights
            and could only attend the meeting on Invitation.
            ● His addition to the council was to help in making laws, thus his work was fully legislative.
            Macauly was first one to be appointed.
            ● However, The Court of Directors was given the power to veto any law made by the GG in
            the council.
            ● The Decision of GG would prevail at the time of dispute on any matter in the GG council.
            ● Abolition of Commercial Privileges -
            ○ The Company’s exclusive trade privileges were ended and the Company was
            asked to wind up its commercial business as soon as possible.
            ○ Thus Role of the company was changed from that of a commercial body to a
            purely administrative body.
            ○ This Act also extended the Company’s Charter for 20 Years more in matter
            other than trade, like the right to raise armies, wage war and rule conquered
            territories.
            ● Minister for Indian Affairs -
            ○ The President of the Board of Control was replaced by Minister for Indian Affairs
            ● Formation of Law Commission - To codify laws this act provided for the appointment of
            Law Commission. The first Commission was appointed in 1834 under the chairmanship of
            Lord Macauly.
            ○ The Indian Penal Code and Codes of Civil and Criminal Procedure were enacted
            by its efforts.
            ● Civil Service Reform -
            ○ The Charter Act of 1833 became the first act that made provision to freely admit the
            natives of India to share an administration in the country.
            ○ The Act gave the power to the Court of Directors to nominate candidates through the
            process of Competitive examination,
            ○ The Act stated that ‘Fitness’ is henceforth to be the criteria of eligibility.
            ○ No native shall by reason only of his religion, place of birth, descent or colour be
            
            disabled from holding any employment under the Company.
            ○ However, this system of open competition was not effectively operated in near future.
            ● GG was directed to work towards abolition of slavery in India.
            ○ (It was ultimately abolished by Indian Slavery Act of 1843.)
            ● The Act provided for a new presidency of Agra and Fort William by the division of the
            Bengal Presidency, but this provision never came into existence.
            Charter Act of 1833 (Significance)
            ● Marked the culmination of the centralisation process which first began with the
            Regulating Act.
            ● This act paved the way for the unification of British India and the establishment of a
            strong central government in the country. It was the final step towards legislative and
            executive centralisation in India.
            ● However, the transfer of powers from the Governor General of Madras and Bombay
            to the GG of India often restrained the government’s ability to resolve public matters.
            Charter Act of 1853
            Background, Significant challenges
            ● High Costs and business delays due to the Board of Control and Court of Directors
            raised concerns about the EIC efficiency.
            ● During this time, Politically informed Indians formed groups like Bombay Association
            and the Madras Native Association who demanded De - centralisation and greater
            participation in Governance.
            ● These issues collectively set the stage for the Act.
            Charter Act of 1853 (Main Provisions)
            ● It said that Indian Territories would remain ‘in trust for the Crown, until Parliament
            should otherwise direct’.
            ● Now the Company disappeared as a commercial agency, remaining as a political
            
            agent for the Crown.
            Legislative Provisions
            ● For the first time, The GG Council’s Legislative and Executive functions were separated.
            Legislation was treated as a special function of the Government. Local representation was
            introduced in the Indian legislature.
            ● Act Provided for addition of 6 new members to the council, known as Legislative
            Councillors. It created a distinct GG’s Legislative Council, which came to be known as ICS
            - Indian Legislative Council (Though no Indian representation was there)
            ○ The Chief Justice and the puisne judge of the Calcutta Supreme Court and
            Four representatives, one each from Bengal, Madras, Bombay and
            North-Western Provinces. The provincial representatives were to be civil servants
            of the Company with not less than a standing of 10 Years.
            ● Powers with these councillors in Legislative Assembly -
            ○ Questions could be asked, policy of the Executive Council could be discussed,
            Government could be discussed for its lapses, Bills were referred to Select
            Committees, and the Legislative Business was conducted in public.
            ○ However, a law to be promulgated needed the assent of the governor-general,
            and the Governor-General could veto any Bill of the legislative council.
            Executive Provisions -
            ● The Law member was made a full member of the GG Executive Council.
            ● Taking the strength back to 4.
            Charter Act of 1853
            ● Directors and their powers -
            ○ The number of directors was reduced from 24 to 18 of which 6 were to be
            appointed by the Crown.
            ■ The Court of Directors was divested of its patronage and power of
            appointments as the services were thrown open to Competitive
            examinations, in which no discrimination of any kind was to be made.
            (MaCaulay Committee).
            ● However, the Court of Directors was empowered to -
            ○ Constitute a new Presidency
            ○ Alter the boundaries of the existing state.
            ○ Incorporate the newly acquired state.
            
            ● This was because of the difficulties that were faced in administering the increasingly large
            Indian territories of Britain. Using this power, a separate Lt. Governorship was created
            for Punjab in 1859.
            ● Governor of Bengal - A separate post of Governor of Bengal also created.
            ● Open Competition System - It established an open competition system for civil servant
            selection and recruitment. As a result, the covenant civil service was made available to
            Indians as well.
            Assessment of the Act -
            ● The Charter Act of 1853 marked the beginning of the Parliamentary system in India.
            Under the Act -
            ○ Discussions in the Council, when acting in its legislative capacity, became verbal
            instead of in writing.
            ○ Bills passed through the usual stages and were referred to select committees.
            ○ Legislative Business was conducted In public instead of in secret and reports of
            proceedings were officially published.
            
            Indian National Movement (1885 - 1907)
            Events Leading to Formation of INC
            By 1885, the need for a pan Indian political organisation was evident, recognised by
            nationalists across India, with momentum building since 1870’s through various initiatives -
            ● Laws like Vernacular Press Act (1878) and Arms Act (1878) were met with widespread
            discontent.
            ● Ilbert Bill controversy
            ● Beginning of political actions in India in the form of groupings such as Indian National
            Association established by Surendranath Bannerjee in Kolkata.
            ● In 1884, at the Theosophical Society annual meet in Madras, delegates from across
            India recognised the need for a mediator to bring regional leaders together into a single
            organisation.
            ○ The delegates then chose A.O Hume as a mediator due to his wide acceptance
            across regions and his liberal political views.
            ● In May, 1885, Hume secured the viceroy’s approval to create an “Indian National Union”
            which would be affiliated with the Govt. and act as a platform to voice Indian public opinion.
            This led to the inception of INC.
            The Indian National Congress
            ● First session of the Indian National Congress was held at Gokuldas Tejpal Sanskrit
            College in Bombay in the week of 25 to 31 December 1885. (GG at this time - Lord
            Dufferin) (1885 to 1906, Hume was the General Secretary of the Congress).
            ● Hume and Lord Rippon were of the view that the educated middle class in India is here to
            stay and its legitimate aspirations should be met and grievances addressed.
            ○ Earlier the INC was to be known by the name of ‘Indian National Union’ whose first
            session was to be convened in Poona but due to outbreak of Cholera, the venue
            was shifted to Bombay.
            ● Controversy surrounding inception of INC (Mains Oriented)
            ○ Safety Valve Theory
            ○ Lightning Conductor Theory
            ● The first session of the Indian National Congress was attended by 72 delegates and
            presided over by Womesh Chandra Bonnerjee. (He was a Barrister and also the first
            Indian to contest the election for the British House of Commons, although he lost the
            
            elections)
            ● In total, 9 resolutions were passed in the first session which stated (No Need to remember
            them, Just have an Idea)
            ○ To abolish the Indian Council of Secretary of State for India.
            ○ Expansion and reform of the Imperial and Local Legislative Councils created by the
            Indian Councils Act of 1861.
            ○ Establishing Legislative Councils for Punjab etc.
            ○ Implementation of simultaneous Public Service Examination in England and India,
            as well as an increase in the minimum age of candidates.
            ● Early Composition of INC -
            ○ Educated Middle Class had the largest share of representation.
            ○ The Legal Profession was most heavily represented among all professions.
            ○ Brahmins were comparatively larger in number among the castes
            ○ Among the provinces, the presidencies of Bombay,Calcutta and Madras took the
            leading part.
            ○ The masses and landed class were majorly absent.
            Early Nationalists (1885 - 1905) - The Moderates
            Moderate Phase (1885 – 1905)
            ● Period of Tea-Party Politics or Political Mendicancy.
            ● From 72 delegates in Bombay, the Congress swelled to 434 delegates in Calcutta
            sessions (1886). These delegates were elected from various local bodies from different
            parts of the country. They decided to meet every year in a different part of the country
            every time.
            ● They were called as Moderates because they trusted in British Justice and aimed to
            protest within the bounds of the Law
            ● Period of Tea-Party Politics or Political Mendicancy.
            ● From 72 delegates in Bombay, the Congress swelled to 434 delegates in Calcutta
            sessions (1886). These delegates were elected from various local bodies from different
            parts of the country. They decided to meet every year in a different part of the country
            every time.
            ● They were called as Moderates because they trusted in British Justice and aimed to
            protest within the bounds of the Law
            
            Method of Work of the Moderates
            ● Peaceful, Constitutional Agitations within the four walls of the Law, they relied mainly
            on prayers and petitions, meetings and speeches and resolutions. (Through Press and
            Congress Sessions).
            ● The Congress sent deputations of Indian Leaders to Britain to present the Indian
            view point.
            ○ Dadabhai Naoroji became a British MP and spent a large part of his life working
            for Indian Interests over there.
            ○ In 1889, A British Committee of Indian National Congress was set up. In
            1890, the weekly Journal ‘India’ was started by this committee to educate the
            British people about Indian Issues.
            ○ In 1892, a session of the INC was scheduled to be held in London to spread
            awareness about Indian grievances but the idea was postponed due to British
            exceptions and was never taken up again.
            Political Demands
            Certain Provisions of 1892 Act were notable achievements for Moderates such as -
            ● Expansion of Legislative Council with more powers and representation of Indians in them.
            ● Introduction of discussions on budget matters.
            OTHER DEMANDS -
            ● Formation of Provincial Council
            ● Abolition of Indian Council
            ● More Opportunities for Indians in Civil Service and simultaneous examination for
            ICS in London and in India.
            ● End of aggressive foreign policy against Indian Neighbours.
            Economic Demands
            End of Economic Drain.
            ● Enquiry into the economic and industrial condition of the country.
            ● Reduction of Land Revenue
            ● Abolition of Salt Tax
            ● Encouragement to Modern Industry through direct Government Aid
            Agriculture Related Demands -
            ● Development of Agricultural Banks to end the atrocities of moneylenders.
            ● Extension of Irrigation facilities to save the people from famines.
            ● Introduction of permanent settlement in other parts of the country
            
            Military Demand
            ● Repeal of Arms Act
            ● Appointment of Indians in commissioned ranks in the Army
            ● Reduction of Military ExpenditureSocial Demands
            Social Demands
            ● More Expenditure on Welfare Activities
            ● Civil Rights like freedom of speech, thought, association and press.
            ● Improvement in condition of Plantation labourers.
            British Reaction to Congress
            ● Initially the attitude of the British was quite flexible towards the INC, However after 1887,
            their attitude stiffened and they began to brand Nationalists as ‘Disloyal Babus’,
            seditious Brahmins’ and ‘Violent Villians’.
            ● Challenging the national character of the Congress, Lord Dufferin stated that ‘Congress
            only represented a Microscopic Minority’. Henceforth the British began to encourage
            the pro-British elements like Sir Sayed Ahmed Khan and Raja Shiv Prasad of
            Banaras.
            ● The Aligarh Movement and United India Patriotic Association was formed to counter
            Congress. The British further pushed the policy of ‘Divide and Rule’ to increase the
            divide between Hindus and Muslims.
            ● In 1890’s, Government Employees were forbidden to attend Congress meetings.
            Achievements of the Moderates
            Political Achievements
            ● Indian Councils Act of 1892.
            ● ICS Examination in India - In 1893, The House of Commons passed a resolution for
            simultaneous examination for ICS in London and in India
            ● Demand for Swaraj - In 1905, during the Benaras Session, the Congress first demanded
            Swaraj or Self Rule for Indians within the British Empire on the model of self Governing
            colonies like Australia or Canada. (This demand was first referred to by GK Gokhale).
            ○ In 1906, at the Calcutta Session, Swaraj was explicitly demanded by Dadabhai
            Naoroji.
            ● Birth of Democratic Ideas.
            ● Training of Indians in Political Work and laid the foundation for rise of Extremists.
            
            Economic Achievements
            ● Economic Critique of British Rule - Early economists like Dadabhai Naoroji, RC Dutt
            and DE Wacha and others put forth the throw of ‘Drain of Wealth’ which explained how
            Indian Poverty was a result of colonial exploitation.
            ○ Naoroji first mentioned the drain theory in his book ‘Poverty and Un-British Rule in
            India’. In his book he ascertained that per capita Income was Rs 20 in 1867-68.
            ○ RC Dutt mentioned it in his book ‘Economic History in India’.
            ● Welby Commission on Indian Expenditure - in 1895, The Welby Commission on
            Indian Expenditure was appointed to enquire into Indian Expenditure.
            Constitutional agitation within the confines of the Law
            ● The Moderates believed that the British basically wanted to be just to the Indians but
            were not aware of the real conditions.
            ● Therefore, if public opinion could be created in the country and public demands be
            presented to the government through resolutions, petitions, meetings, etc., the
            authorities would concede these demands gradually.
            ● To achieve these ends, they worked on a two-pronged methodology.
            ○ Create a strong public opinion to arouse consciousness and national spirit
            and then educate and unite people on common political questions.
            ○ Persuade the British Government and British public opinion to introduce
            reforms in India on the lines laid out by the nationalists.
            ● They used the method of ‘prayer and petition‘ and if that failed, they resorted to
            constitutional agitation.
            Extremists
            Rise of the Extremists (Phase of Militant Nationalism or Radical Politics)
            (Please understand that terms like ‘Militant’ and ‘Terrorists’ should not be confused with their contemporary
            meanings. During that era, these words described extremist approaches, contrasting with Moderates.
            Similarly, Revolutionaries could be seen as a militant form of extremists, symbolizing aggressive resistance
            against British rule across different factions)
            Growth of Militant Nationalism
            ● A radical trend of a militant nationalist approach to political activity started emerging in
            the 1890s and it took a concrete shape by 1905 (during the Bengal Partition.
            
            Recognition of the True Nature of British Rule
            ● The economic miseries of the 1890s - Severe famines killed 90 lakh persons
            between 1896 and 1900.
            ● Bubonic plague affected large areas of the Deccan. There were large scale riots in the
            Deccan.
            ● Instead of giving more rights, the government was taking away existing ones.
            Imprisonment of Tilak (sedition), Official Secrets Act, Indian Universities Act etc.
            Growth of Confidence and Self-Respect
            ● There was a growing faith in self-effort. Tilak, Aurobindo and Bipin Chandra Pal
            repeatedly urged the nationalists to rely on the character and capacities of the Indian
            people.
            ● A feeling started gaining currency that the masses had to be involved in the battle
            against the colonial government as they were capable of making the immense
            sacrifices needed to win freedom.
            International Influences
            ● The defeat of the Italian army by Ethiopians (1896), the Boer wars (1899-1902) where
            the British faced reverses and Japan‘s victory over Russia (1905) demolished myths
            of European invincibility.
            ● Also, the nationalists were inspired by the nationalist movements worldwide—in Ireland,
            Russia, Egypt, Turkey, Persia and China.
            Growth of Education
            ● While, on the one hand, the spread of education led to an increased awareness among
            the masses, on the other hand, the rise in unemployment and underemployment among
            the educated drew attention to poverty and the underdeveloped state of the
            country‘s economy under colonial rule.
            Reaction to Increasing Westernisation
            ● Intellectuals like Swami Vivekananda, Bankim Chandra Chatterjee and Swami
            Dayananda Saraswati inspired many young nationalists with their forceful and
            articulate arguments, painting India‘s past in brighter colours than the British ideologues
            had.
            
            ● These thinkers exploded the myth of western superiority by referring to the richness of
            Indian civilization in the past. Dayananda‘s political message was India for the
            Indians‘.
            Dissatisfaction with Achievements of Moderates
            ● The younger elements within the Congress were dissatisfied with the achievements of
            the Moderates during the first 15-20 years.
            ● They were strongly critical of the methods of peaceful and constitutional agitation,
            popularly known as the ―Three ‭̳P‘s‖—prayer, petition and protest—and described
            these methods as political mendicancy‘.
            ● The Indian Council Act of 1892 was a complete disappointment.
            ● In 1897, The Natu brothers were deported without trial Just on the charge that they
            were associated with editorials in “Poona Vaibhav” Newspaper.
            ● In 1897, Tilak and other editors were sentenced to long terms of imprisonment for
            arousing the people against the Government.
            ● In 1898, exciting ‘Feeling of disaffection towards the British Government’ was made
            an offence.
            ● In 1899, in the Calcutta Corporation, the number of Indian Members was reduced.
            ● In 1904, The Indian Official Secrets act was passed restricting the freedom of the
            Press.
            ● Tilak described Congress as ‘A Congress of flatterers’ and the Congress session as ‘a
            Holiday Recreation’
            ● Tilak in 1880, founded the New English School which later became the Fergusson
            College. He did not believe in an Armed Struggle, rather he felt that Indians should
            withdraw from all active cooperation with the British and attain Swaraj through
            Self Help, determination and sacrifice. He gave the slogan - ‘Swaraj is my Birth
            Right and I shall have it’
            ● He was the first Congress leader to serve several terms of Imprisonment for
            speaking his mind against the Government.
            Reactionary Policies of Curzon
            ● A sharp reaction was created in the Indian mind by Curzon‘s seven-year rule in India
            which was full of missions, commissions and omissions. He refused to recognise India as
            a nation.
            
            ● Administrative measures adopted during his rule—the Official Secrets Act, the Indian
            Universities Act, the Calcutta Corporation Act and, above all, the partition of
            Bengal— left no doubt in Indian minds about the basically reactionary nature of British
            rule in India.
            Existence of a Militant School of Thought
            ● By the dawn of the twentieth century, a band of nationalist thinkers had emerged who
            advocated a more militant approach to political work.
            ● These included Raj Narain Bose, Ashwini Kumar Datta, Aurobindo Ghosh and Bipin
            Chandra Pal in Bengal; Vishnu Shastri Chiplunkar and Bal Gangadhar Tilak in
            Maharashtra; and Lala Lajpat Rai in Punjab.
            ● Tilak emerged as the most outstanding representative of this school of thought.
            The basic tenets of this school of thought were:
            ● Hatred for foreign rule;
            ● Swaraj to be the goal of national movement;
            ● Direct political action required;
            ● Belief in capacity of the masses to challenge the authority;
            ● Personal sacrifices required and a true nationalist to be always ready for it.
            Emergence of a Trained Leadership
            ● The new leadership could provide a proper channelisation of the immense potential
            for political struggle which the masses possessed and, as the militant nationalists
            thought, were ready to give expression to.
            ● This energy of the masses got a release during the movement against the partition of
            Bengal, which acquired the form of the swadeshi agitation.
            ● They firmly believed that the Swaraj could be obtained only through Direct Political Action
            based on self-reliance, self-sacrifice, and strong will.
            ● They had faith in the capacity of the masses and worked to make Both the Congress as
            well as the National Movements - Mass Movements.
            ● Inspired by India’s Glorious Past, But at the same time they had no desire to eternally
            dwell upon the past, but wanted to manfully build the future.
            Mode of Action - The Extremists Ideology
            ● They were inspired by the ideology of Swami Vivekanand, Dayanand Saraswati and
            
            Bankim Chandra Chatterjee.
            ● Programmes of Boycott and Swadeshi
            ● National Education - National Scheme of Education was set up for students who
            boycotted the Government Schools and Colleges.
            ○ Bengal Council of National Education was set up by Gurudas Banerjee.
            ● Non Cooperation or Passive Resistance
            ● Co-Operative Organisations.
            Work of Extremists
            ● In 1891, Tilak opposed the Age of Consent Bill (introduced after the death of Child
            Bride from sexual Injuries), Bill aimed to raise the age of consent for sexual intercourse
            for all girls, married or married, from 10 to 12 years (Violation subject tor rape) and
            insisted that British should not interfere in the private life of India.
            ○ (Ganpati Festivals) (Shivaji Festival) (Tilak Newspaper - Mahratta - English and
            Kesari - Marathi)
            ○ In 1895, Tilak ousted Gokhale and Ranade from Poona Sarvajanik Sabha and
            now the control of the Sabha came in the hand of the extremists.
            ○ Gokhale organised a separate association Called “Deccan Sabha” - This
            Completed the division between the Moderates and Extremists in
            Maharashtra.
            ● During 1893-94, Aurobindo published a series of Articles - “New Lamps for the Old” in
            the Indu Prakash wherein he described Congress as being out of Contact with the
            ‘Proletariat’ and as ‘Dying of Consumption.
            ● Extremists Group - They arose in three Geographical Regions - The Maharashtra
            Group led by Bal Gangadhar Tilak , the Bengal Group led by Bipin Chandra Pal
            and Aurobindo Ghose, and the Punjab Group led by Lala Lajpat Rai. (Lal - Bal -
            Pal).
            Difference between Moderates and Extremists
            ● Difference in the understanding of Swaraj.
            ● Difference in attitude towards British Benevolence
            ● Difference in Methodology - 3 P’s vs 3 S’s
            
            Some Important Moderates
            ● Surendranath Banerjee was called the Indian Burke.
            ○ He firmly opposed the Partition of Bengal.
            ○ He founded the Indian Association (1876) to agitate for political reforms.
            ○ He had convened the Indian National Conference (1883) which merged with
            the Indian National Congress in l886.
            ● G. Subramanya Aiyar preached nationalism through the Madras Mahajana Sabha.
            ○ He also founded the The Hindu and Swadesamitran
            ● Dadabhai Naoroji was known as the Grand Old Man of India.
            ○ He is regarded as India‘s unofficial Ambassador in England.
            ○ He was the first Indian to become a Member of the British House of
            Commons.
            ○ In his famous book Poverty and UnBritish Rule in India wrote his Drain Theory. He
            showed how India‘s wealth was going away to England in the form of:
            ■ salaries
            ■ savings
            ■ pensions
            ■ payments to British troops in India
            ■ profits of the British companies.
            ● Dayanand Sarawasti was the first man to give a call for Swaraj (India for Indians in
            1876), Some Historians call him ‘Grandfather of Indian Nation’
            ● Bankim Chandra Chaterjee (1838 - 1894) - He wrote the poem ‘Vande Matram’ to
            counter the British attempt, and was later published in Bengali script in his Novel
            Anandmath (1882), set in the backdrop of Sanyasi Rebellion.
            ○ During the Congress Session of 1896, the poem was first sung by
            Rabindranath Tagore.
            ○ In October 1937, The Congress adopted the first 2 verses of the Song was
            National Song of India.
            
            Indian National Struggle (1900 - 1911)
            ● Partition of Bengal
            ● Swadeshi Movement
            ● Surat Split
            Lord Curzon
            Curzonian Attack - GG and Viceroy of India (1899 - 1905)
            ● It is believed that he knew more about India and Indian Problems more than any other
            Viceroy.
            ● He came to India in the backdrop of the famine and plague of 1896-97. He tried to
            reform every department of the Indian System, his system of work was to first appoint an
            Expert Commission and the enact the required legislation.
            ● Police Reforms - Police Commission in 1902 under Sir Andrew Frazer - It reported that
            the Police was corrupt, oppressive and failed to secure the trust of the common man -
            Recommended increase in Police Salaries, setting up training schools, provincial police
            service and Central Department of Criminal Intelligence.
            ● Education Reforms - Universities Commission in 1902, in order to improve discipline
            and failing educational standards. He believed that Universities had become factories of
            political Revolutionaries. Based on the recommendations, he passed the Indian
            Universities Act of 1904 - Increased control over the Indian Universities and strict
            control over affiliation of private college.
            ● Army Reforms - Carried out by Lord Kitchener, commander in chief of army at that
            time, Indian Army was grouped in two commands, The Northern Command and the
            Southern Command. Every army battalion was to pass a sever test “Kitchener Test”
            ● Foreign Policy - He sent a special mission to Tibet headed by Colonel
            Younghusband to counter the Russian influence, and to Persian Gulf the mission was
            sent under Sir Henry McMohan
            Other Works
            ● Famine Commission - under Sir Anthony MacDonnell
            ● Irrigation Commission - Under Sir Colin Scott Moncrieff
            ● Co-Operative Credit Societies Act,1904 - Loans to cultivators at cheap rates.
            ● Ancient Monuments Act, 1904 - To protect and save the Historical Monuments in India.
            ● Partition of Bengal (1905) was announced.
            
            ● PUSA Agricultural Institute in 1903 established
            Partition of Bengal
            ● The British authorities said that Bengal had grown too big to be efficiently administered by
            a single provincial government. Bengal was a province with a population of 78 million
            which formed about a quarter of the population of British India. However, the plan they
            worked out showed other motives in mind. Bengal originally comprised Bengal, Bihar,
            Orissa and Assam.
            ○ (In 1874, Assam was separated from Bengal and made into Chief
            Commissioner’s Province).
            ● The British government‘s decision to partition Bengal had been made public in December
            1903.
            ● The idea was to have two provinces: Bengal comprising Western Bengal as well as
            the provinces of Bihar and Orissa, and Eastern Bengal and Assam.
            ● It sought to divide Bengal by putting the Bengalis under two administrations by
            dividing them:
            ○ On the basis of language, thus reducing the Bengalis to a minority in Bengal
            itself .
            ○ On the basis of religion, as the western half was to be a Hindu majority area
            and the eastern half was to be a Muslim majority area.
            (Please Note - The Nationalists had themselves supported the separation of
            
            Linguistically divergent Orissa and Bihar. They felt that the administrative
            efficiency could be better achieved by partition along linguistic lines)
            ● The Scheme for partition began with Curzon’s Minute on Terrestrial Redistribution on 1st
            June 1903.
            ● On 20 July 1905, Curzon issued the orders to divide Bengal into two parts.
            ○ Eastern Bengal and Assam - with a population of 31 Million.
            ○ Rest of Bengal with a population of 54 Million (18 Million Bengalis and 36
            Million Biharis and Oriyas).
            Anti – Partition Campaign
            ● During this period, the leadership was provided by men like Surendranath Banerjee,
            K.K. Mitra and Prithwishchandra Ray.
            ● The methods adopted were petitions to the Government, public meetings, memoranda,
            and propaganda through pamphlets and newspapers such as Hitabadi, Sanjibani and
            Bengalee.
            ● The objective was to exert pressure on the government through an educated public
            opinion in India and England to prevent the unjust partition from being implemented.
            ● Ignoring a loud public opinion against the partition proposal, the Government announced
            partition of Bengal in July 1905.
            ● On August 7, 1905, with the passage of the Boycott Resolution in a massive meeting held
            in the Calcutta Town hall, the formal proclamation of the Swadeshi Movement was
            made. The message was to boycott Manchester cloth and Liverpool salt.
            ● October 16, 1905, the day the partition formally came into force, was observed as a day
            of mourning throughout Bengal.
            ○ Vande Matram song became the song of the National Movement and Rabindranath
            Tagore composed the song ‘Amar Sonar Bangla’ for the occasion. (Later adopted
            by Bangladesh as its National Anthem in 1971).
            ○ Tagore also gave the call for Raksha Bandhan or tying of Rakhi as a symbol of
            unbreakable unity of Bengalis.
            ○ Ramendra Sunder Trivedi urged the observance of Aradhana - keeping the
            hearth unlit as a sign of mourning in all homes.
            ● Later in the day, Surendranath Banerjee and Ananda Mohan Bose addressed huge
            gatherings
            ● Soon, the movement spread to other parts of the country—in Poona and Bombay under
            
            Tilak, in Punjab under Lala Lajpat Rai and Ajit Singh, in Delhi under Syed Haider Raza,
            and Madras under Chidambaram Pillai.
            ● The Indian National Congress, meeting in 1905 under the presidentship of Gokhale,
            resolved to
            ○ Condemn the partition of Bengal and the reactionary policies of Curzon
            ○ Support the anti-partition and Launch of Swadeshi Movement of Bengal.
            ● The militant nationalists led by Tilak, Lajpat Rai, Bipin Chandra Pal and Aurobindo
            Ghosh wanted the movement to be taken outside Bengal to other parts of the
            country and go beyond a boycott of foreign goods to become a full-fledged political
            mass struggle with the goal of attaining swaraj.
            ● But the Moderates, dominating the Congress at that time, were not willing to go that far.
            Swadeshi Movement
            ● In the Congress session held at Calcutta (1906) under the presidentship of Dadabhai
            Naoroji, it was declared that the goal of the Indian Congress was 'self-government or
            swaraj like the United Kingdom or the colonies’ (For the First time Swaraj explicitly
            given)
            ○ Four Resolutions were passed in 1906 - Swaraj, Swadeshi, Boycott and National
            Education.
            ● The Moderate-Extremist dispute over the pace of the movement and techniques of
            struggle reached a deadlock at the Surat session of the Indian National Congress
            (1907) where the party split. (Rash Behari Ghosh)
            The Extremists found expression in periodicals like
            ● New India (edited by Bipin Chandra Pal)
            ● Vande Matram (edited by Aurobindo Ghose)
            ● Sandhya (edited by Brahmabandhav Upadhyaya)
            ● Yugantar (Edited by Bhupendranath Dutt)
            New Forms of Struggle
            ● Boycott of foreign goods: This included boycotts and public burning of foreign cloth,
            boycott of foreign made salt or sugar, refusal by priests to solemnize marriages involving
            exchange of foreign goods, refusal by washer-men to wash foreign clothes.
            ○ Boycott was first suggested in Krishna Kumar Mitra’s journal - Sanjibani.
            
            ○ Public meetings and processions: These emerged as major methods of
            mass-mobilisation and simultaneously as forms of popular expression.
            ○ Corps of volunteers or samitis: Samitis such as the Swadesh Bandhab
            Samiti of Ashwini Kumar Dutta (in Barisal) emerged as a very popular and
            powerful method of mass mobilisation.
            ● Imaginative use of traditional popular festivals and, melas
            ○ The idea was to use such occasions as a means of reaching out to the masses
            and spreading political messages.
            ○ For instance, Tilak's Ganapati and Shivaji festivals became a medium of
            swadeshi propaganda not only in western India, but also in Bengal.
            ○ In Bengal also, the traditional folk theatre forms were used for this purpose.
            ● Emphasis given to self-reliance or atma shakti
            ○ Swadeshi or indigenous enterprises: The swadeshi spirit also found expression
            in the establishment of swadeshi textile mills, soap and match factories, tanneries,
            banks, insurance companies, shops etc.
            ○ This implied reassertion of national dignity, honour and confidence and social and
            economic regeneration of the village.
            ■ Acharya PC Ray set up the famous Bengal Chemicals Factory
            Programme of swadeshi or national education
            ● Bengal National College, inspired by Tagore's Shantiniketan, was set up with Aurobindo
            Ghosh as its principal.
            ● Soon national schools and colleges sprang up in various parts of the country.
            ● On August 15, 1906, the National Council of Education was set up to organize a
            system of education—literary, scientific and technical—on national lines and under
            national control. (Vernacular was the chief medium of instruction)
            ● Education was to be imparted through the medium of vernaculars.
            ● A Bengal Institute of Technology was set up for technical education and funds were
            raised to send students to Japan for advanced learning.
            ○ Charly’s Circular - The Chief Secretary of Bengal Government threatening
            the students activists with Disciplinary action.
            Workers
            ● In October 1905, the first real labour union named the Printer’s Union was formed as a
            result of bitter strikes in Government Owned Presses.
            
            Peasants
            ● The movement was unable to make much headway in mobilising the peasantry, their
            participation remained negligible.
            ● In Southern Areas of Andhra and Tamil Nadu, gatherings were organised in the support
            of Bengal Anti partition Movement in 1906, known as Vandemataram Movement.
            Muslims
            ● They were unable to gain support of the Muslims as well, which proved to be its main
            weakness.
            ● In 1906, All India Muslim League under the leadership of Nawab Sallimullah of Dacca
            with the Government Patronage as a political Organisation dedicated to advance Muslim
            political interests.
            ○ At the peak of the Swadeshi Movement, Eastern Bengal saw a series of
            Communal Riots.
            The Surat Split
            ● In December 1905, at the Benaras session of the Indian National Congress presided over
            by Gokhale, the Moderate-Extremist differences came to the fore.
            ○ The Extremists wanted to extend the Boycott and Swadeshi Movement to regions
            outside Bengal and also to include all forms of associations (such as government
            service, law courts, legislative councils, etc.) within the boycott programme and
            thus start a nationwide mass movement.
            ○ The Moderates, on the other hand, were not in favour of extending the movement
            beyond Bengal and were totally opposed to boycott of councils and similar
            associations.
            ● They advocated strictly constitutional methods to protest against the partition of Bengal
            ● At the Calcutta session of the Congress in December 1906, the Moderate enthusiasm
            had cooled a bit because of the popularity of the Extremists and the revolutionary
            terrorists.
            ○ Dadabhai Naoroji was elected as the president and as a concession to the
            militants, the goal of the Indian National Congress was defined as swarajya or
            self-government like the United Kingdom or the colonies.
            ○ The Extremists, emboldened by the proceedings at the Calcutta session, gave a
