            ○ Both were determined to avert a repeat of the decisive 1907 Surat Split.
            ○ Following Gandhi’s advice, they opted to remain in Congress but operate
            independently. Thus leading to -
            ● Congress Special Session - Delhi - September 1923 - Maulana Azad president.
            
            ○ Allowed Congressmen to contest forthcoming elections.
            ○ Later, at the annual session at Kakinada, in 1923 allowed the council-entry by
            maintaining that Non-Cooperation could be practiced inside the councils also. Thus the
            split was avoided.
            Swarajists in Action
            ● 1923 - Swarajists first gathering at Allahabad. Swaraj Party declared ‘Dominion Status’ as
            its preliminary objective, keeping its long term goal undefined.
            ○ Released Election manifesto for forthcoming polls, focusing on following agenda -
            ■ Right for Indians to control their own government.
            ■ Disrupting 1919 reforms and halting administrative functions.
            ■ Aligning the Government with Indian Perspective.
            ■ Backing Gandhi’s constructive initiatives outside the Council.
            ● The Constitution and Ideology of Swaraj as a Party will evolve over time, also the Swaraj party
            solidified its position in Congress through the 1924 Belgaum Session, presided by Gandhi.
            ● The Party aimed to attain Swaraj through lawful and non violent means, although the exact
            definition of Swaraj remained unspecified. Also the Party was skeptical of mass political
            involvement.
            Party Makes it Mark
            ● General elections were held in British India in November 1923 for both the Central Legislative
            Assembly and Provincial Assemblies. The Central Legislative Assembly had 145 seats, of which
            105 were elected by the public.
            ● The Swarajists formed alliances with figures like M.A. Jinnah to
            
            create the Nationalist Party in the Central Legislative Assembly.
            ○ Motilal Nehru thus served as opposition Leader. They came to be known as the
            ‘Parliamentary Wing of the Congress’.
            ■ In March 1925, the Swarajists succeeded in electing Vithalbhai Patel as the
            President of the Central Legislative Assembly.
            ○ They emerged to be the largest party in Bombay, Central Assembly and Bengal Councils.
            Similar alliances were crafted in various provinces. Their strategy was to consistently
            challenge the government.
            ● The Constitution and Ideology of Swaraj as a Party will evolve over time.
            ● The Party aimed to attain Swaraj through lawful and non violent means, although the exact
            definition of Swaraj remained unspecified. Also the Party was skeptical of mass political
            involvement.
            Swarajists Mean Business
            ● Focused on obstructing reforms from within the system. British Authorities observed that
            this form of ‘sabotage posed greater challenges than outright rebellion’.
            ○ Rejected Votable parts of the budgets and expenditures to affirm their rights.
            ○ Veto any legislation that might enhance bureaucratic power.
            ● On the constructive side, they aimed to -
            ○ Establishing economic strategies to prevent the outflow of wealth - Boosting local
            economic and industrial sector.,
            ○ Champion the rights of workers and other labour working class.
            
            CR Das says - “I want you to enter the Councils and secure a majority and put forward national
            demands. If they are not accepted. I want you to oppose the Government in every measure, good, bad,
            and indifferent and make the work of Council impossible”
            Gandhi’s Evolving Stance
            ● Released from Jail in February 1924, disputes surfaced within the Congress concerning the
            issue of Council entries, something he was opposed to ideologically.
            ● June 1924, AICC meeting in Ahmedabad, Pushed for resolution to sideline the Swarajists,
            mandating dissenters to resign from AICC.(Hand Spinning Resolution)
            ○ However, by now, Swarajists faction has also grown stronger, Thus Gandhi moderated his
            stance leading to compromised resolution.
            ○ Eventually he supported the Swarajists, endorsing them as Congress’s official negotiator
            with the government.
            ● 1924, Congress Session of Belgaum - Only Session where Gandhi presided.
            ○ Truce between Pro and No Changers established. Gandhi gave the majority of seats to
            Swarajists in his Working Committee.
            ○ Session reinforced the Constructive programs as the primary strategy for achieving
            Swaraj,
            Achievements of Swarajists
            ● Legislative Achievements -
            ○ Advocated for release of all political prisoners, provincial autonomy and an immediate
            RTC to discuss full self governance.
            ○ 1928, defeated Public Safety Bill, targeting Socialist and Communist activities by
            allowing detention and deportation without trial.
            ○ Swarajists overthrew Dyarchy in Bengal and Central Provinces, cut salt tax and train
            fares, secured tax relief for provinces, scrapped excise duties and pushed for tariffs to
            support local industries.
            ○ Other Key Legislations actively passed by Swarajists -
            ■ Workmen’s Compensation Act,1923
            ○ Indian Trade Union Act,1926
            
            ○ Indian bar Councils Act,1926
            ○ Child Marriage Prevention Act,1926
            ● Repressive Laws repealed like State Prisoners Act, 1850, Prevention of Seditious
            meetings Act of 1921 to name the few.
            Vithalbhai Patel became the speaker of the Central Legislative Assembly in 1925.
            ● Muddiman Commission - Due to Swaraj Party’s persistence led to Indian Government
            establishing a committee to investigate defects in 1919 Act and propose solutions.
            ● Filling the Political Vacuum
            ● They also promoted Constructive work outside the Council like -
            ○ Promotion of Khadi - Although resisted its compulsory status for Congress
            membership
            ○ Eradication of Untouchability - Made a resolution in support of Vaikom Satyagraha
            ○ Advocation of Alcohol prohibition
            Failure of Swarajists
            ● Lack of coordination with Mass Struggle.
            ● Limitation of Obstructionist Strategy.
            ● Conflicting Ideas with Coalition Partners.
            ● Failure to resist Perks and Privileges.
            ● Failure of Support Peasants’ Causes.
            Bipin Chandra writes - ‘The Swarajists failed to provide a coherent strategy for the national movement’
            and their tendency to rely on the councils alone was a strategic Error’
            Decline of Swaraj Party
            ● 1925 onwards, Swarajists position in the councils gradually weakened due to -
            ○ The sudden demise of C.R. Das
            ○ Widespread communal riots and Loss of Popular Base
            ○ Split among communal Responsivists and Non-Responsivist Lines.
            All these led to major losses for the Swaraj party in the 1926 elections, Also the arrival of Simon
            Commission completely changed the political Landscape. Thus, the Council Entry movement lost its
            
            impact, leading to the Swaraj Party merger with Congress for a unified push towards complete
            Independence.
            In 1930, Swarajists left the councils due to Lahore Congress resolution on Poorna Swaraj and the start of
            Civil Disobedience Movement.
            Understanding Responsivists and Non-Responsivists
            Responsivists
            Advocated for cooperation with the Government and holding office wherever possible
            Leaders - Pandit Malviya, Lala Lajpat Rai
            Non - Responsivists
            Against the idea of office acceptance and wanted to follow the path of obstruction rather than
            cooperation.
            Leaders - Motilal Nehru
            Formation of Swaraj Party (1st January 1923)
            (Please Note - In Merged Notes, Part 1 we have covered British Acts and Regulations till 1853)
            British Acts and Regulations (1858 - 1935)
            
            GOI Act, 1858 - Background
            ● The Revolt of 1857 gave a death blow to the existing system of Governance, and gave an
            immediate push to demand for transfer of power from the Company to the crown.
            ● The bill for the transfer of control of Government of India from the East India Company to the
            Crown was first introduced by Lord Palmerston. (British PM)
            ● Later, Edward Henry Stanley (He became the first Secretary of State for India) introduced a bill -
            “An Act for the Better Government of India” was introduced and passed on 2 August 1858.
            GOI Act, 1858 - Main Provisions
            ● The Act ended the Company’s rule in India and power was transferred from the Company to the
            Crown.
            ● The Court of Directors and the Board of Control were abolished and their powers transferred
            to the Principal Secretary of State for India and India Council.
            ○ A 15 Member Indian Council to aid and advise the Secretary of State. They were to
            govern India in the name of the Crown.
            ■ The Secretary of State was a member of the British Cabinet as such responsible
            to the Parliament . He was required to submit a periodic report on the moral and
            material progress of India.
            ○ The Act also declared the Secretary of State for India as a corporate body who could
            sue and be sued in England and in India.
            ● Also, The Governor General received the title of Viceroy, as he was now the direct
            representative of the Crown.
            
            GOI Act, 1858 - Significance
            ● Brought an end to the Company’s rule in India and at the same time, the transfer of power was
            more ‘formal’ than ‘substantial’.
            ○ It was intended that the change of the name will immediately condone the past and give
            a fresh starting point to the empire.
            ● After a few months, The Act was followed by Queen Victoria's proclamation on 1 November
            1858 at grand Durbar held at Allahabad.
            ● This document is known as ‘Magna Carta of the people of India’ and it declared the future policy
            of British rule in India.
            Indian Councils Act, 1861 - Background
            ● The Regulating Act had begun the process of centralisation culminating in the Act of 1833
            which took away legislative powers from the province, but the Indian Councils Act of 1861
            started an opposite process.
            ○ A process of decentralisation.
            ○ Lord Canning (Governor General) and Sir Charles Wood (Secretary of State) were possible
            for the passing of the Act of 1861.
            Indian Councils Act, 1861 - Main Provisions
            ● Changes in the Viceroy’s Executive Council -
            ○ Enlarged by the addition of a fifth member who was a jurist.
            ● Changes in the Central Legislature -
            ○ Changes in size and Composition -
            ■ For the purpose of legislation, it enlarged the Central Council by addition of not
            less than six and not more than twelve. Additional Members were to be
            nominated by the GG for a period of 2 years and it was provided that at least half
            the them were to be non-official. (Further, the function of the Council for
            legislative purposes was confined strictly to legislature only)
            ○ Changes in functions -
            ■ The Act restored the legislative powers of Councils of Bombay and Madras.
            However, rigid restrictions were placed on legislative powers of these provinces
            
            and no laws passed by the provincial councils were to be valid until they received
            the assent of the GG.
            Other Changes Introduced -
            ● The Act allowed establishments of new Councils in other Provinces. Accordingly, North
            Western Provinces (1886) and Burma and Punjab (1897) were established.
            ● The GG was empowered to issue ordinances in case of emergency and without the
            concurrence of the Legislative Council, which could remain in force for a maximum period of six
            months.
            ● GG was empowered to create new provinces for legislative purposes and appoint
            Lieutenant Governors for them. It also empowered him to make territorial adjustments between
            different provinces.
            ● Provision regarding decentralisation of Business
            ○ The Act empowered the GG to make rules for more convenient transactions of business in
            council.
            ○ Using this authority, Lord Canning introduced the portfolio system in India for the first
            time. Canning now divided the various departments among the council members, thus
            laying the foundation of the cabinet system in India.
            Indian Councils Act, 1861 - Limitation
            ● However, it is noteworthy that no attempt was made to demarcate the legislative powers of
            Central and Local Legislatures and the GG could legislate for the whole of India.
            Indian Councils Act, 1892 - Main Provisions
            ● Changes in the Central Legislature -
            ○ Act increased the number of additional members in the Central Legislature ,
            ■ Increased to not less than 10 and not more than 16 and at least half were to be
            non official.
            ■ It also provided for non official members in the Provincial Legislature. (8 to 12).
            ○ However in the central as well as provincial legislature official majority was maintained.
            ● Other Changes -
            ○ The Act empowered the GG to introduce a system of limited elections.
            
            ■ The Act empowered the GG-in-Council to make regulations for nomination of
            additional members and to prescribe the manner in which such regulations should
            be put into effect. (it must be noted that these members occupied their seats in the
            council not as representatives of the various bodies but as nominees of the GG)
            ■
            ■ Under this provision, the GG could introduce a half hearted system of election
            (limited or indirect elections), even as the provision itself very carefully avoided
            the use of word election.
            ● Other Changes -
            ○ The Act enlarged the functions of the Councils -
            ■ The members of the Central Legislature were now allowed to hold a discussion on
            the annual financial statement. (but the statement was unalterable)
            ■ The members could not only discuss their views, they could also ask questions to
            the Government on matters of public interest after six days' notice.
            ■ Supplementary questions were not allowed.
            ○ This Act was criticised by the Congress during its session in 1892 and 1893 because it
            failed to introduce the system of direct elections in India.
            Morley Minto Reforms 1909 - Background
            ● These reforms were introduced in the backdrop of increasing political unrest and
            dissatisfaction with British rule in India.
            ● In 1906, The British Liberal Party won the general election. This boosted the possibility of
            reform in British India. Liberals believed that increasing the participation of Indians in
            legislatures would improve British reign over India.
            ● 1906 - Shimla Deputation led by Agha Khan and other Muslim leaders presented Lord Minto
            with a proposal for separate Muslim electorates.
            Morley Minto Reforms 1909 - Main Provisions
            ● Indian Entry in Viceroy’s Executive Council -
            ○ Satyendra Sinha became the first Indian appointment to the Governor’s executive council
            in 1909.
            ● Expansion of Members (No Need to remember the exact numbers)
            
            ○ Elected member numbers increased in both Imperial and Provincial Legislative Councils,
            with a non-official majority in provincial councils.
            ○ Central Legislative Council - Total 69 members, 37 official, 5 nominated non official and 27
            elected non officials, with seats specifically reserved for Muslims, British Capitalists,
            Landlords and a General Electorate.
            ○ Declared that territorial representation did not suit India and that ‘representation by
            classes and interests is the only practical method of embodying the elective principle of
            Legislative Councils.
            ● Legislative Powers
            ○ Under the Act of 1909, the functions of the Councils were enlarged in three ways -
            ■ The members were allowed to discuss the financial statement at length and
            move resolutions on it, before it was presented in final form.
            ■ The members were given the right to move resolutions on matters of general
            public interest. But these resolutions were to be in the form of recommendations
            only and may or may not be accepted by the Government. However, there were
            certain subjects on which questions could not be asked such as foreign relations,
            expenditure on railways, interest on debt etc.
            ■ The right to ask questions was also extended and all the members asking original
            questions were given the right to ask supplementary questions as well.
            ● Introduction of Elections
            ○ Indirect elections for non-official council members were introduced, allowing Indian
            participants based on class and community.
            ■ The Act enabled certain recognized bodies and associations to recommend
            candidates.
            ● Provision of Separate Electorates
            ○ For Muslims in the Central Legislative Council.
            ○ Also, Muslims given representation beyond their population, with low income
            qualifications for voting serving strategic Interests for the British authority.
            Montagu Chelmsford Reforms 1919 - Background
            ● The Act carried a preamble which laid down the basic principle on which reforms were to be
            carried out. It was based on the Montagu declaration of 20th August 1917 -
            
            ● Montagu Declaration of 1917
            ○ "The policy of His Majesty's Government is that of the increasing association of Indians in
            every branch of the administration and the gradual development of self-governing
            institutions with a view to the progressive realization of responsible government in India
            as an integral part of the British Empire."
            Montague Chelmsford Reforms 1919 - Provisions
            Changes in Home Government
            ● High Commissioner for India -
            ○ Appointed in London, responsible for Indian Trade in Europe.
            ○ Also SOS would not be paid out of British Exchequer and not out of Indian Revenue.
            Changes in Government of India
            ● Viceroy Executive Council
            ○ To implement the policy of increasing association of Indians, of the eight members of the
            GG’s executive council, three would be Indians.
            ● Bicameral System -
            ○ The Act replaced the single Indian Legislative Council with two separate houses - The
            Council of States (Upper House) and the Legislative Assembly (Lower House).
            ○ Members in both the houses were elected, although Voting was of limited franchise.
            ● Expansion of Functions of Councils -
            ○ The right to ask Supplementary questions was extended to all members of the
            Legislature. (Under the Act of 1909, members asking original questions alone could ask
            supplementary questions). Short notice questions could be asked, The members
            enjoyed the freedom of speech.
            Changes in Provincial Governments
            ● Provincial Autonomy was introduced for the first time in India.
            ○ The independent powers of the provinces were defined in the ‘Devolution Rules’.
            Devolution was brought about in areas of Finance, legislation and administration.
            ● Legislative Devolution -
            ○ This was brought about by separating the items of legislation by the Centre and the
            Provinces. Accordingly, two lists were drawn: Central List and Provincial List. Residual
            
            powers were vested in the GG-in-council
            ○ Inside this Provincial List - Powers were divided into “Transferred” (Administered by
            Indian Ministers accountable to the Legislative council) and “Reserved” subjects
            (Administered by Governors and his Executive Council without being accountable to
            Legislative Council). This dual system is called Dyarchy.
            Changes in Provincial Governments
            ● Financial Devolution -
            ○ Complete separation of sources of revenue between the centre and the provinces.
            Accordingly land revenue, excise, irrigation and stamps were made exclusively
            provincial items.
            ○ Provincial budgets separated from the Central Budget, and provincial legislatures were
            authorized to make their own budgets.
            Other Provisions
            ● Direct Elections with Limited Franchise.
            ○ Direct Elections introduced for the first time. People could vote for members in both
            Upper and Lower House.
            ● Communal Representation
            ○ The Act extended communal representation, providing separate electorates for Sikhs,
            Indian Christians, Anglo-Indians, Europeans in addition to Muslims. (Seats distributed as
            per Strategic Importance)
            ● Public Service Commission -
            ○ The Central Public Service Commission was established in 1926 under this Act to recruit
            civil servants.
            ● Statutory Commission -
            ○ Act provided for appointment of a statutory commission to inquire it and report on its
            workings after 10 Years.
            Points to Know
            ● Originally the Act of 1919, applied only to 8 provinces
            ○ Bengal, Madras, Bombay, UP, Punjab, Bihar, Orissa and Central Provinces.
            ○ In 1923, the Act was extended to Burma and later to NWFP.
            ● Women couldn't vote nor contest elections.
            
            ○ In Britain itself, women got the right to vote only after 1918.
            ● Muddiman Report
            ○ Analysis of Dyarchy.
            GOI Act 1935
            ● GOI Act 1935 was based on White Paper on the Indian Constitution prepared by the British
            Government after the Third Round Table Conference.
            ○ Falling short of Complete Independence, the White paper was criticised by all the political
            parties of that time.
            ● GOI Act of 1935 contained 4 major provisions -
            ○ All India Federation, Provincial Autonomy, dyarchy at the centre and safeguards which
            vested special powers with the central and provincial executive.
            ● It also provided legal safeguards against misbehaviour of Indian Ministers and Legislators.
            Another notable feature was the absence of Preamble.
            GOI Act 1935 - Changes at National Level
            ● Proposal for an All India Federation (Never materialised)
            ○ For the States, accession to Federation was voluntary and Federation could not be formed
            unless two conditions were met -
            ■ States entitled to at least half of the seats (52) allotted to the states in the proposed
            
            Council of States should agree to join the federation.
            ■ The aggregate population of states in the above category should be at least 50% of
            the total population of the Indian States.
            ● Abolition of Indian Council - which was established under the Act of 1858, on account of much
            agitation in India against its Anti-India policies.
            GOI Act 1935 - Changes in GOI
            ● Dyarchy at the Centre
            ○ Dyarchy now from provinces was introduced in the dyarchy at centre.
            ○ Reserved Departments were under the GG who was to be administered by his counsellors
            appointed by him and responsible only to him.
            ○ Transferred subjects were to be administered by the GG on the advice of popular ministers
            answerable to the legislature.
            ● Bicameral Central Legislature
            ○ GG and the two houses - The Council of States and the Federal Assembly.
            ○ Both Houses had representation from British India as well as Princely States,with Princely
            States weightage being disproportionate as the states were expected to act as checks
            upon the nationalists.
            GOI Act 1935 - Changes in Provisional Govt.
            ● Provincial Autonomy -
            ○ All provincial departments would be controlled by elected members.
            ○ For the first time, the Act recognised provinces as separate legal entities, free from
            directions and control of GOI and SOS except for specific purposes.
            ○ Now, the province’s derived their power and authority directly from the British Crown.
            ○ Special Powers of Governor -The Governor could issue ordinances or enact Governor’s
            Acts.
            ● Abolition of dyarchy in provinces -
            ○ The distinction between Reserved and Transferred subjects was abolished and a fully
            responsible Government was established subject to certain safeguards.
            ● Bicameral Legislature
            ○ Introduced in 6 provinces - Bengal, Madras, Bombay, United Provinces, Bihar and Assam.
            
            GOI Act 1935 - Other Provisions
            ● Creation of new provinces - Burma was separated from India.
            ● Franchise - It was lowered, though still remained tied to property qualification.
            ● Extension of Separate Electorates - Now it also includes women.
            ● Other Federal Institutions -
            ○ This act provided for the establishment of the Federal Bank and Federal Court (1935 and
            1937).
            ○ The Act vested the control of Railways in a new authority called Federal Railway Authority,
            free from control of ministers and councillors.
            Simon Commission (1927)
            Simon Commission (Indian Statutory Commission)
            Background and Formation
            ● The Government of India Act, 1919, included a provision for a commission to review India's
            progress in governance after 10 years and suggest further reforms.
            ● Despite reforms being due in 1929, the Conservative government (led by Stanley Baldwin)
            advanced the commission to November 8, 1927, fearing defeat by the Labour Party and
            potential policy changes.
            ● The Simon Commission was an all-white, seven-member body chaired by Sir John Simon. It
            excluded Indians, justified on the grounds that the committee reported to the British Parliament
            (despite two Indian members serving in the British Parliament).
            ● Purpose: To assess India's readiness for further constitutional reforms and recommend their
            nature.
            ● Appointed under the pressure of Swaraj Party activities and youth movements led by Jawaharlal
            Nehru and Subhas Chandra Bose.
            Important Reports before Simon Commission
            ● Lee Commission (Examined the Racial composition of Imperial Civil Services)
            ● Muddiman Commission (Examined the problems of dyarchy in GOI ACT 1919)
            ● Linlithgow Commission (Explored the crisis in Indian Agriculture and examine Rural
            
            Economy)
            Congress and Other Political Reactions
            ● Congress Opposition:
            ○ At the Madras Session (December 1927), presided over by M.A. Ansari, Congress resolved
            to boycott the commission "at every stage and in every form."
            ○ Jawaharlal Nehru pushed for Complete Independence instead of Dominion Status.
            ○ Congress decided to set up an All Parties Conference to draft a constitution for India in
            consultation with other organizations.
            ● Other Boycotters:
            ○ Hindu Mahasabha, Liberal Federation (led by Tej Bahadur Sapru), majority faction of the
            Muslim League (under Jinnah), and Indian Industrial and Commercial Congress
            opposed the commission.
            ● Supporters to Commission:
            ○ Unionists in Punjab, Justice Party in the South, and All India Achyut Federation chose
            not to boycott the commission.
            Delhi Proposals (December 1927)
            ● Aimed to unite the Congress and Muslim League for common demands. Key demands by Muslim
            leaders, with Jinnah presiding:
            ○ Joint electorates with reserved seats for Muslims.
            ○ One-third representation for Muslims in the Central Legislative Assembly.
            ○ Representation in Punjab and Bengal proportional to the Muslim population.
            ○ Formation of new Muslim-majority provinces: Sindh, Balochistan, NWFP.
            ● Congress accepted most demands (not every), but internal splits in the Muslim League and
            opposition from the Hindu Mahasabha derailed consensus.
            All Parties Conference (1928)
            ● M.A. Ansari convened the conference, with representatives from Congress, Muslim League, Hindu
            Mahasabha, Sikh Central League and others.
            ● Agreed on Dominion Status as the goal for a unified constitution.
            
            ● Leading to the formation of a constitutional report led by Motilal Nehru in May 1928. (Marking
            India’s first efforts to frame its constitution)
            Challenges and Outcomes
            ● The Simon Commission's exclusion of Indians was defended by Lord Birkenhead, citing "vital
            differences" among Indian political groups.
            ● Birkenhead challenged Indians to draft a constitution acceptable to all sections of society, laying
            the groundwork for the Nehru Report (1928).
            Lord Birkenhead’s Challenge
            ● Lord Birkenhead criticized Indian leaders for their inability to present a unified constitution
            acceptable to all communities.
            ● This challenge spurred efforts to draft a constitution to counter his claims and respond to the
            Simon Commission's exclusion of Indians.
            The Impending Challenge of Constitution Drafting
            Key Recommendations of Nehru Report (May 1928)
            ● Dominion Status: India to be a self-governing dominion, known as the Commonwealth of India.
            ● Fundamental Rights:
            ○ Universal adult suffrage with Equal rights like Freedom of Religion, speech for all citizens,
            irrespective of religion, caste, or gender.
            ● Minority Safeguards:
            ○ Joint electorates with reserved seats for minorities.
            ○ Special provisions for Muslim-majority provinces (e.g., Sindh separated from Bombay
            Presidency, NWFP given provincial status).
            ● Provincial Reorganization: Provinces to be reorganized on a linguistic basis.
            ● Federal Structure:
            ○ Federal government with provincial autonomy.
            ○ Residual powers with the center (seen as more unitary than federal).
            ● Responsible Government: Bicameral legislature and hierarchy of courts at the center.
            
            Additional Recommendations:
            ● Baluchistan to be granted full provincial status.
            ● Communal representation to be reconsidered after 20 years.
            Point to Note - The Nehru Committee faced a complex problem with regard to the status of Princely
            states. In 1927, the subjects of Princely states had formed the States People Conference with the
            objective of introducing self Governing Institutions.
            The Princes felt threatened and sought help from the British who appointed a committee under
            the chairmanship of Sir Harcourt Butler to look into the issue. The Butler committee laid stress on the
            preservation of Princely states through British Paramountcy.
            Calcutta Congress Session (December 1928)
            ● The Nehru Report was approved, but younger leaders like Jawaharlal Nehru, Subhas Chandra
            Bose, and S. Satyamurthy expressed dissatisfaction with dominion status as the goal. (In April
            1928 - Independence of India League was formed by J.L Nehru and Bose as Secretary and
            S.Srinivasa Iyengar as President.)
            ● Under pressure from these younger leaders, Congress reduced the grace period for dominion
            status acceptance to one year.
            ● Congress resolved to demand complete independence if dominion status was not granted by the
            end of 1929, launching a civil disobedience movement to achieve it.
            Opposition and Amendments
            ● At the Calcutta Session (December 1928), the Nehru Report faced opposition from:
            ○ Muslim League (led by Jinnah).
            ○ Hindu Mahasabha.
            ○ Sikh League.
            ● Jinnah’s Proposed Amendments (Rejected by Congress):
            ○ One-third representation for Muslims in the central legislature.
            ○ Proportional representation in Punjab and Bengal.
            
            ○ Residual powers with provinces.
            Result: Jinnah presented his 14 Points (March 1929), forming the basis for Muslim League’s future
            demands.
            Nehru Report Compromises and Submission
            ● Due to lack of support from other other parties, certain compromises were made to the Nehru
            Report like -
            ○ Joint Electorate everywhere except where they are in majority.
            ○ Sindh to be detached from Bombay only after Dominion Status is granted.
            ○ Additionally, a unitary political structure was proposed, as residual powers rested with the
            center.
            ● All Parties Conference approved the Nehru Report but did not include Jinnah’s proposed
            amendments. Congress gave the British one year deadline to accept the report, threatening
            with a Non Violent protest in 1930 if they failed to do so.
            March to Civil Disobedience Movement and The Story
            Gandhi’s Preparations for Action
            ● Nationwide Mobilization:
            ○ Gandhi traveled extensively in 1929, preparing the public for direct political action.
            ○ Encouraged youth to brace for challenges and emphasized constructive work in villages.
            ● Boycott of Foreign Goods:
            ○ The Congress Working Committee (CWC) formed a Foreign Cloth Boycott Committee to
            promote public burning of foreign cloth and encourage the use of Indian-made goods.
            Lord Irwin’s Declaration (October 1929) (Diwali Declaration)
            ● Acknowledged dominion status as the ultimate goal of India’s constitutional progress,
            referencing the Montagu Declaration (1917).
            ● Promised a Round Table Conference post-Simon Commission's report but without a specific
            timeline, which disappointed Indian leaders.
            
            Delhi Manifesto (November 2, 1929)
            ● Prominent national leaders outlined conditions for attending the Round Table Conference:
            ○ The conference should aim to formulate a constitution to implement dominion status,
            acting as a constituent assembly.
            ○ Congress should have majority representation.
            ○ A general amnesty for political prisoners.
            ● Viceroy Irwin rejected these demands, escalating tensions and paving the way for
            confrontation.
            Lahore Congress Session (December 1929)
            ● Jawaharlal Nehru was elected as the Congress President.
            ● Key Resolutions:
            ○ Declared complete independence (Poorna Swaraj) as Congress's ultimate goal.
            ○ Decided to boycott the Round Table Conference.
            ○ Empowered the CWC to launch a civil disobedience movement, including non-payment
            of taxes.
            ○ Called upon Congress members in legislatures to resign their seats.
            First Independence Day (January 26, 1930)
            ● January 26, 1930, was observed as the first Independence Day (Swarajya Day).
            ● December 31, 1929:
            ○ On the banks of the Ravi River, Nehru hoisted the tricolor flag, symbolizing freedom,
            amidst slogans of Inquilab Zindabad.
            ○ Across India, public meetings were held, the Independence Pledge was read in local
            languages, and the national flag was hoisted in towns and villages.
            GANDHI’S 11 POINT ULTIMATUM
            Demands Of General Interest
            1. Reduce expenditure on Army and salaries of highest-grade services by 50%.
            2. Prohibition of intoxicants.
            
            3. Reform in the Criminal Investigation Department (CID).
            4. Release political prisoners.
            5. Accept Postal Reservation Bill.
            6. Changes in the Arms Act 1878, allowing the popular control of issues of firearms licenses.
            Demands Catering To Capitalists' Interests
            7. Reduce rupee-sterling ratio to 1s 4d (explained in the box below).
            8. Reserve coastal shipping for Indians.
            9. Protect Indian Textiles.
            Demands Catering To Peasants' Interests
            10. Reduce Agricultural tax by 50% and make it a subject of legislative control.
            11. Eliminate the government's monopoly on salt and abolish the salt tax.
            ● Please Note - When the Gov’t didn’t adequately address Gandhi’s demands, the CWC authorized
            Gandhi to launch the CDM at his discretion.
            ● Gandhi wrote “On bended knees I asked for bread and received a stone instead. The English
            nation responds only to force and I am not surprised by the viceregal reply”
            (Special Mention - Royal Commission on Indian Currency and Finance 1926 also known as Hilton
            Young Commission - Upon the Recommendation of this commission - RBI was established in Year 1935).
            
            Launch of CDM
            Preparations for Civil Disobedience
            ● Congress Resolution: The Congress Working Committee (CWC) empowered Gandhi to decide
            the timing and place to launch the Civil Disobedience Movement.
            ● Salt as a Symbol: By February 1930, Gandhi identified the salt tax as a unifying issue due to its
            universal impact on Indians.
            Dandi March (March 12 – April 6, 1930)
            ● The Plan:
            ○ Gandhi informed the Viceroy of his intentions on March 2, 1930.
            ○ Starting with 78 followers from Sabarmati Ashram, Gandhi marched 240 miles to Dandi,
            addressing crowds along the way.
            ○ On April 6, 1930, he violated the salt law by picking up a lump of salt, inspiring millions to
            follow suit.
            ● Symbolism:
            ○ The march highlighted nonviolent resistance and India's collective resolve against British
            laws.
            ○ Subhas Chandra Bose compared it to Napoleon’s march to Paris.
            Civil Disobedience Movement - Phase 1
            Spread of Civil Disobedience
            ● Regional Movements:
            ○ Tamil Nadu: C. Rajagopalachari salt march to Vedaranniyam, supported by anti-liquor
            campaigns.
            ○ Malabar: K. Kelappan led salt marches; P. Krishna Pillai resisted police brutality at Calicut.
            ○ Andhra Pradesh: Organized salt marshes in East and West Godavari, Krishna, and Guntur.
            ○ Bengal: Salt protests in Midnapur and rural areas, combined with tax resistance.
            ○ Assam: Students protested the Cunningham Circular restricting political participation.
            ○ Peshawar: Demonstrations led by locals, supported by Garhwal Rifles, which refused to
            fire on unarmed crowds.
            
            ○ Sholapur: Workers struck, destroyed symbols of British authority, and established a
            parallel government.
            ○ Manipur and Nagaland - Rani Gaidinliu, a 13-year-old Naga spiritual leader, raised the
            banner of revolt against British rule, following her cousin Haipou Jadonang.
            ● Dharasana Raid:
            ○ After Gandhi’s arrest on May 4, 1930, Sarojini Naidu and others led a nonviolent raid on
            the Dharasana Salt Works, facing brutal repression.
            Spread of Civil Disobedience
            ● Other Forms of Mobilization:
            ○ Mass Engagement Tools:
            ■ Prabhat pheris (early morning processions), vanar senas (youth brigades),
            manjari senas (women’s brigades).
            ■ Secret pamphlets (patrikas) and magic lantern shows were used to spread
            awareness.
            ○ Forest Law Violations: Protests in Central Provinces and Karnataka.
            ○ No-tax Campaigns: Villagers in Gujarat avoided paying taxes by migrating to neighboring
            princely states.
            Gov’t Response to the Movement
            ● 2 Ordinances and other measure were promulgated to limit civil liberties, in response of
            India’s CDM -
            ○ The Prevention of Intimidation Ordinance
            ○ The Unlawful Instigation Ordinance
            ○ In June 1930, Congress and its affiliated organisations were declared illegal and also
            Imposed censorship on press and correspondence.
            ● In July 1930, Lord Irwin, Viceroy of India, proposed a RTC and emphasized the goal of
            achieving dominion status for India. He also agreed to the suggestion of allowing Tej Bahadur
            Sapru and M.R Jayakar to explore the possibility of peace between Congress and The
            Government.
            ● In August 1930, Motilal and JL Nehru met Gandhi in Jail and decided to reiterate their
            
            demands -
            ○ Right for India to separate from British Rule
            ○ Established a fully independent National Govt. Rule with control over defense and finance.
            ○ Creation of an Independent Tribunal to settle Britain’s financial claims against India.
            First RTC (12 NOV - 19 JAN 1931)
            First Round Table Conference (November 1930 – January 1931)
            ● Participants: Indian minorities, princely states, and British officials. The Congress and prominent
            business leaders boycotted it.
            ● Outcome:
            ○ Agreement on the principle of an All-India Federation, including British provinces and
            princely states.
            ○ Autonomy for British Provinces.
            ○ Dr. B.R. Ambedkar demanded separate electorates for depressed classes.
            Gandhi Irwin Pact (5 MARCH 1931)
            ● Appeal by Liberals: Members of the Indian Liberal Party, including Tej Bahadur Sapru, C.Y.
            Chintamani, and Srinivasa Sastri, urged Gandhi to negotiate with the Viceroy.
            ● Release of Congress Leaders: On January 25, 1931, Gandhi and other Congress Working
            Committee (CWC) members were released unconditionally. The CWC authorized Gandhi to hold
            discussions with the Viceroy.
            Agreements by the British Government
            ● Release of Political Prisoners: Immediate release of all political prisoners not convicted of
            violence.
            ● Remission of Fines: Remission of all fines that had not yet been collected.
            ● Return of Land: Return of all confiscated lands that had not been sold to third parties.
            ● Lenient Treatment: Leniency for government servants who had resigned during the movement.
            ● Salt Law Relaxation: Right to make salt in coastal villages for personal consumption (not for
            sale).
            ● Picketing Rights: Right to peaceful and non-aggressive picketing of liquor and foreign goods
            
            shops.
            ● Withdrawal of Emergency Ordinances: Emergency ordinances imposed during the Civil
            Disobedience Movement to be withdrawn
            Demands Rejected by Irwin
            ● Public Inquiry into Police Excesses: Gandhi’s demand for an investigation into police violence
            during the movement was declined.
            ● Commutation of Death Sentences: The demand to commute the death sentences of Bhagat
            Singh and his comrades to life imprisonment was also rejected.
            KARACHI SPECIAL CONGRESS SESSION (MARCH 26 TO 31, 1931)
            ● PRESIDENT - Sardar Vallabhai Patel
            ● Important Points -
            ○ Session approved Gandhi Irwin Pact.
            ○ Adopted 2 important resolutions on -
            ■ Fundamental Rights
            ■ National Economic Programme (Drafted by JL Nehru with the help of MN Roy
            ○ Congress expressed its disapproval of political violence but admired the national
            contribution of martyrs.
            ○ Goal of Poorna Swaraj reiterated.
            Point to remember - Karachi Session is significant because for the First time, clear definition of Swaraj was
            spelled out for the masses.
            Second RTC (September 7 to December 1, 1931)
            Participants:
            ● Congress, represented by Gandhi, joined along with Madan Mohan Malviya, Sarojini Naidu, G.D.
            Birla, and others.
            ● Muslim League, Hindu Mahasabha, and depressed class representatives, including Dr. Ambedkar,
            also attended.
            Key Issues:
            ● Political Challenges - Labour Government replaced by a National Government (Lack of unified
            
            approach)
            ○ Lord Irwin replaced by Lord Willingdon - Broke the channel of communication.
            ● Communal representation dominated discussions. Separate electorates for Muslims, depressed
            classes, and other minorities led to a Minorities’ Pact.
            Outcome:
            ● Ended without agreement; MacDonald’s Communal Award was hinted at as a unilateral
            solution.
            ○ Establishment of two Muslim Majority provinces (NWFP and Sindh)
            ○ Creation of Indian Consultative Committee and three expert committees to address
            finance, franchise and federation.
            ○ Declaration of unilateral British Communal Award if Indians failed to reach a consensus.
            Civil Disobedience Movement - Phase 2
            CDM - Relaunched
            ● Following the Failure of RTC II - CWC made the decision on December 29, 1931 to restart CDM:
            ● British Response -
            ○ Banned Congress Organisations at all Level
            ○ Took away control of properties such as Gandhi’s ashrams
            ○ Media Outlets - heavily restricted.
            ● In a direct response to Gandhi’s leadership - The new Viceroy Willingdon, refused to meet Gandhi and
            arrested him on January 4, 1932.
            Let’s Understand the Public Reaction in this Second Phase of CDM (List your opinion first)
            Third RTC (Nov 17 to Dec 24, 1932)
            ● This time there was negligible Indian Participation.
            GOI Act 1935 (Already Covered Before)
            ● GOI Act 1935 was based on White Paper on the Indian Constitution prepared by the British
            Government after the Third Round Table Conference.
            ○ Falling short of Complete Independence, the White paper was criticised by all the political
            parties of that time.
            
            ● GOI Act of 1935 contained 4 major provisions -
            ○ All India Federation, Provincial Autonomy, dyarchy at the centre and safeguards which
            vested special powers with the central and provincial executive.
            ● It also provided legal safeguards against misbehaviour of Indian Ministers and Legislators.
            Another notable feature was the absence of Preamble.
            GOI Act 1935 - Changes at National Level
            ● Proposal for an All India Federation (Never materialised)
            ○ For the States, accession to Federation was voluntary and Federation could not be formed
            unless two conditions were met -
            ■ States entitled to at least half of the seats (52) allotted to the states in the proposed
            Council of States should agree to join the federation.
            ■ The aggregate population of states in the above category should be at least 50% of
            the total population of the Indian States.
            ● Abolition of Indian Council - which was established under the Act of 1858, on account of much
            agitation in India against its Anti-India policies.
            GOI Act 1935 - Changes in GOI
            ● Dyarchy at the Centre
            ○ Dyarchy now from provinces was introduced in the dyarchy at centre.
            ○ Reserved Departments were under the GG who was the be administered by his
            counsellors appointed by him and responsible only to him.
            ○ Transferred subjects were to be administered by the GG on the advice of popular ministers
            answerable to the legislature.
            ● Bicameral Central Legislature
            ○ GG and the two houses - The Council of States and the Federal Assembly.
            ○ Both Houses had representation from British India as well as Princely States,with Princely
            States weightage being disproportionate as the states were expected to act as checks
            upon the nationalists.
            GOI Act 1935 - Changes in Provisional Govt.
            ● Provincial Autonomy -
            
            ○ All provincial departments would be controlled by elected members.
            ○ For the first time, the Act recognised provinces as separate legal entities, free from
            directions and control of GOI and SOS except for specific purposes.
            ○ Now, the province’s derived their power and authority directly from the British Crown.
            ○ Special Powers of Governor -The Governor could issue ordinances or enact Governor’s
            Acts.
            ○ Abolition of dyarchy in provinces -
            ■ The distinction between Reserved and Transferred subjects was abolished and full
            responsible Government was established subject to certain safeguards.
            ○ Bicameral Legislature
            ■ Introduced in 6 provinces - Bengal, Madras, Bombay, United Provinces, Bihar and
            Assam.
            GOI Act 1935 - Other Provisions
            ● Creation of new provinces - Burma was separated from India.
            ● Franchise - It was lowered, though still remained tied to property qualification.
            ● Extension of Separate Electorates - Now it also includes women.
            ● Other Federal Institutions -
            ○ This act provided for the establishment of the Federal Bank and Federal Court (1935 and
            1937).
            ○ The Act vested the control of Railways in a new authority called Federal Railway Authority,
            free from control of ministers and councillors.
            
            Indian National Movement Post CDM (1934 - 1939)
            Understand Depressed Classes Politics in 1900’s
            Introduction
            ● The early 20th century was a transformative period for the Depressed Classes in India. British
            colonialism introduced new social mobility opportunities, challenging the traditional caste
            hierarchy.
            ● This period saw heightened awareness among the Depressed Classes about their rights,
            intensifying tensions with caste Hindus.
            ● Representation and equality became central issues, with key leaders and policies shaping their
            struggle for political and social justice.
            
            KEY DEVELOPMENTS - The Minorities Pact
            ● Context:
            ○ At the Round Table Conference (RTC), Dr. B.R. Ambedkar initially supported joint
            electorates with reservations.
            ○ However, he shifted to aligning with Muslim, Anglo-Indian, and Christian
            representatives (WHEN HIS DEMAND OF UNIVERSAL ADULT SUFFRAGE WAS TURNED
            DOWN)
            ● Provisions:
            ○ The Minorities Pact proposed separate electorates for the Depressed Classes, with a
            referendum to reassess the arrangement in 20 years.
            ● Reason for Shift:
            ○ Ambedkar’s proposal for universal adult suffrage was rejected by:
            ■ The Muslim League and princely states.
            ■ The British government, which hesitated on universal suffrage.
            ○ Without universal suffrage, Dalit representatives risked being predominantly elected by
            caste Hindus, leaving their political influence unchanged.
            KEY DEVELOPMENTS - Additional Information - The Rajah-Moonje Pact (1932) (Just Have an idea, No
            Need to memorize)
            ● Collaboration:
            ○ The Hindu Mahasabha, concerned about Hindu unity, collaborated with M.C. Rajah in
            1932.
            ● Provisions:
            ○ Reserved seats for Dalits within joint electorates to foster unity while maintaining Hindu
            consolidation.
            ● Impact:
            ○ Rajah’s shift from advocating for separate electorates to joint electorates divided Dalit
            opinion.
            ○ Highlighted the evolving political landscape and the lack of consensus among Dalit
            leaders.
            
            ● The Communal Award was announced by British Prime Minister Ramsay MacDonald on August
            16, 1932, based on the recommendations of the Lothian Committee (Indian Franchise
            Committee), formed after the Second Round Table Conference.
            ● It aimed to ensure representation for various minorities and marginalized groups, including
            Muslims, Sikhs, Christians, Anglo-Indians, Europeans, and Depressed Classes
            Recommendations of the Lothian Committee: (Only to be Understood)
            ● Expansion of Electorates:
            ○ Significant increase in the electorate for provincial and federal assemblies.
            ● Reservation for Women:
            ○ 2-5% of seats reserved for women.
            ● Representation for Depressed Classes:
            ○ Specific provisions for Depressed Classes to be treated as a separate minority.
            
            ● Decision on representation for princely states was left to the states themselves.
            Key Provisions of the Communal Award:
            ● Separate Electorates: Allocated for Muslims, Sikhs, Christians, Anglo-Indians, Europeans, and
            Depressed Classes.
            ● Reserved Seats for Depressed Classes:
            ○ 71 seats in provincial legislatures, later raised to 148 under Gandhi.
            ○ 18% of total seats in the central legislature.
            ● Weightage to Muslims:
            ○ Provided weightage in minority provinces.
            ● Doubling of Existing Seats:
            ○ Seats in provincial legislatures were doubled for better representation.
            ● Women’s Reservation:
            ○ 3% reserved for women in most provinces.
            ● Special Representation for Marathas:
            ○ Seven seats are reserved in Bombay.
            ● Educational Grant:
            ○ Allotments to enhance educational facilities for Depressed Classes.
            Responses to the Communal Award:
            ● Congress’s Position:
            ○ Opposed the idea of separate electorates but refrained from altering the Award without
            the consent of minorities.
            ○ Adopted a stance of non-acceptance and non-rejection.
            ● Gandhi’s Reaction:
            ○ Viewed the Award as a threat to Indian unity.
            ○ Opposed the segregation of Depressed Classes as a separate political entity.
            ○ Launched an indefinite fast to demand the adoption of joint electorates with reserved
            seats.
            ● Agreement signed between Mahatma Gandhi and Dr. B.R. Ambedkar at Yerwada Jail, Poona, to
            resolve the dispute over separate electorates for Depressed Classes.
            
            ● Key Provisions:
            ○ Reserved Seats:
            ■ Raised from 71 to 148 in provincial legislatures.
            ■ 18% reserved in the central legislature.
            ○ Joint Electorates: Depressed Classes would vote in joint electorates but with reserved
            seats.
            ○ Primary Election System: An electoral college of Depressed Class candidates to finalize
            nominations.
            ○ Educational and Disability Provisions: Educational grants and no disabilities for
            Depressed Classes.
            ○ Mutual Agreement: Reservation system subject to future mutual agreement.
            ● Marked an amendment to the Communal Award, approved by the British Government.
            Post-CDM and the Transition to Constitutional Politics
            End of Civil Disobedience Movement (CDM):
            ● April 1934: Gandhiji withdrew the Civil Disobedience Movement, marking the transition from
            mass struggles to a new phase of non-mass political activity.
            ● Discussions began on the course of action during this non-mass struggle period, leading to
            three distinct perspectives:
            ○ Gandhian Constructive Work: Focus on Harijan upliftment and rural reconstruction.
            ○ Constitutional Struggle: Advocacy by leaders like M.A. Ansari and Asaf Ali to participate in
            elections and fight within legislative frameworks.
            ○ Leftist Perspective: Advocated continuing non-constitutional mass movements. Nehru
            criticized the Gandhian approach of "Struggle-Truce-Struggle," proposing instead
            "Struggle-Victory."
            Understanding these Factions (Neo Swarajists)
            ● Council Entry by Swarajists - Congress Leaders advocated for participation in the Central
            Legislative Assembly, which was to be held in 1934.
            ○ Earlier in 1933, S. Satyamurthy had founded the Madras Swaraj Party - to support Council
            
            Entry.
            ○ C. Rajagopalachari advocated council entry to broaden Congress political base and
            prepare the ground for the next mass struggle.
            ○ Their intention this time was to ensure cooperation in Legislative councils and they were
            prepared to assume office and establish ministries.
            ● But these are Neo Swarajists - What makes them NEO - Connect the dots…
            Understanding these Factions (Socialists)
            ● Class Struggle by Socialists - Within the Congress this trend was championed by figures like JL
            Nehru, Subash Chandra Bose and JP Narayan with their actions focused at -
            ○ Mobilizing the Working Class and peasants to demand a more equitable and just society.
            ○ Nationalising Key Industries and resources and undertaking Land Reforms.
            ○ Establishing a Planned Economy
            ● They Believed that any alternative approach, such as council entry, would divert attention from
            the anti-Imperialist struggle, thus Nehru strongly opposed participating in Council Elections.
            Gandhi Resigns from Congress (1934)
            ● By 1934, Gandhi felt that he was out of sync with significant currents within the congress -
            ○ Many favoured Parliamentary Politics - Opposition by Gandhi
            ○ Over Emphasis on Spinning and Harijan Work - Many Workers felt alienated
            ○ Jawaharlal Nehru’s Socialist Group was growing in influence.
            ● Gandhi acknowledged all of this, and resigned from Congress in October 1934, aiming to serve
            better in thought, word and deed.
            Shift to Constitutional Politics:
            ● May 1934 (AICC at Patna): Congress set up a Parliamentary Board to contest forthcoming
            elections.
            ● November 1934 (Central Legislative Assembly Elections):
            ○ Congress won 45 out of 75 seats reserved for Indians (Became Largest Party) and thus
            marking a shift from confrontation to constitutional politics.
            
            Congress Nationalist Party
            ● To protest against the Communal Award of 1932, M.M Malviya and Madhav Shrihari Aney split away
            from the Congress and formed the Congress Nationalist Party. After the 1934 elections, Congress and
            Nationalist Party formed the majority in the CLA.
            
            Lucknow Session (April 1936)
            ● President - JL Nehru
            ● Key Outcomes -
            ○ Socialist Reflections-
            ■ CWC has three socialists - JP Narayan, Acharya Narayan Dev and Achyut
            Patwardhan
            ■ Emphasized Socialism as a crucial situation for both global and Indian Issues - Thus
            paying greater emphasis on Peasant and Labour Issues.
            ○ Congress resolved to contest elections on the basis of the election manifesto.
            ○ Congress advocated for equal rights for residents of princely states, similar to the rest of
            India.
            However, the Questions of Office acceptance was kept pending
            Faizpur Congress Session (December 1936) (First Rural Session)
            ● President - JL Nehru
            ● Key Outcomes -
            ○ Nehru condemned Fascism
            ○ Session called for the Constituent Assembly to create India’s own Constitution.
            ○ Adoption of Agrarian Programme -
            ■ Reduction of Land Tax and rent by 50%.
            ■ Recognition of peasant Unions (Kisan Sabhas)
            ■ Complete abolition of Taxes paid to landlords
            Shift to Constitutional Politics:
            ● 1937 Provincial Elections:
            ○ Held in eleven provinces; Congress secured a majority (7) and was able to form ministries
            except in Bengal, Punjab and Sindh.
            ○ Congress performed poorly in the Upper House elections due to limited franchise favoring
            landlords and moneylenders.
            
            Please Note -
            ● Provincial elections were held as per GOI Act 1935 whereas CLA elections of 1934 were held as per
            GOI Act 1919.
            Question of Office Acceptance (1937):
            ● March 1937 (AICC Meeting): Presided by Rajendra Prasad - Conditional Acceptance of Office
            ○ Condition - Governors would not use their special powers as given in GOI Act 1935 to
            intervene in the functioning of the Ministries.
            ○ In June 1937, Viceroy announced that Governors would not interfere in Govt. Affairs,
            making clear their position on special powers.
            ○ Consequently, CWC was convened in Wardha (July 1937) - Authorized the acceptance of
            office roles.
            ● Congress Parliamentary Sub Committee was also set up under Sardar Patel, Maulana Abul
            Kalam Azad and Rajendra Prasad to prevent the British from dividing Congress.
            ● Thus, Ministries were formed, initiating 28 months of Congress rule, which focused on public
            welfare and administrative reforms.
            ● In Harijan, Gandhi commented - “These offices have to be held lightly and not tightly. They are
            crowns of thorns"
            
            Please Note
            ● Leftists opposed office acceptance as cooperation with imperialism. Right-wing leaders like
            C. Rajagopalachari supported it, arguing it would enable reforms for peasants and workers.
            Key Developments During Congress Rule (1937-1939)
            ● Administrative Reforms:
            ○ Repealed emergency laws (Public Safety Act) and lifted press restrictions.
            ○ Released political prisoners and removed ban on groups (Hindustan Seva Dal) (Ban of
            Communist Party stayed)
            ● Social Reforms:
            ○ Promoted Harijan welfare (e.g., temple entry, scholarships).
            ○ Expanded educational facilities and public health initiatives.
            ● Economic Reforms:
            ○ Encouraged khadi production through subsidies.
            ○ Imposed prohibition in certain areas.
            ● Labour Reforms:
            ○ Addressed trade union protests with mediation. (Industrial Disputes Bill 1838)
            ● Agrarian Focus:
            ○ Congress adopted a radical agrarian program at the Faizpur Congress Session (1936).
            Resignation of Congress Ministries due to India being made a part of WWII without consultation
            (October 1939)
            
            INDIAN NATIONAL MOVEMENT (1937 - 1947)
            Congress Leadership and Ideological Divides
            Subhas Chandra Bose's Leadership
            ● Early Actions -
            ○ 1921, Participated in NCM and became a disciple of C.R Das, became the editor of
            ‘Forward’, Newspaper of Swaraj party.
            ○ In 1930, Founded the Congress Democratic Party, and assumed the presidency of AITUC.
            ○ In 1933, Exiled after CDM to Europe - Returned in 1936.
            ● Haripura Congress (1938):
            ○ Bose was elected president; believed Congress ministries had immense revolutionary
            potential.
            ○ Advocated economic planning and initiated a National Planning Committee.
            ○ Session also had resolutions like -
            ■ Establishment of a Socialist State in India
            ■ Abolition of Landlordism
            ■ Protection of Workers Right and welfare of Peasants and Farmers.
            ○ At the end of session, Congress also decided to send a medical mission to work with
            Chinese armed forces, who were facing Japanese aggression.
            Basic Information - Congress leaders were unable to collaborate with a president who publicly
            questioned their nationalist credentials, Bose criticized prominent leaders in his book - “The Indian
            Struggle” where he labelled them as ‘Rightist’ and accused them of compromising with the British by
            accepting the federal scheme outlined in 1935 Act.
            ● Presidential Re-Election - Tripuri Congress Session (1939):
            ○ Despite Gandhi’s opposition, Bose defeated Pattabhi Sitaramayya.
            ○ Gandhi described Pattabhi defeat as his own defeat.
            ● Ideological Differences:
            ○ Bose favored immediate struggle for independence, proposing a six-month ultimatum to
            Britain.
            
            ○ Gandhi opposed this, believing neither the Congress nor the masses were ready.
            ● Resignation and Forward Bloc Formation:
            ○ Bose resigned in April 1939 due to lack of support - Rajendra Prasad new president.
            ○ Formed the Forward Bloc in May 1939 to mobilize radical elements within the Congress,
            but faced disciplinary action for calling an All India Protest against the AICC resolution.
            ■ Thus removed from his post as President of Bengal Provincial Congress Committee.
            ■ Banned holding any elective office in Congress for next 3 Years
            World War II and Congress Response
            Resignation of Congress Ministries (October 1939):
            ● Protesting Britain’s unilateral decision to involve India in the war without consultation.
            ● Gandhi welcomed the resignations, calling them a "bitter pill" necessary to cleanse the Congress.
            Divergent Views Within Congress:
            ● Support for Britain:
            ○ Gandhi and Rajagopalachari sympathized with Britain’s anti-fascist stance but sought
            conditional support.
            ● Opposition to British Policies:
            ○ Nehru opposed participation until India gained freedom.
            ○ Socialists and Subhas Bose viewed the war as imperialist and demanded immediate
            resistance.
            ● CWC resolution, 1939 -
            ○ Argued India shouldn’t Fight a war if it wasn’t free itself.
            ○ Ask for an end to British Rule in colonies and full democracy in India.
            ○ Wanted the Govt. to state its goals for the war and how it would bring democracy to India.
            ○ Offered support for 2 basic conditions -
            ■ After the War, A constituent Assembly to determine the political structure of free India
            ■ Immediately, some form of responsible Govt should be established at the center.
            ● Muslim League Response -
            ○ Agreed to support Britain in exchange of protecting Muslim rights in India.
            ○ Jinnah demanded that Muslim League should be recognised as the only voice for Indian
            Muslims and wanted the power to reject further changes in the Constitution.
            
            GOVERNMENT RESPONSE
            Viceroy Linlithgow turned down the Congress Demands -
            ● Argued giving significant powers to Indians was not suitable during the War.
            ● Emphasised the differences between Indian parties and attempted to use Muslim League and
            Princes against Congress.
            October 1939, Linlithgow Govt. released a White Paper -
            ● Declined to specify British Goals in War - Says Britain fighting against aggression.
            ● Offering Dominion Status for Indians post War and an increase in the Indian Members in the
            Executive Policy.
            ● Suggested forming a consultative committee with members from major political parties instead of
            quickly setting up a national govt. at the center
            Resignation of Congress Ministries
            October 23, 1939, CWC meeting at Wardha -
            ● Dismissed the Viceroy’s statement as just a repeat of Old colonial policies
            ● Chose not to support the War
            ● Asked the Congress led Provincial Govt to step down. (Non Congress ministries cooperated with
            British)
            Also Note - On Dec 22, 1939, Jinnah marked this day as “Day of Deliverance” to celebrate the resignations
            from the Indian National Congress.
            Debate on Immediate Mass Struggle
            There was a general disagreement on immediate mass struggle because-
            ● Since the cause of Allies was just, INC should not embarrass them during the War.
            ● Worried about Hindu Muslim unity, a protest might turn into communal violence.
            ● Congress itself in Weak State, with disorganisation and lack of discipline, which would make a big
            protest difficult.
            Ramgarh Session (1940, Jharkhand) - President Maulana Abul Kalam Azad (Al-Hilal)
            ● JL Nehru reiterated complete Independence as the precondition for Congress support.
            ● Congress declared - Indian Freedom cannot be in the form of dominion or any other status within
            the Imperial Structure. Sovereignty must rest with the people of India.
            ● Also, CDM would be resorted to as soon as Gandhi seems fit.
            
            August Offer (August 1940)
            Background - After the Fall of Belgium, Holland and France, England became more conciliatory towards
            India, Congress also now willing to compromise asked the British Govt. to allow it to form an interim
            government during the war, but the Govt. was not interested. Instead Viceroy Linlithgow presented its
            own offer -
            ● Dominion Status to India after War (Formation of Constituent Assembly with Indians mainly)
            ● Expansion of Viceroy's executive council - Majority of India drawn from major Political Parties.
            ● Recognition of India’s rights to frame their own constitution after War
            ● No future Constitution to be adopted without the consent of minorities.
            Indian Response - Mixed
            ● Indian Congress rejected
            ● Muslim League - Welcomed the Veto assurance and reiterated Partition to be only solution
            Individual Satyagraha (1940 -1941)
            ● Objectives:
            ○ Show that nationalist patience was not due to weakness.
            ○ Protest against India’s forced involvement in the war.
            ○ Demand freedom of speech through anti-war declarations - “Delhi Chalo Andolan”
            ● Leadership:
            ○ Vinoba Bhave was the first satyagrahi, followed by Nehru.
            ○ 25,000 individuals were arrested by May 1941.
            ● Congress leader's Dilemma -
            ○ Threat of Japan’s aggressive actions - Congress also wants a resolution now.
            ○ CWC passed a resolution offering to cooperate with the Govt, provided that full
            independence was granted after the war and the substance of power was transferred
            immediately.
            ○ Gandhi at this time chose Nehru as his political successor.
            
            Cripps Mission (March 1942)
            Provisions:
            ● Dominion Status:
            ○ India to be granted Dominion Status after World War II.
            ○ A Constituent Assembly to be elected for drafting the constitution. (Entirely Indian)
            ● Provinces:
            ○ Provinces could choose to join the Indian Union or remain outside it.
            ○ Provinces opting out could secede from India.
            ● Defence and Communication:
            ○ These to remain under British control until the war ends.
            ○ Post-war, India could decide to retain the British Army for defence.
            ● Minorities:
            ○ A commission to safeguard minority interests.
            ○ Minorities could secede if they felt their rights were not protected.
            ● Princely States:
            ○ No imposition on princely states to join the Indian Union.
            ○ States unwilling to join could form a separate union with their own constitution
            Reaction to Cripps Mission:
            ● Congress -
            ○ Objected to offer of Dominion Status
            ○ Representation of princely states by nominees, and not elected
            ○ Right of Provinces to secede
            ○ Absence of a plan which talks about Immediate transfer of power - GG supremacy still
            retained.
            ● Muslim League -
            ○ Criticised the idea of a Single Indian Union
            ○ Thought that proposal denied the right of Muslims the right to self determination and
            creation of Pakistan.
            ● Hindu MahaSabha
            ○ Criticised the right to secede
            
            ● Depressed Classes:
            ○ Thought Partition would leave them at the mercy of upper caste Hindus.
            ● Sikhs -
            ○ Objected the Partition would take away Punjab from them.
            Assessment of Cripps Mission:
            ● Rigid - “Take it or leave it” attitude.
            ● Accession Procedure poorly defined.
            Gandhi dismissed the scheme as “Post Dated Cheque”, Nehru criticised it for preserving the existing
            autocratic powers. Following the unsuccessful mission - Nationalists now perceived the right moment for
            starting the next phase of the Civil Disobedience movement.
            Quit India Movement (August 1942)
            August Resolution - ‘Quit India’ “Bharat Chodo Andolan”
            ● July 1942 - CWC meeting Wardha - Resolution giving Gandhi the command to launch mass
            movement - 8 August 1942 Resolution formally ratified.
            Directives given to Various Sections of Society -
            ● Government Servants: - Do not resign but declare allegiance to Congress.
            ● Soldiers: Stay in the Army but refrain from firing on compatriots.
            ● Students: If confident, leave studies to join the movement.
            ● Peasants: If zamindars are anti-government, pay mutually agreed rent & If zamindars are
            pro-government, do not pay rent.
            ● Princes: Support the masses and acknowledge the sovereignty of the people.
            ● People in Princely States: Support rulers only if they are anti-government & Declare allegiance to
            being part of the Indian nation.
            Who All Opposed ? (I hope you can now understand History - Why are they opposing?)
            ● Muslim League
            ● Communist Party of India
            ● Hindu Mahasabha
            
            Course of Movement -
            ● In a single Sweep, (9 August 1942) all the Top Leaders of Congress were arrested and taken to
            unknown locations.
            ● Declared Congress organisations unlawful, Public meeting banned under Defense of India Act,
            1919.
            ● Led to emergence of Local Level Leaders and unregulated mass movements - Leading to violent
            incidents.
            ○ Parallel Governments (September 1942)
            ■ Balliya Sarkar - Chittu Pandey - Ballia (UP) - Low Budget Gandhi Marriages
            ■ Tamluk Sarkar = Satish C. Samanta - Tamluk (Bengal) - Vidyut Vahini (Armed
            Militia)
            ■ Satara Sarkar - Nana Patil = Satara (MH) - Nyayadan Mandals (People’s Court) &
            Toofan Senas (Youth Militia)
            ● Emergence of Underground Activities -
            ○ Disrupting Communications
            ○ Underground Radio Station - Bombay - Usha Mehta (Movie - Ae Watan Mera Watan)
            Govt Response to Moment -
            ● Utilised Large Military Force - initially stationed to confront japanese.
            ● Wartime censorship.
            ● In Feb 1943, Gandhi took fast unto death - Govt, anticipated Gandhi’s death and made
            arrangements for his funeral.
            ● Bengal Famine of 1943 - One of the reasons for decline of movement
            Significance of Movement
