            ● Unprecedented Mass Participation with Grassroot leadership
            ● Use of Violence
            Indian National Army (1942 - 1945)
            Indian Independence League (IIL):
            ● In March 1942, Rash Behari Bose, a revolutionary leader in Japan since 1915, organized a
            meeting in Tokyo with other Indian leaders to form the IIL.
            ● In June 1942, during a meeting in Bangkok, Rash Behari Bose was appointed as the President of
            
            the IIL.
            ● A resolution was passed to establish the Indian National Army (INA), also known as the Azad
            Hind Fauz.
            ● It was decided that Subhas Chandra Bose would lead the movement in the future.
            Formation of the INA:
            ● INA enlisted volunteers from Indians overseas and ex-British Indian soldiers in Southeast Asia.
            ● Captain Mohan Singh was made the Commander-in-Chief.
            ● The INA grew to about 40,000 members, preparing to confront British forces in India.
            ● Disagreements between Captain Mohan Singh and the Japanese led to the transfer of INA
            leadership to Subhas Chandra Bose.
            INA under Subhas Chandra Bose:
            ● In July 1943, Rash Behari Bose handed over the leadership to Subhas Chandra Bose.
            ● Subhas Bose established the Arzi Hukumat-e-Azad Hind (Provisional Government of Free India)
            in Singapore on October 21, 1943.
            ● The Provisional Government declared war on Britain and the USA and was recognized by the Axis
            powers.
            ● In Dec 1943, Japanese handed over Andaman and Nicobar Island to Bose - Renaming Shaheed
            and Swaraj Dweep.
            ● INA HQ’s shifted from Singapore to Rangoon in January 1944 and army prepared to march
            towards Delhi (Dilli Chalo)
            ○ At this time regiment of INA were named after Gandhi, Nehru, Azar, Bose, Rani Jhansi
            (Captain Lakshmi Sehgal)
            ○ INA Army was arrested by Allied Forces in 1945 meanwhile Subash Bose died in an air crash
            on August 18, 1945 in Taipei, Taiwan
            The Story Continues - CR Formula (1944)
            Following Gandhi’s release from Jail on May 5, 1944 - He tried to revive Congress and also initiated
            discussions with Muslim League in July 1944.
            C. R. Formula (1944)
            ● Proposed by: C. Rajagopalachari
            ● Objective: To resolve the deadlock between the Indian National Congress and the Muslim
            
            League over the demand for Pakistan and to facilitate cooperation between the two parties for
            achieving Indian independence.
            ● Main Points -
            ● The Muslim League was to cooperate with the Congress in forming a Provisional Interim
            Government for a transitional period and in return League's demand for Pakistan was
            recognized, but only through a democratic process involving a plebiscite.
            ○ All parties would be allowed to express their views on partition before the
            plebiscite.
            ○ After World War II, a plebiscite would decide whether Muslim-majority areas in the
            North-West and North-East would:
            ■ Form a separate sovereign state (Pakistan), or Remain a part of India.
            ○ If separation occurred, agreements would be entered into for essential services
            such as:
            ■ Defence
            ■ Communication
            ■ Commerce.
            Reactions to C. R. Formula (1944)
            Muslim League's Stand:
            ● Supported the concept of a two-nation theory.
            ● Muhammad Ali Jinnah proposed that:
            ○ Only Muslims in the North-West and North-East should vote in the plebiscite.
            ○ The rest of the Indian population should not be involved in the decision-making process.
            Congress's Stand:
            ● Congress was willing to work with the Muslim League to achieve independence.
            ● However, Congress rejected the formula as:
            ○ It accepted the principle of partition.
            ○ The League's primary goal was the creation of a separate nation, which Congress opposed.
            Other Reactions:
            ● Hindu Leaders:
            ○ Led by Vir Savarkar, Hindu leaders opposed the formula, as they disagreed with
            
            conceding to the demand for Pakistan.
            ● Sikh Leaders:
            ○ Leaders like Master Tara Singh opposed the plan, fearing division of the Sikh community
            in Punjab, leaving Sikhs on both sides of the dividing line.
            Desai Liaquat Pact (1945)
            Context:
            ● The failure of the Gandhi-Jinnah Talks (1944) left a deadlock between the INC and the Muslim
            League.
            ● Secret talks were held between Bhulabhai Desai, a senior Congress leader, and Liaquat Ali Khan,
            a prominent leader of the Muslim League, in January 1945.
            Objective:
            ● To negotiate an agreement for a future coalition government representing both Hindus and
            Muslims equally in the independent government of India.
            Key Points of the Pact:
            ● Recognition of the Muslim League: - Desai acknowledged the Muslim League as the
            representative of Muslims.
            ● Formation of an Interim Government: An interim government would be formed with equal
            representation of the Congress and the League.
            ● Liaquat's Concessions: Liaquat Ali Khan gave up the demand for a separate Muslim state
            (Pakistan).
            ● Seats for Minorities: The pact proposed 20% reserved seats for minorities in the government.
            Outcome
            ● The Desai-Liaquat Pact was never formally endorsed by either the Congress or the Muslim
            League.
            Special Mention - Sapru Committee Report (1945)
            Wavell Plan (1945)
            Background
            ● By the end of 1944, with the war turning in favor of the Allies, the British realized that:
            ○ The Indian situation, post-Quit India Movement, could not be ignored and prolonged use
            
            of force to subdue India indefinitely was impractical
            ● Urgency of Finding a Solution - The upcoming 1945 General Elections in England required the
            Conservatives to urgently address Indian issues
            ● The plan was designed to prevent Congress leaders from exploiting the post-war climate of
            unemployment and economic challenges.
            Key Features of the Wavell Plan
            ● Restructured Executive Council: - The Executive Council was to be restructured with all
            members, except the Governor-General and Commander-in-Chief, being Indian representatives.
            ● Interim Government: - The interim government would function within the framework of the
            1935 Government of India Act and would remain answerable to the Central Assembly.
            ● Joint List of Nominees:
            ○ Political parties were to collaborate in creating a joint list of nominees for the Executive
            Council.
            ○ If consensus was not achieved, separate lists could be submitted.
            ● Equal Representation - Equal representation was to be provided to caste Hindus and Muslims in
            the Executive Council.
            ● Governor-General's Powers -The Governor-General would exercise veto power, but only on
            the advice of his ministers.
            ● Future Constitution - Provisions were made to explore negotiations for a new constitution after
            the war.
            Simla Conference (June 1945)
            To implement the Wavell Plan, Lord Wavell organized a conference in Simla:
            ● Congress was represented by Maulana Azad, while Jinnah represented the Muslim League.
            ● Mahatma Gandhi also advised the Viceroy during the talks.
            Reactions to the Wavell Plan
            Muslim League's Response:
            ● Feared loss of representation if other minority groups (like Sikhs, Christians, and Depressed
            Classes) aligned with the Congress.
            ● Demanded that all Muslim members in the Executive Council be nominated by the League.
            
            Congress's Response:
            ● Perceived the plan as an attempt to limit Congress’s influence, seeing it as a measure to make
            Congress solely a caste Hindu party.
            ● Asserted its right to nominate representatives from all communities.
            
            INDIAN NATIONAL MOVEMENT (1945 - 1947)
            Political Negotiations and Developments
            The Release of Congress Leaders (Post-1944)
            ● Post-World War II, the British sought to establish a coalition government in India. (Wavell Plan
            1945)
            ● Congress leaders were released from prison in May 1944 on grounds of ill health.
            ● Public enthusiasm for these leaders signaled renewed resolve to fight for independence.
            Elections in Britain (1945)
            ● The Labour Party, sympathetic to India, won the July 1945 elections.
            ● Clement Attlee became Prime Minister, replacing Winston Churchill.
            ● The Labour Party manifesto highlighted promoting India's self-government within the
            Commonwealth.
            ● In August 1945, elections for India’s central and provincial legislatures were announced.Viceroy
            Wavell indicated in September that following the election, Government would intend to -
            ○ Hold Negotiations for establishing a Constitution making body.
            ○ Form an Executive Council with support from the major political parties.
            Election in India (1945 & 1946)
            ● Muslim League Manifesto:
            ○ Asserted Muslims of India are One Nation.
            ○ Emphasized Pakistan as the solution to India's constitutional problem.
            ● Congress Manifesto:
            ○ Advocated for Indian unity and opposed partition.
            ○ Raised public issues like Police excessed during QIM and INA Trails.
            Election Results (1946): The Elections to Central Legislative Assembly were held in December 1945
            and the provincial elections were held in January 1946.
            ● Congress dominated the elections, winning 91.3% of non-Muslim votes and 57/102 seats in the
            Central Legislative Assembly.
            ● Muslim League secured 86.6% of Muslim votes, winning 30/30 Muslim seats in the Assembly and
            402/509 Muslim seats in provinces.
            
            ● Congress formed ministries in 8 provinces (Majority everywhere except Sindh, Punjab and
            Bengal), while the Muslim League led in Sindh and Bengal.
            ○ In Punjab, Congress, Union Party and Akalis formed a coalition Government.
            Popular Uprising - INA Trials (1945-46)
            Importance and Impact of INA Trials
            ● Widespread Political Support:
            ○ All major political parties, including Congress, Akalis, Communist Party of India, Hindu
            Mahasabha, Justice Party, Muslim League, RSS, and Unionist Party.
            ● Broadened Appeal:
            ○ Attracted new social groups like government officials, armed forces members, and
            loyalists who were previously outside the nationalist movement.
            ● Geographic Spread of Support:
            ○ Support extended beyond major cities like Delhi, Bombay, Calcutta, and Madras to
            remote regions such as Assam, Balochistan, and Coorg.
            ● Public Symbolism and Outrage:
            ○ Held publicly at the Red Fort, the trials symbolized British oppression and sparked mass
            public outrage.
            ● Prominent Figures:
            ○ Key INA leaders, Shah Nawaz Khan, Prem Sehgal, and Gurbaksh Dhillon, were charged
            with treason.
            INA Defence Committee and Trial Outcome
            Formation of INA Defence Committee (1945):
            ● The INC established the INA Defence Committee, including prominent lawyers like:
            ○ Jawaharlal Nehru, Bhulabhai Desai, Asaf Ali, and Tej Bahadur Sapru.
            Outcome of the Trial:
            ● INA members were convicted of conducting war but were not executed.
            ● They were terminated from service with no long-term punishment.
            
            Popular Uprising - RIN Mutiny
            ● Growth of the Royal Indian Navy:
            ○ The Royal Indian Navy expanded rapidly during World War II, becoming 10 times larger
            between 1939 and 1945.
            ● Surge in Nationalism:
            ○ A growing sense of nationalism among Indian sailors played a crucial role in shaping the
            mutiny.
            ● Contributing Factors:
            ○ Disparities in pay and working conditions.
            ○ Racial discrimination and unequal treatment by British officers.
            ○ Poor living conditions and inadequate food supplies.
            ○ Resentment over being used to suppress freedom movements in other colonies.
            ○ Influence of the INA trials and broader anti-colonial sentiment.
            Mutiny Events:
            ● Started on December 1, 1945 at HMIS Talwar in Bombay. (Training Facility for Royal Indian Navy)
            ● Formation of Naval Central Strike Committee formed with Lt. M.S. Khan (President) and officer
            Madan Singh (Vice President). This committee had representatives from all Bombay ships and
            barracks and renamed RIN to Indian National Navy.
            ● The Committee presented demands like -
            ○ Release of all political prisoners including that of INA Trials.
            ○ Equal Pay and Better food quality.
            ○ End of British Rule in India.
            ● Expanded to include 20, 000 ratings and several naval stations across India.
            ● Ratings raised Congress, Muslim League, and Communist Party flags, symbolizing unity.
            Political Response:
            ● Clement Attlee called for a forceful resolution .
            ● Congress and Muslim League opposed the mutiny, fearing violence, Gandhi too opposed the
            unplanned revolt.
            ● Communist Party of India fully supported the naval ratings and mobilised workers in Bombay by
            shutting down textile mills.
            
            End of Mutiny & Significance
            ● Mutiny was eventually quelled after negotiations, Principal demands were met and under
            pressure from Congress Naval Central Strike Committee was forced to surrender.
            ○ Following the RIN Mutiny, all participating Navy personnels were dismissed and many
            faced court martials. However in 1972, GOI recognised their contribution and granted
            them freedom fighter’s pensions.
            ● The timing of Cabinet Missions announcement, just a day after the Mutiny starts underscores its
            influence on British POlicies towards India.
            Cabinet Mission (1946)
            The Labour Government, led by Clement Attlee, sent the Cabinet Mission to India to explore solutions
            to Indian problems and resolve constitutional deadlock - The mission comprised three British officials:
            ● Lord Pethick-Lawrence (Secretary of State for India).
            ● Sir Stafford Cripps (President of the Board of Trade and a supporter of Indian
            self-determination).
            ● A.V. Alexander (First Lord of the Admiralty).
            Objectives of the Cabinet Mission
            ● Resolve Constitutional Issues:
            ○ Design a framework for India’s constitution to address competing demands of Congress
            and the Muslim League.
            ● Avoid Partition:
            ○ Prevent the division of India into separate sovereign states (India and Pakistan).
            ● Negotiate Interim Government:
            ○ Facilitate the formation of an interim government to oversee the transition.
            After initial dialogue, Mission proposed its plan for composition of new Government on 16 May 1946.
            Mission came with short term (Formation of Interim Government) and long term proposals (Federal
            and Constituent Assembly proposals) to ensure a peaceful transition while addressing the complex
            political and communal issues in India -
            ● Interim Government:
            ○ An Interim Government was to be established with representatives from major political
            
            parties to manage the transition.
            ● Rejection of a Separate Pakistan:
            ○ Geographical impracticality of dividing provinces like Bengal and Punjab.
            ○ Risk of economic and administrative challenges in divided territories.
            ● Constituent Assembly :
            ○ It was to be elected by provincial assemblies by proportional representation
            ○ Recommended the formation of a 389-member Constituent Assembly:
            ■ 292 members from British provinces.
            ■ 93 members from princely states.
            ■ 4 members from chief commissioners' provinces.
            Federal Structure:
            ● Proposed a three-tier government system:
            ○ Union Government: Control over foreign affairs, defense, and communications.
            ■ Communal Questions in the Central Legislature were to be decided by simple
            majority in both communities present and voting.
            ○ Provincial Governments: Autonomy in other areas and also Residual Powers.
            ○ Groups of Provinces:
            ■ Section A - Hindu Majority Provinces (Madras, bombay, United Provinces, Bihar and
            Orissa)
            ■ Section B - Punjab, NWFP, Singh (Muslim majority provinces of North West)
            ■ Section C - Bengal and Assam (Muslim dominated provinces of North East)
            ● After the first General Elections, a province was to be free to come out of
            a group and also eligible for reconsideration.
            ○ Princely States were no longer to be under the paramountcy of the British Govt. They
            would be free to enter into an agreement with successor governments of the British Govt.
            Reaction to the Cabinet Mission
            ● Congress’s Response:
            ○ Accepted the idea of a Constituent Assembly and went ahead to form the Constituent
            Assembly (15 June)
            ○ Rejected the groupings of provinces, (10 July) as it contradicted their vision of a unified
            
            India. (Initially, thought that grouping was optional for provinces and free to join or not)
            ● Muslim League’s Response:
            ○ Muslim League took the grouping of provinces to be compulsory and thus leading
            realization of Pakistan indirectly.
            ○ Initially accepted (June 6) the plan but later withdrew, accusing Congress of undermining
            the grouping principle (29 July) and the right of Muslim League only to nominate Muslim
            candidates (Zakir Hussain form Congress)
            ○ Called for Direct Action Day (August 16, 1946) to press for the creation of Pakistan after
            Congress was invited by Wavell to form Interim Govt at center sidelining Muslim League.
            ● Princely States’ Response:
            ○ The princely states were reluctant to join the proposed federal structure, fearing loss of
            autonomy.
            Formation of Interim Government with Congress Alone
            ● Invitation to Congress:
            ○ On 12 August 1946, the Viceroy invited Jawaharlal Nehru to form the Interim
            Government as Prime Minister.
            ○ A 12-member cabinet was formed, representing diverse sections of society:
            ■ 5 Hindus
            ■ 3 Muslims
            ■ 1 each from Scheduled Castes, Sikhs, Christians, and Parsis.
            ● Provincial Governments:
            ○ At the provincial level, Congress-led governments were formed in most provinces,
            including:
            ■ North-West Frontier Province (NWFP).
            ■ Punjab: Coalition with Akali Dal and Unionist Muslim League.
            ■ Bengal and Sindh: Muslim League formed governments.
            ● Constituent Assembly’s Role:
            ○ The Constituent Assembly was instructed to commence work on drafting a new
            constitution for India.
            
            ● Muslim League's Opposition
            ○ Muhammad Ali Jinnah condemned the Interim Government and pledged to continue the
            fight for Pakistan.
            ○ This led to widespread unrest and violence in Punjab, Delhi, Bombay, and other regions.
            ● Wavell's Appeal:
            ○ In light of communal tensions, Viceroy Wavell urged Nehru to include the Muslim League
            in the Interim Government, hoping it would restore peace.
            ○ Despite this, the Muslim League did not abandon its demand for Pakistan and direct
            action to achieve it.
            ● League’s Inclusion:
            ○ On 26 October 1946, the Muslim League joined the Interim Government, with Liaqat Ali
            Khan appointed as Finance Minister.The League aimed to disrupt the functioning of the
            government, using its position to block key decisions.
            ○ These tactics convinced Congress leaders of the futility of the Interim Government.
            ● Breakdown of the Interim Government
            ○ Mountbatten’s Arrival: With the arrival of the new Viceroy, Lord Mountbatten, in early
            1947, Congress leaders expressed that the coalition was unworkable.
            ○ Acceptance of Partition: The failure of the Interim Government, coupled with escalating
            communal tensions, led to the acceptance of the idea of Partition, paving the way for the
            division of India.
            Constituent Assembly: Formation and Election
            The Cabinet Mission aimed to make immediate arrangements for Indians to decide the future
            constitution of India, resolving political conflicts.The assembly was constituted under the Government
            of India Act, 1935, using restricted adult franchise.
            Composition and Electoral College
            ● Composition of Assembly -
            ○ The Constituent Assembly had 389 seats:
            ■ 296 seats for British India provinces (e.g., Bengal, Bihar, Odisha, etc.).
            ■ 93 seats for princely states.
            
            ● Division of Seats -
            ○ Seats were distributed based on population: (Roughly one seat per million people)
            ○ Seats in British provinces were also divided among: Muslims, Sikhs, and General
            communities based on their population.
            ● Election Process:
            ○ Representatives of each community were elected by members of that community in the
            provincial legislative assembly.
            ○ Voting followed the method of proportional representation.
            ● Princely States:
            ○ Representatives were nominated by the heads of princely states.
            ● First Session (9 December 1946):
            ○ Only 207 of the 296 members attended the inaugural session.
            ○ Members from the Muslim League boycotted the session due to disagreements over
            partition.
            ○ J.B. Kripalani invited Dr. Sachchidanand Sinha to act as the temporary chairman due to
            his seniority.
            ● Election of Permanent Chairman (10 December 1946):
            ○ Dr. Rajendra Prasad was elected as the permanent chairman of the Constituent
            Assembly.
            ● Objective Resolution (13 December 1946):
            ○ Jawaharlal Nehru proposed the Objective Resolution, which later formed the basis of
            the Preamble to the Indian Constitution.
            ● Dual Role:
            ○ Acted as the Constituent Assembly, framing the Constitution.
            ○ Functioned as the Legislative Assembly, governing and making laws.
            ● Timeframe:
            ○ The Assembly held 11 sessions over 2 years and 11 months for a total of 166 days.
            ○ It completed the Constitution of India in 1950.
            
            Partition and Freedom
            Muslim League's Direct Action Day and Aftermath
            ● Direct Action Day (16 August 1946):
            ○ Declared by the Muslim League to demand Pakistan.
            ○ Resulted in communal riots, creating a crisis-like situation across India.
            ● League's Position:
            ○ Despite Viceroy Wavell's efforts, the League:
            ■ Refused to cooperate in the Interim Government.
            ■ Declined to participate in the Constituent Assembly.
            ● Escalation:
            ○ On 5 February 1947, Congress requested the resignation of League members from the
            Interim Government.
            ○ Prime Minister Clement Attlee acknowledged the situation in the British Parliament on 20
            February 1947, marking an irreversible breakdown of the Interim Government.
            Attlee's Declaration (20 February 1947)
            ● Key Points:
            ○ British Withdrawal Date: Scheduled for 30 June 1948, regardless of whether a
            constitution was finalized.
            ○ If the Constituent Assembly lacked full representation by June 1948, power would be
            transferred to multiple central governments.(If Provinces with Muslim majority did not
            join)
            ○ British powers over princely states would cease but would not transfer to any subsequent
            Indian government.
            ○ Lord Louis Mountbatten was appointed to replace Wavell as Viceroy in March 1947.
            ● Reactions:
            ○ Congress: Accepted the idea of transferring power to multiple central governments.
            ○ Muslim League: Jinnah became resolute in his demand for Pakistan and launched:
            ■ Efforts to overthrow non-League governments in Punjab, Assam, and NWFP using
            violence.
            ■ A civil disobedience movement in Punjab, causing the collapse of Khizr Hayat
            
            Khan’s coalition ministry.
            Mountbatten Plan (June 1947)
            Initial Proposals:
            ● Suggested dividing provinces into independent successor states under the Balkan/Dickie Bird
            Plan.
            ● This plan allowed provinces like Punjab and Bengal to choose independence, Pakistan, or India
            through votes.
            ● Jawaharlal Nehru strongly opposed the Balkan Plan, leading to its abandonment.
            Key Features of Mountbatten Plan -
            ● Partition: India would be divided into two dominions—India and Pakistan with Dominion Status
            and their respective Constituent Assemblies.
            ● Partition of Punjab and Bengal - Members of Legislative Assemblies of Punjab and Bengal
            should meet separately to vote for partition, if both sections of each of these assemblies voted for
            partition, then that province would be partitioned.
            ● Referendum in Sylhet, NWFP and Voting in Sindh Legislative Assembly to decide their fate.
            ● Boundary Commission under Sir Cyril Radcliffe for demarcating the boundaries of new parts of
            Punjab and Bengal.
            ● Princely States - British Suzerainty over Princely states will end, they were theoretically
            independent as per plan. However it was made clear in the long run that had the option to either
            choose India or Pakistan.
            ● Transfer of Power - British would leave India on 15 August,1947.
            Indian Reaction and Result -
            ● Nehru, Jinnah and Sardar Baldev Singh representing Sikhs, endorsed the plan on June 4, 1947.
            ● On June 20, Bengal Assembly and on June 23, Punjab assembly both voted in favour of partition.
            ● As per referendum, NWFP and Sylhet went to Pakistan.
            ● On June 26, 1947, at a special session SIndh Assembly also decided to join Pakistan.
            ● The Boundary Commission award was ready by 12 August, 1947 - Ready to divide India.
            ● Thus, Indian Independence Act 1947 was passed by the British Parliament. (4 July 1947)
            Indian Independence Act 1947
            Declaration of Independence
            
            ● India and Pakistan were declared independent and sovereign dominions from August 15, 1947,
            with the partition dividing British India into two sovereign states. The title of the King of England
            was modified to exclude “Emperor of India.”.
            Status of Princely States
            ● British paramountcy over princely states and tribal territories lapsed, granting princely states the
            autonomy to:
            ○ Join India or Pakistan.
            ○ Opt for independence.
            Governance Changes
            ● The Viceroy’s office was abolished, and each dominion was to have a Governor-General
            appointed by the British Crown on the recommendation of the dominion's government. The
            Governors-General and provincial governors acted as nominal heads, following ministerial
            advice.
            ● The Secretary of State for India’s office was abolished, transferring duties to the Secretary of
            State for Commonwealth Affairs, and entitlements for civil servants appointed before August 15,
            1947, were continued.
            Legislative Functions
            ● Constituent Assemblies of both dominions served as legislative bodies with powers to:
            
            ○ Dissolve the Central Legislative Assembly and Council of States.
            ○ Annul any Act of the British Parliament, including the Independence Act itself.
            ● The Government of India Act, 1935, served as interim constitutional law until new constitutions
            were adopted.
            British Involvement
            ● His Majesty’s Government in Britain bore no responsibility for the governance of India or
            Pakistan after August 15, 1947. British Parliamentary Acts could not extend to the dominions
            without approval by the dominion's legislature.

            British Expansion in India
            ● Direct Conquest - Bengal, Mysore, Marathas, Sindh and Punjab
            ● Ring Fence Policy - Doctrine implemented by Warren Hastings
            ○ To protect their neighbouring frontier in order to safeguard their own empire.
            ● Subsidiary Alliance - System perfect by Lord Wellesley
            ○ Under this system, Indian rulers who entered into treaties with the company received
            protection. (I hope you understand this concept in detail)
            ● Doctrine of Lapse - Lord Dalhousie -
            ○ Under this, any princely state in India under subsidiary alliance or suzerainty of the British
            would lose its princely status and be absorbed into British India if ruler was deemed unfit
            or passed away with a male heir.
            British Administrative System
            By the late 18th century, the East India Company transitioned from a trading entity into a political
            authority in India. To sustain their rule, the British implemented a robust administrative system with a
            foundation in three primary pillars:
            ● Judicial System
            ● Civil Services
            
            ● Army
            The British administration aimed to:
            ● Implement British policies efficiently.
            ● Secure revenue collection and maintain law and order.
            ● Profoundly alter the Indian socio-economic landscape.
            The administrative framework was primarily intended to:
            ● Establish British supremacy.
            ● Exploit Indian resources for imperial ambitions.
            British India - Judicial System
            Judicial System in Pre-Colonial India: The system lacked uniformity and proper organization where
            Hindu Litigation was Resolved by caste elders, village panchayats, or zamindars while Muslim
            Litigation was Presided over by Qazis with religious expertise.
            Judicial Reforms Under Warren Hastings (1772–1785): (Only Understanding)
            ● Goals:
            ○ Efficient dispute resolution (tenants, farmers, government officers).
            ○ Address complex inheritance issues. (A code of Gentoo Laws or Ordination of Pundits in
            1776)
            ● Reforms:
            ○ Creation of Mofussil Diwani Adalat: For civil cases like property disputes or contracts.
            ○ Establishment of Faujdari Adalat: For criminal cases involving theft or assault.
            ○ Higher appellate courts established in Calcutta:
            ■ Sadar Diwani Adalat (civil appeals).
            ■ Sadar Nizamat Adalat (criminal appeals).
            ● Role of Islamic Authorities:
            ○ Qazis and Muftis presided over criminal courts under the supervision of the
            
            Governor-General.
            ● Regulating Act of 1773:
            ○ Created the Supreme Court in Calcutta:
            ■ Jurisdiction over original and appellate cases.
            Judicial Reforms Under Lord Cornwallis (1786–1793):
            ● Judicial Plan of 1787:
            ○ Merged tax and judicial systems.
            ○ Revenue and civil disputes presided over by the Collector:
            ■ Maal Adalats for revenue cases.
            ■ District Diwani Adalats for civil disputes.
            ● Judicial Plan of 1790:
            ○ Criminal justice administration transferred to Company officers from Muslim Law Officers.
            ○ District Faujdari Courts abolished and replaced by Circuit Courts.(Travelling Courts)
            ○ Sadar Nizamat Adalat retained for criminal appeals.
            ● Judicial Plan of 1793:
            ○ Clear separation of revenue and judicial administration:
            ■ Maal Adalats abolished; revenue issues handled by Collectors.
            ■ District Judges presided over District Diwani Adalats.

            British India - Civil Services
            The Indian Civil Services (ICS) were established by the British to ensure law and order, facilitate
            governance, and manage trade in the colony. Over time, the structure of the ICS evolved significantly to
            align with the administrative needs of British India.
            Civil Services Evolution Under British
            1. Formative Stage (1675–1765):
            ● The Covenanted Civil Service was formed, separating commercial agents from the company's
            military/naval officers.
            ● Members managed trade stations along the coast under a signed “covenant” (agreement), hence
            the name "covenanted civil servants."
            2. Transition to Administrative Service (1765–1853):
            ● Regulating Act of 1773:
            ○ Separated the company's civil and commercial functions.
            ○ Prohibited private trade and bribery among civil servants.
            ● Pitt’s India Act of 1784:
            ○ Set age limits for recruitment.
            ○ Empowered the Crown to remove corrupt civil servants.
            ○ Lord Cornwallis introduced the two divisions of civil service: (Father of Civil Services in
            India)
            ■ Covenanted Service (higher posts, restricted to Europeans).
            ■ Uncovenanted Service (lower posts, open to Indians).
            ● Charter Act of 1793:
            ○ Vacancies filled from the company's existing civil servants.
            ● Reforms Under Lord Wellesley (1800):
            ○ Founded Fort William College in 1800 for training civil servants in Oriental culture, law,
            and administration.
            ○ Replaced by Haileybury Institution (England) in 1806, which operated until its abolition
            in 1857.
            ● Charter Act of 1833:
            
            ○ Proposed competitive exams for recruitment.
            ○ Open competition concept introduced but implemented only in 1853.
            ● Charter Act of 1853:
            ○ Allowed Indians to enter the covenant service through open competition.
            ○ Macaulay Committee of 1854:
            ■ Civil Service Commission established in 1855 in London to oversee civil service
            examination
            3. Imperial Civil Services (1858 - 1917)
            ● Indian Civil Service Act of 1861:
            ○ Allowed any Indian or European residing in India for at least seven years - eligible to
            qualify.
            ○ In 1870, the British Parliament approved Indian appointments to civil service positions,
            which led to formation of -
            ● Statutory Civil Service (1879):
            ○ Reserved 20% of covenant posts for Indians.
            ○ These positions were to be filled by Indians of high families through nominations by Local
            Government.
            ● Major Commissions and Reforms:
            ○ Aitchison Commission (1886): Formed to devise a plan for broader Indian Inclusion in
            Civil Services.
            ■ Proposed abolishing the term “covenanted civil service.”
            ■ Recommended classification into:
            ● Imperial, Provincial, and Subordinate Services.
            ○ Islington Commission (1912):
            ■ Proposed reserving 25% of civil service posts for Indians.
            ■ Categorizing services under the GOI into Class I and Class II
            ● 1919 Act:
            ○ Divided services into All India, Central, and Provincial categories.
            ○ Proposed a Public Service Commission to oversee recruitment, From 1922 onwards ICS
            Examination began to be held in India.
            ○ Lee Commission (Royal Commission) (1923)
            
            ● 1935 Act:
            ○ Introduced provincial autonomy - Following Provincial Autonomy, only Indian Civil
            Service, Indian Police Service, Indian Medical Service remains as All India Service while other
            services were provincialised.
            ○ Mandated Public Service Commissions for provinces and federation.
            British India - Army
            The Royal Indian Army was crucial for the maintenance and expansion of the British Empire in India. It
            was primarily composed of local soldiers, led by British Officers.
            ● The armies in the Presidencies of Bengal, Bombay and Madras functioned as the respective
            Presidency Army under April 1895, when the Presidency Armies were unified into a Single Indian
            Army.
            ● For Administrative convenience, it was divided into 4 commands -
            ○ Punjab
            ○ Bengal
            ○ Madras
            ○ Bombay
            ● In 1902, Then Commander-in-Chief Lord Kitchener carried out Army reforms which aimed at
            -
            ○ Dividing Indian Army into 2 commands - North and South
            ○ There were 3 brigades in every division of the army - Two of Indian Battalions and one of
            English Battalions.
            ○ To increase the efficiency of Soldiers - Every battalion was subject to a severe test called as
            “Kitchener Test”
            Other Committees attached with this topic -
            ● Peel Commission (1859)
            ● Eden Commission (1879)
            ● Rawlinson Committee (1923)
            ● Skeen Committee (1925) (Indian Sandhurst Committee)
            
            British India - Police
            First understand the Law Enforcement structure under Mughal’s -
            ● Law Enforcement Structure:
            ○ In rural areas, law and order were upheld by faujdars.
            ○ In urban centers, kotwals managed law enforcement.
            ● Zamindars’ Role:
            ○ Under the dual administration system in Bengal, zamindars were responsible for
            maintaining law enforcement.
            ○ They were supported by staff such as thanedars to aid in this duty.
            ● Institutional Changes in 1770:
            ○ The institution of the faujdar was abolished, altering the enforcement mechanism of law
            and order.
            Police System During British Rule
            1774 – Warren Hastings’ Reforms:
            ● Reestablished the faujdars to maintain law and order.
            ● Directed zamindars to assist in suppressing crimes such as:
            ○ Dacoity (robbery by armed gangs).
            ○ Violence.
            ○ Disorder in the regions.
            1775:
            ● Established faujdar thanas (major police stations) in significant towns and regions.
            ● These were supported by minor police stations to create a decentralized enforcement structure.
            Major Objectives of Police under British Rule -
            ● Maintenance of law and order across British-controlled territories.
            ● Suppression of rebellions and uprisings to ensure stability.
            ● Prevention and control of crimes, including loot, theft, robbery, and dacoity.
            1791 – Lord Cornwallis’ Reforms:
            ● Modernized the traditional Indian policing system to align with British administrative needs.
            ● Key Changes Introduced:
            ○ Relieved zamindars of their policing duties to reduce abuse of power.
            
            ○ Divided districts into multiple thanas (police circles) under a daroga (an Indian officer).
            ○ Appointed a Superintendent of Police (SP) as the head of the district policing system.
            1828-1835 – Lord William Bentinck’s Reforms:
            ● Abolished the position of Superintendent of Police (SP).
            ● New Structure:
            ○ The Collector/Magistrate became the head of the police force within his jurisdiction.
            ○ A Commissioner in each division functioned as the SP.
            ● Impact:
            ○ This arrangement led to a poorly organized police force.
            ○ It placed an excessive administrative burden on the collector/magistrate, affecting
            efficiency.
            1861 – Indian Police Act:
            ● The recommendations of the Police Commission (1860) resulted in the passage of the Indian
            Police Act of 1861.
            ● Key Provisions of the Act:
            ○ Creation of a police force in every state and union territory.
            ○ Established guidelines for:
            ■ Recruitment of police personnel.
            ■ Training and deployment procedures.
            ○ Defined police powers and responsibilities, including:
            ■ Maintaining public order.
            ■ Preventing and detecting crimes.
            ■ Enforcing British laws across India.
            ● Limitations:
            ○ The Act failed to create an All-India Police Service, leaving significant differences across
            regions.
            1902 – Indian Police Commission Recommendations:
            ● Commission Head: Andrew Fraser.
            ● Key Recommendations:
            ○ Establishment of specialized branches:
            ■ Criminal Investigation Department (CID) in provinces.
            
            ■ Central Intelligence Bureau at the Centre.
            ○ Separation of law and order enforcement from investigation duties.
            ○ Creation of two distinct service branches:
            ■ Imperial Service Branch: Recruited from England for senior positions.
            ■ Provincial Service Branch: Entirely recruited from within India for lower ranks.
            Economic Impact of British Rule
            Village Conditions
            ● Demographics:
            ○ In the mid-18th century, villages housed around 90% of the total population.
            ● Primary Occupation:
            ○ Agriculture was the main occupation, with landowners and tenants growing crops mainly
            for self-consumption.
            ○ Other occupations included artisans, village officials, and menial laborers.
            ● Social Structure:
            ○ Traditional customs determined rent, wages, and prices.
            ○ Sir Charles Metcalfe described villages as “Little Republics”, highlighting their
            autonomy and self-sufficiency.
            ○ Villages provided nearly everything required for their inhabitants.
            Urban Conditions
            ● Demographics:
            ○ Urban dwellers constituted 10% of the population.
            ● Types of Urban Centers:
            ○ Religious Centers:
            ■ Gaya and Banaras became prominent pilgrimage sites.
            ○ Administrative Capitals:
            ■ Towns like Delhi, Agra, and Lucknow functioned as provincial capitals.
            ○ Commercial Hubs:
            ■ Towns such as Surat and Hubli were vital trading and port centers.
            
            Agriculture
            ● Characteristics:
            ○ Farmers cultivated crops for sustenance and shared their surplus with village artisans.
            ○ Agricultural growth was limited due to reliance on monsoons.
            ● Crops:
            ○ Food crops: Rice, wheat, bajra, jowar, and minor cereals.
            ○ Commercial crops: Jute, raw cotton, groundnut.
            ● Land Revenue System:
            ○ Land revenue was a significant source of state income.
            ○ Zamindars collected taxes and oversaw local judicial and police duties.
            ○ Revenue-sharing system:
            ■ The crop was split between the peasants and the state, with the state typically
            receiving one-third.
            Trade in Pre-British India
            ● Nature of Trade:
            ○ Extensive domestic and international trade, connected with Asia and Europe via land and
            maritime routes like the Silk Road.
            ● Exported Items:
            ○ Cotton textiles, raw silk, indigo, opium, rice, wheat, sugar, pepper, spices, precious stones,
            and drugs.
            ● Imported Items:
            ○ Pearls, wool, dried fruits, and rosewater (from Persia and the Gulf); coffee and gold (from
            Arabia); tea and sugar (from China); metals and paper (from Europe).
            ● Key Ports:
            ○ Surat, Cambay, Calicut, and Masulipatnam were major trade centers.
            ● Trade Balance:
            ○ India had a favorable trade balance, exporting more than it imported.
            ● Local Business Communities:
            ○ Hindu, Jain, and Bohra merchants in Gujarat; Khatris and Lohanis in Punjab; and Marwari
            Banias in Rajasthan.
            
            Important Keywords
            ● Aurang – A permanent market or warehouse for storing and trading goods, used during Mughal
            and British rule.
            ● Banian – Indian intermediaries or brokers who assisted European traders in trade and finance.
            ● Mirasidar – A hereditary landowner in South India, primarily under the Ryotwari system.
            ● Hundi – A financial instrument like a bill of exchange, used for money transfers and trade credit.
            ● Jotedar – Wealthy landholders in Bengal who controlled land and rivaled zamindars in revenue
            collection.
            Industries in Pre-British India
            Handicraft Industries
            ● Contributed 6% to the 18th-century GDP.
            ● Types of Production:
            ○ Jajmani/Balutedar System: Artisans created goods for local consumption.
            ○ Urban Artisanal Production: Specialized in producing high-quality items.
            ○ Dadni System: Merchants advanced cash to artisans for specific goods.
            ○ Karkhanas: Produced luxury items for royalty and nobility.
            Textile Industry
            ● A significant industry, with centers in Dhaka, Surat, Ahmedabad, and Lucknow.
            ● Famous Products:
            ○ Muslins of Dhaka, brocades of Banaras, and chintzes from the Coromandel Coast.
            Industries in Pre-British India
            Shipbuilding
            ● India was a leading shipbuilder, with centers in Bombay, Surat, and Tuticorin.
            ● Indian Ships were sturdier and cheaper than those from Europe.
            ● The Wadia Group led shipbuilding in the 18th century.
            Metallurgy and Other Industries
            ● Wootz steel (High Grade Metal) was highly valued globally.
            ● Other products included jute, paper, oils, and perfumes.
            
            Significant Agricultural Changes
            ● New Land Revenue Policies:
            ○ Altered ownership.
            ● Land Fragmentation -
            ○ British policies led to deindustrialisation, pushing people towards agriculture, resulting in
            land sub division and fragmentation.
            ● Commercialization of Agriculture:
            ○ Focus shifted to cash crops, impacting food security.
            ● Entry of Middlemen:
            ○ Intermediaries exploited farmers.
            British Land Revenue Policies
            Background
            ● In 1765, the East India Company obtained the Diwani of Bengal, Bihar, and Orissa, which meant
            supervision of land and collection of revenue.
            ● Robert Clive:
            ○ Secured revenue rights but used Nawab’s officials for indirect tax collection.
            ○ These officials overtaxed peasants and kept profits, creating inefficiency.
            ● Warren Hastings:
            ○ Attempted reforms like five-year and annual settlements but faced limited success.
            ● Cornwallis’ Contribution:
            ○ Introduced the Permanent Settlement in 1793, which redefined the landlord-tenant
            relationship.
            British Land Revenue Policies - Permanent Settlement (1793)
            ● In 1790, GG Cornwallis announced a decennial (10 Years) settlement, which evolved into the
            Permanent System in 1793. This is also known as the Zamindari System.
            ● Area under this system - 19% of British Indian, mainly in Bengal, Bihar and Orissa.
            Features
            
            ● Hereditary Rights:
            ○ Zamindars gained hereditary rights to sell or transfer land.
            ● Revenue Obligations:
            ○ Zamindars had to pay 10/11th of the revenue to the government, retaining 1/11th for
            themselves.
            ● ‘Sunset Law’:
            ○ Fixed payment deadline; failure resulted in auctioning of zamindari rights.
            ● Landlord-Tenant Formalization:
            ○ Zamindars were required to issue pattas (contracts) to tenants specifying land area and
            rent.
            Drawbacks
            ● Impact on Zamindars:
            ○ High revenue targets and rigid deadlines led to frequent land loss.
            ○ Many zamindars failed to pay, leading to auctioning of estates.
            ● Impact on Peasants:
            ○ Peasants faced oppression through property seizure, beatings, and evictions.
            ○ They often borrowed from moneylenders to pay rent, increasing indebtedness.
            ● Impact on Agriculture:
            ○ Stagnation in agriculture due to absentee landlordism and neglect of land care.
            ○ Disconnect between revenue demand and agricultural productivity.
            Absentee Landlordism
            ● Emerged as zamindars delegated land management to middlemen and moved to urban centers
            like Calcutta.
            ● Reinforced feudalism and widened the gap between rich and poor.
            British Land Revenue Policies - Ryotwari System (1820)
            Introduction
            ● Developed by Thomas Munro in 1820.
            ● First executed by Alexander Reed in 1792 in the Madras Presidency.
            
            ● Under this system, the rights to use and control of land were held by the family, which provided
            the primary labour force on the farm. This system fundamentally changed the relationship
            between State and cultivator (ryots) by allowing direct tax collection from the cultivators, who
            were recognised as landowners.
            ● Covered 51% of British India, including Assam, Coorg, Madras, and Bombay.
            Features
            ● Direct Tax Collection:
            ○ Revenue collected directly from cultivators (ryots), eliminating intermediaries.
            ● Ownership Rights:
            ○ Ryots were granted ownership along with occupancy and transfer rights without limits on
            landholding.
            ● Flexible Revenue System:
            ○ Revenue was adjusted based on production.
            ● Government Control over Barren Land:
            ○ Such land could be cultivated, with revenue shared between cultivators and the state.
            ● Periodic Revenue Revision
            ○ The agreement for revenue could be revised at any time, allowing for adjustments based
            on varying circumstances.
            Drawbacks
            ● Overestimated Revenue:
            ○ Unrealistic targets burdened cultivators.
            ● Harsh Collection Methods:
            ○ Oppressive techniques were common.
            ● Rise of Moneylenders:
            ○ Ryots often fell into debt traps due to high rents.
            While the Ryotwari system removed intermediaries like Zamindars and aimed to simplify the tax collection
            process, the system, in effect, led to problems of landlessness and rural indebtedness.
            
            British Land Revenue Policies - Mahalwari System (1822)
            Introduction
            ● Conceptualized by Holt Mackenzie (1819) and implemented by R.M. Bird (1822) in North West
            territories under the British. It was later revised by GG William Bentinck in 1833.
            ● Integrated features of both Zamindari and Ryotwari systems, came to be known as Modified
            Zamindari System.
            ● Under this system, land was structured into Mahals (village), overseen by village headmen. The
            Village acted as a revenue unit, with land being distributed among Individual peasants. Revenue
            was collected by them and paid to the government, by the collective body.
            Features -
            ● Village-Based Revenue Collection:
            ○ Revenue collected by village headmen, emphasizing community-driven administration.
            ● Mahal as Revenue Units:
            ○ Villages were grouped into mahals for revenue assessment.
            ● Fixed State Share and Regular Revisions:
            ○ Initially set at 50%, later increased to 66%.
            ● Revenue Based on Net Produce:
            ○ Calculations included deductions for expenses and wages.
            
            Drawbacks
            ● Inaccurate Records:
            ○ Manipulation of records by collectors.
            ● Exorbitant Tax Assessments:
            ○ Led to indebtedness and sale of lands.
            ● Undermined Authority:
            ○ Replaced traditional authority with British-appointed village heads.
            Commercialisation of Agriculture
            ● Major Cash Crops of 18th century -
            ○ Silk
            ○ Opium
            ○ Indigo
            ○ Pepper
            ○ Sugarcane
            ○ Tea
            ● Result (Negative) -
            ○ Income Instability - Exposure to Global market - Leading to Revolts.
            ○ Bonded Labour.
            ○ Exploitation and Dependency on Middlemen, moneylenders.
            
            ○ Decline in Food production.
            ● Result (Positive) -
            ○ Growth of Exports.
            ○ Special and Economic Transactions.
            ○ Regional Specialization.
            Deindustrialisation
            ● Deindustrialization refers to the process of economic change where employment in the
            manufacturing sector declines due to economic or political reasons.
            ● Under British rule, this decline was a result of:
            ○ Import of machine-made British goods.
            ○ Loss of traditional Indian craft industries.
            ● Notable economists like R.C. Dutt and Madan Mohan Malaviya highlighted the surge of imports
            of Manchester-made cotton cloth.
            Reasons for Deindustrialization
            ● Decline in Royal Patronage:
            ○ Decreased demand for handmade crafts, as artisans faced competition from
            machine-made goods.
            ● Increase in Trade Imbalance:
            ○ Raw materials exported to Britain were used to create finished goods, which were sold
            back in India.
            ○ Indigenous textiles were replaced by British imports.
            ○ The Charter Act of 1813 promoted one-way free trade, imposing tariffs on Indian goods
            while British goods faced none.
            ● Lack of Modern Industrialization:
            ○ British policies neglected technological investment in Indian industries, rendering them
            unable to compete with British products.
            ● Impact of Railways (Post-1853):
            ○ Facilitated raw material extraction from rural areas.
            ○ Enabled distribution of British goods throughout India.
            
            Outcomes of Deindustrialization
            ● Ruralization:
            ○ Artisans abandoned professions and moved to villages, burdening the agriculture sector.
            (Between 1881 and 1931, the farming population increased from 70 million to 100
            million, while those in industries decreased from 21 million to 13 million.)
            ● Collapse of Weavers and Artisans:
            ○ Earlier, artisans were free to sell to anyone.
            ○ Post-1780s: Gomasthas (EIC agents) forced artisans to sell only to the British.
            ○ Artisans were legally tied to selling only to the Company through the Khatbandi system.
            Outcomes of Deindustrialization
            ● Shift in India’s Trade Dynamics:
            ○ 18th century: India’s main export was cotton cloth (81% of exports).
            ○ Post-deindustrialization:
            ■ Exports shifted to raw materials like opium and indigo.
            ■ Cotton cloth export dropped to 3.7%.
            ■ British imports to India surged, reversing trade trends.
            ● Legislative and Policy Impact:
            ○ The 19th-century policies favored British-built ships, undermining Indian shipping further.
            
            Socio-Religious Reform Movements
            Understanding Socio-Religious Reform Movements
            ● Aim: Transform society and religion for the better by addressing social evils and promoting
            enlightenment.
            ● Key Issues Addressed:
            ○ Women’s Emancipation:
            ■ Abolition of practices like sati, infanticide, child marriage.
            ■ Advocacy for widow remarriage.
            ○ Caste Reform:
            ■ Eradication of caste discrimination and untouchability.
            ○ Promotion of Education:
            ■ Fostered societal enlightenment.
            ○ Religious Reforms:
            ■ Combating idolatry and polytheism.
            ■ Countering exploitation by priests.
            Factors Leading to Socio-Religious Reforms
            ● Contact with the West:
            ○ Modern ideas of liberty, equality, and democracy influenced India’s middle class.
            ● Christian Missionary Efforts:
            ○ The Charter Act of 1813 allowed missionaries to critique religious practices.
            ● Education:
            ○ Reformers like Indian and European scholars fostered pride in India’s heritage,
            empowering movements.
            Reform Strategies and Approaches
            ● Reforms from Within:
            ○ Advocated self-initiated reforms through awareness.
            ● Reforms through Legislation:
            ○ Sought legal solutions but faced limited colonial support.
            
            ● Reforms through Symbols of Change:
            ○ Radical actions (e.g., Derozians’ work).
            ● Reforms through Social Work:
            ○ Practical efforts alongside intellectual endeavors.
            Types of Reformers
            ● Revivalists:
            ○ Restored religion to its original greatness (e.g., Wahabi and Deobandi movements).
            ● Reformists:
            ○ Reshaped society with modern ideas (e.g., Brahmo Samaj, Aligarh movement).
            Socio-Religious Reform Movements - Eastern India
            Raja Ram Mohan Roy and Brahmo Samaj
            ● Known as the Father of Modern India.
            ● Key Contributions:
            ○ Wrote “Tuhfat-ul-Muwahhidin” (A gift to Monotheists) in Persian (1805)
            ○ Atmiya Sabha (1815):
            ■ Critiqued idolatry and polytheism.
            ■ Advocated monotheism and rational thought.
            ○ Calcutta Unitarian Committee (1823)
            ■ RRMR along with Dwarka Nath Tagore established this committee to bring together
            prominent Brahmins and European residents to promote social reforms among
            Hindus.
            ○ Sati Abolition (1829):
            ■ Influenced its banning with the support of William Bentinck. (Dayabhaga and
            Mitakshara school)
            ■ Advocated widow rights and women’s education.
            ● Known as the Father of Modern India.
            ● Key Contributions:
            ○ Education Initiatives:
            ■ Supported Hindu College (1817).
            
            ■ Founded Vedanta College (1825) to blend Western and Indian sciences.
            ○ Publications:
            ■ Translated Vedas and Upanishads.
            ■ Published works like Sambad Kaumudi (Bengali) and Mirat-ul-Akbar (Persian).
            ● Brahmo Samaj (1828):
            ○ Focused on monotheism, social equality, and human dignity.
            Evolution of Brahmo Samaj After Raja Ram Mohan Roy
            ● After the death of Raja Ram Mohan Roy in 1833, the leadership of the Brahmo Samaj passed to
            Debendranath Tagore, the father of Rabindranath Tagore.
            Debendranath Tagore’s Contributions
            ● Tattvabodhini Sabha (1839):
            ○ Founded by Debendranath to carry forward the ideas of Raja Ram Mohan Roy aiming to
            Promote rationalism by focusing on India’s history and indigenous languages.
            ● Reforming Hinduism:
            ○ Focused on internal reform by removing idolatry and irrational customs.
            ○ Strongly resisted Christian missionaries' critiques of Hinduism and their conversion
            efforts.
            ● Expansion Beyond Bengal:
            ○ Debendranath leadership extended the influence of the Brahmo Samaj to regions like:
            ■ United Provinces, Punjab, Bombay, Madras, and others.
            Keshab Chandra Sen’s Role - A prominent reformer, joined the Brahmo Samaj in the mid-19th century
            providing -
            ● New Directions:
            ○ Established the Tabernacle of New Dispensation (1867):
            ■ Aimed to integrate Hinduism, Islam, and Christianity into a unified spiritual
            framework.
            ○ Founded the Indian Reform Association (1870) to address issues related to:
            ■ Women’s rights.
            
            ■ Caste discrimination.
            ■ Education.
            ● Split in the Brahmo Samaj:
            ○ Keshab Chandra Sen’s radical ideas caused divisions within the Samaj:
            ■ Supported teachings from all religions.
            ■ Advocated for inter-caste marriages.
            ○ In 1866, this led to the formation of:
            ■ Brahmo Samaj of India (led by Keshub Chandra Sen).
            ■ Adi Brahmo Samaj (led by Debendranath Tagore).
            Sadharan Brahmo Samaj (1878)
            ● Another split occurred when Keshub Chandra Sen conducted his underage daughter's marriage
            using orthodox rituals, which contradicted the reformist principles of the Brahmo Samaj.
            ● Disgruntled members established the Sadharan Brahmo Samaj.
            ○ Focused on the original Brahmo ideals:
            ■ Faith in a singular supreme being.
            ■ Emphasis on reason and morality.
            ■ Opposition to orthodox practices.
            Henry Vivian Derozio and Young Bengal Movement
            ● Derozio:
            ○ Anglo-Indian radical thinker, professor at Hindu College, Kolkata.
            ○ Founded the Academic Association and advocated free thought.
            ○ Promoted liberty, equality, and fraternity.
            
            ● Young Bengal Movement:
            ○ Influenced by Western thinkers.
            ○ Promoted RRMR’s ideas.
            ○ Focused on education, press freedom, and justice.
            Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar
            ● Key Contributions:
            ○ Reformed Sanskrit education; admitted non-Brahmins in Sanskrit College (1851).
            ○ Led the Hindu Widow Remarriage Act (1856).
            ○ Advocated women's education and established schools for girls.
            ● Authored works like Barna Parichay (Modern Bengali Language Grammar) and played a crucial
            role in societal reform.
            Socio-Religious Reform Movements - Western India
            Balshastri Jambhekar
            ● Known as the "Father of Marathi Journalism".
            ● Key Contributions:
            ○ Founded the first Marathi newspaper, Darpan, in 1832.
            ● Social Reforms:
            ○ Opposed caste discrimination, sati, and child marriage.
            ○ Advocated women’s education and widow remarriage.
            Manav Dharma Sabha
            ● Founded in Surat (1844) by Durgaram Manchharam, Dadoba Pandurang, and Dalpatram
            Bhagubai.
            ● Focus:
            ○ Challenged caste systems and societal superstitions.
            ○ Held weekly meetings discussing rationalism and equality.
            Paramahansa Mandali
            ● Established in Bombay (1849) by Ram Bal Krishna Jayakar and Dadoba Pandurang.
            
            ● Operated as a radical socio-religious group.
            ● Teachings:
            ○ Advocated monotheism and morality over rituals.
            ○ Opposed caste discrimination.
            ○ Supported widow remarriage and women’s education.
            Prarthana Samaj
            ● Founded by Atmaram Pandurang in Bombay (1867).
            ● Goals:
            ○ Influenced by Brahmo Samaj ideals.
            ○ Advocated for women’s education and abolition of caste discrimination.
            Jyotirao Phule and the Satyashodhak Samaj
            Jyotirao Phule:
            ● Criticized orthodox Brahmins and caste dominance.
            ● Advocated for education of women and lower castes.
            ● Published Gulamgiri and other works emphasizing equality.
            ● Awarded title “Mahatma” for social reform works.
            Savitribai Phule: (Wife)
            ● Opened India’s first school for girls (1851).
            ● Founded Mahila Seva Mandal (1852).
            Satyashodhak Samaj (1873):
            ● Promoted inter-caste marriages, child marriage elimination, and widow remarriage.
            ● Aimed at societal equality and education for marginalized sections.
            Gopal Hari Deshmukh (Lokhitwadi)
            ● A prominent social reformer and writer from Maharashtra.
            ● Key Contributions:
            ○ Advocated for modern human values and criticized social evils through his journal
            Prabhakar (1848–1889).
            ○ Supported the Swadeshi movement, promoting self-reliance and indigenous production.
            
            ○ Championed women’s education and spoke against caste discrimination and
            untouchability.
            Gopal Ganesh Agarkar
            ● Co-founder of several significant educational institutions:
            ○ New English School (1880) in Poona, Deccan Education Society (1884), Fergusson
            College (1885).
            ● Advocated for Age of Consent Act (1891), which raised the legal age for marriage.
            ● Social Reforms:
            ○ Worked for women's rights, opposing child marriage and advocating legal changes.
            ○ Supported legislative reforms over traditional methods.
            Maharshi Dhondo Keshav Karve
            Known as a pioneer for widow remarriage and women’s education.
            Key Achievements:
            ● Founded:
            ○ Vidhwa Vivah Uttejak Mandali (1893) to encourage widow remarriage.
            ○ Hindu Widows Home Association (1896) to provide shelter and education for widows.
            ○ SNDT Women’s University (1916), India’s first women’s university, in Maharashtra.
            ● Advocated for women’s self-reliance through education and vocational training.
            ● Married a widow after his first wife’s death to set an example for society.
            ● Honored with the Bharat Ratna (1958) for his contributions to social reform.
            Servants of India Society
            ● Founded by Gopal Krishna Gokhale in 1905 at Poona.
            ● Core Ideals:
            ○ Advocated for selfless service to the masses, focusing on the underprivileged, rural
            communities, and tribal populations.
            ○ Promoted reforms in education, sanitation, and health care.
            ● Gopal Krishna Gokhale:
            ○ Leader of the moderate faction of the Indian National Congress.
            ○ Mentored Mahatma Gandhi, influencing his political philosophy.
            
            ○ Famous for the quote: “What Bengal thinks today, India thinks tomorrow.”
            ○ Published The Hitavada (Nagpur, 1911), a newspaper to propagate reformist ideas.
            Behramji Malabari
            ● A Parsi social reformer known for his work on women’s rights.
            ● Key Contributions:
            ○ Advocated for Age of Consent Act (1891), raising the marriageable age for girls.
            ○ Founded Seva Sadan (1908), which provided shelter, education, and vocational training
            for destitute women.
            ○ His writings in The Indian Muse in English Garb inspired societal changes and brought
            attention to women’s issues.
            Pandita Ramabai
            ● First Indian woman awarded the titles “Pandita” and “Saraswati” by the University of Calcutta
            for her knowledge and scholarship.
            ● Reforms and Contributions:
            ○ Founded:
            ■ Sharada Sadan (Bombay): A home for widows.
            ■ Mukti Mission (1890s): A Christian charity in Pune for destitute women.
            ○ Toured the US and raised funds to support Indian widows.
            ○ Authored:
            ■ Stri Dharma Niti (Morals for Women) (1882).
            ■ The High-Caste Hindu Woman, which critiqued child marriage and widow
            exploitation.
            ○ Honored with the Kaisar-i-Hind Medal in 1919 for her social work.
            Rukhmabai Case
            ● Rukhmabai (1864–1955):
            ○ A physician and feminist who became one of India’s first women doctors.
            ● Legal Battle:
            ○ Fought against child marriage in Rukhmabai vs. Dadaji Bhikaji (1885).
            ○ Refused to live with her husband, leading to a landmark court case.
            
            ○ The verdict, in her favor, questioned the legality of child marriage and drew widespread
            attention to women’s rights in India.
            Social Service League - Founded by N.M Joshi - Bombay (1911)
            Vishnushastri Chiplunkar - Co founded Mahratta and Kesari along with Bal Gangadhar Tilak and Gopal
            Ganesh Agarkar.
            Socio-Religious Reform Movements - Pan India
            Ramakrishna Movement
            Founded by Ramakrishna Paramahansa (1834–1886).
            Core Teachings:
            ● All religions lead to the same goal.
            ● Emphasized spiritual equality and the service of mankind as service to God.
            Objectives:
            ● Establish a monastic order dedicated to spiritual pursuit.
            ● Engage in philanthropic and charitable works across caste, color, and creed.
            Paramahansa disciples under the leadership of Vivekananda, formed the Ramakrishna Math in 1887 and
            subsequently in 1897. The HQ’s of Math and Mission were located at Belur, near Calcutta.
            Swami Vivekananda (Narendra Nath Dutta)
            ● Disciple of Ramakrishna Paramahansa and a key proponent of the Vedantic philosophy.
            ● Key Contributions:
            ○ Represented Hinduism at the World Parliament of Religions (1893) in Chicago, elevating
            India’s spiritual legacy globally.
            ○ Founded:
            ■ Ramakrishna Mission (1897): Focused on education, health care, and social
            welfare.
            ■ Belur Math (West Bengal): Headquarters of the mission.
            ○ Published:
            ■ Prabuddha Bharata (monthly in English).
            
            ■ Udbodhan (fortnightly in Bengali).
            Swami Dayanand Saraswati and Arya Samaj
            Founder: Swami Dayananda Saraswati (original name: Mool Shankar Tiwari) established Arya Samaj in
            Bombay, 1875.
            Famous Slogan: “Go Back to the Vedas”.
            Ideology:
            ● Opposed idolatry, ritualism, and priesthood.
            ● Advocated returning to Vedic principles as the ultimate truth.
            ● Opposed Child Marriages and encouraged remarriage of widows. (Something out of logic)
            Key Contributions
            ● Works:
            ○ Authored Satyarth Prakash (The Light of Truth).
            ○ Gave Slogan - “India for Indians” in 1876. He is known as Martin Luther of India.
            ○ Other works include Veda Bhasya and Veda Bhasya Bhumika.
            ● Ghar Wapsi Movement:
            ○ Introduced Shuddhi Movement, advocating for reconversion of Hindus who had
            converted to other religions.
            ○ Promoted cow protection through his work Gokarunanidhi.
            Post-Dayananda Era
            ● Divisions in Arya Samaj:
            ○ College Party:
            ■ Led by Lala Hansraj, Lala Lajpat Rai, and Lala Lal Chand.
            ■ Focused on modern education, establishing DAV schools and colleges (e.g., DAV
            College in Lahore).
            ○ Mahatma Party (later Gurukul Party):
            ■ Led by Swami Shraddhanand and Guru Datta Vidyarthi.
            ■ Advocated Sanskrit and Vedic education, establishing the Kanya Mahavidyalaya
            for widows and Gurukuls
            ● Arya Mahila Samaj, also known as Arya Women Society, was founded by Pandita Ramabai in
            
            1882
            Indian Social Conference
            ● Founded: 1887 by M.G. Ranade and Raghunath Rao.
            ● Key Features:
            ○ Known as the social reform cell of the Indian National Congress.
            ○ Addressed social issues like caste discrimination and women’s rights.
            The Theosophical Society
            ● Founded: 1875, New York, by Madame Helena Blavatsky and Colonel H.S. Olcott.
            ● Shifted Headquarters: Adyar, Madras, India (1882).
            ● Key Tenets:
            ○ Brotherhood: Instilling fraternity without distinction of race, creed, or gender.
            ○ Religion and Philosophy: Studying both in a parallel manner.
            ○ Mysticism: Investigating nature’s hidden laws and mysteries.
            ● Social Reform Work:
            ○ Advocated caste abolition and untouchability eradication.
            ○ Founded Central Hindu School (1898) in Varanasi, which evolved into Banaras Hindu
            University (BHU) led by Madan Mohan Malviya.
            Radha Swami Satsang
            ● Founded: 1861, by Shiv Dayal Saheb.
            ● Core Teachings:
            ○ Belief in one supreme being and spiritual equality.
            ○ Rejected idol worship and emphasized moral living and ethical practices.
            Dev Samaj
            ● Founder: Shiv Narayan Agnihotri, 1887.
            ● Focus:
            ○ Social integration of castes.
            ○ Promoted women’s education and moral reform.
            
            ● Publication:
            ○ Teachings compiled in Dev Shastra by Agnihotri.
            Bharat Dharma Mahamandal
            ● Founded: 1902 in Varanasi.
            ● Objective:
            ○ Unified organizations like Sanatana Dharma Sabha and Dharma Maha Parishad to
            defend Hinduism against reformist movements like Arya Samaj and Theosophists.
            Socio-Religious Reform Movements - Caste Reform Movements
            Causes for the Rise of Caste Movements
            ● Grievances of Educated Men:
            ○ Discrimination by upper castes.
            ○ Challenges against centuries-old caste-based inequality.
            ● Social Mobility Aspirations:
            ○ Lower castes sought upward movement through Sanskritization.
            ○ Adoption of upper-caste customs, manners, and taboos.
            ● Radical Leaders:
            ○ Leaders like Jyotirao Phule and Periyar fought against Brahminical dominance.
            ● British Interventions:
            ○ From the 1901 Census, castes were categorized hierarchically, creating rigid social
            divisions.
            Caste Reform Movements in South India
            Ayya Vaikundar (Tamil Nadu)
            ● Advocated:
            ○ Equality and fraternity.
            ○ Abolition of caste-based discrimination.
            ● Contributions:
            ○ Simplified marriage customs without Sanskrit rituals.
            ○ Organized Sampanthi Bhojana (community meals) for diverse castes.
            
            ○ Dug common wells for lower-caste communities.
            ○ Inspired priests to apply sacred paste to all, regardless of caste.
            Ezhava Movement (Kerala)
            ● Founder: Sree Narayana Guru Swamy among Ezhava community of Kerala.
            ● Actions:
            ○ Installed a Shivalinga (1888) to challenge the monopoly of higher castes.
            ○ Led the Aruvippuram Kshetra Yogam (1889) to fight for:
            ■ Temple entry for Ezhavas.
            ■ Education and representation in government services.
            ● Legacy:
            ○ Formed SNDP (Sree Narayana Dharma Paripalana Yogam) and worked for reforms like -
            ■ Right to admission in Schools.
            ■ Recruitment in Govt. Jobs.
            ■ Access to Roads and entry to temples.
            ■ Political Representation.
            Palli Movement (Tamil Nadu)
            ● Focused on lower-caste Pallis adopting Kshatriya customs.
            Vokkaliga Sangha (Mysore Region)
            ● Founded in 1905 as an anti-Brahmin movement.
            ● Promoted social, cultural, and educational upliftment of the Vokkaliga community.
            Nadar Movement (Tamil Nadu)
            ● Focused on toddy-tappers achieving Kshatriya status.
            ● Established Nadar Mahajana Sangam (1910).
            Justice Movement (Madras)
            ● Leaders: C.N. Mudaliar, T.M. Nair, P. Tyagaraja Chetti.
            ● Demands:
            
            ○ Greater representation in government services and politics for intermediate castes.
            Self-Respect Movement (E.V. Ramaswamy Periyar)
            ● Advocated:
