            ○ Weddings without Brahmin priests.
            ○ Forcible temple entry and rejecting Manusmriti.
            Periyar (E.V. Ramaswamy Naicker) - Tamil Nadu
            ● Ideology:
            ○ Rejected Brahminical culture and superstitions.
            ○ Promoted Dravidian leadership through Dravida Kazhagam.
            ○ Advocated atheism, education, and anti-caste reforms.
            ● Symbolism:
            ○ Black shirts and flags were used to protest caste hierarchies.
            Vaikom Satyagraha (1924) (Kerala)
            ● Leaders: K.P. Kesava, Sree Narayana Guru, T.K. Madhavan.
            ● Objectives:
            ○ Demanded temple entry for Dalits.
            ○ Supported by Gandhi, it led to the opening of state-managed temples to all in 1936.
            Caste Reform Movements in Rest of India
            Gopal Baba Walangkar (Maharashtra)
            ● Founder: Anarya Dosh-Parihar Mandali (1886).
            ● Works:
            ○ Challenged untouchability and caste oppression through writings like Vital Viddhwansak.
            Kisan Faghu Bansode (Maharashtra)
            ● Contributions:
            ○ Founded Antyaj Samaj for Dalit education.
            ○ Wrote plays, poems, and articles advocating for emancipation.
            
            Shivram Janba Kamble (Maharashtra)
            ● Edited Somvanshiya Mitra, focusing on the Dalit cause.
            ● Organized India’s first Dalit conference.
            Maharshi Vitthal Ramji Shinde (Maharashtra)
            ● Founder of India Depressed Classes Mission (1906).
            ● Advocated untouchability eradication and Dalit upliftment and thus established “All India
            Untouchability League”
            Mahad Movement
            ● Dr. B.R Ambedkar was the frontrunner in this Satyagraha in March 1927, targeting case Hindu
            Traditions.
            ● In 1924, earlier Ambedkar ji had founded Bahishkrit Hitkarini Sabha, aiming to voice the
            challenges that Dalit’s faced.
            ● Guiding Principle - “Educate,Agitate and Organize”
            Parsi Reform Movements
            Rahnomae Mazdayasanan Sabha (1851):
            ● Focused on Zoroastrian religion restoration.
            ● Led by Naoroji Furdonji and Dadabhai Naoroji (Rast Goftar)
            Muslim Reform Movements
            Wahhabi Movement (Muslims) (Revivalist)
            ● Founder: Syed Ahmad Barelvi.
            ● Advocated:
            ○ Unity in Islamic interpretation.
            ○ Resisting British rule and Sikh authority.
            Fairaizi Movement (Revivalist)
            
            ● Founder - Haji Shariatullah.
            ● Aim - To protest against the prevailing un Islamic practices among Muslims.
            ● Influence by Wahabi Movement
            Ahmadiyya Movement (Punjab) (Reformist)
            ● Founder: Mirza Ghulam Ahmad (1889).
            ● Promoted universal brotherhood, Western liberal education, and religious tolerance.
            Syed Ahmed Khan: (Reformist)
            ● Established Muhammadan Anglo-Oriental College (1875), later Aligarh Muslim University.
            ● Advocated modernization of Muslim society.
            ○ Established Scientific Society of ALigarh, modelling on Royal Society of london.
            ● Wanted to leverage British support to promote Muslim Education and employment post revolt
            politics.
            Sikh Reform Movements
            Nirankari Movement:
            ● Founder: Baba Dayal Das (1783–1855).
            ● Rejected idol worship and emphasized adherence to Guru Nanak’s teachings.
            ● Against evils such as Meat Eating and Drinking Liquor.
            Namdhari Movement:
            ● Founder: Baba Ram Singh (1816–1885).
            ● Objectives:
            ○ Revitalized Sikhism and enforced strict vegetarianism.
            Singh Sabha Movement (1873):
            ● Led by Thakur Singh Sandhawalia and Giani Gian SIngh and established Singh Sabha of Amritsar.
            ● Objectives:
            ○ Counter Christian and Muslim conversion efforts.
            ○ Promote Gurumukhi and Punjabi literature.
            
            ○ Established Khalsa College (1892) in Amritsar.
            Akali Movement:
            ● Targeted corrupt Mahants managing Sikh gurdwaras and also the bring back the control of
            Khalsa College of Amritsar under the representatives of Sikh community.
            ● Gave support to Non Cooperation Movement.
            ● Resulted in the Sikh Gurdwara Act (1925) for democratic management.
            
            People’s Resistance Against British Rule (Revolts)
            Meaning of People’s Resistance
            ● The term "people" refers to diverse segments of Indian society who were adversely affected by
            foreign domination. These groups included:
            ○ Peasants
            ○ Artisans
            ○ Tribals
            ○ Ruling Classes (both active and dispossessed)
            ○ Military Personnel (serving under the Company or demobilized from ex-rulers’ armies)
            ○ Religious Leaders (both Hindu and Muslim)
            ● Purpose of Resistance:
            ○ Safeguarding their interests.
            ○ Fought both independently and in collaboration with others.
            Factors for People’s Uprisings Against the British -
            ● Economic and Social Grievances
            ○ Collapse of indigenous industries.
            ○ Exploitation by non-tribal people like moneylenders and traders.
            ○ Discontent due to land policies.
            ● Impact of East India Company’s Rule
            ○ Resentment from zamindars and artisans.
            ○ Erosion of traditional rights and ownership systems.
            ● Cultural Factors
            ○ Desire for autonomy within the Indian framework.
            ○ Resistance to cultural influences like Sanskritization.
            Civil and Peasant Uprisings During British Rule (Pre 1857)
            
            Sanyasi Revolt (1760s–1800)
            ● Nature of Revolt:
            ○ An uprising by Hindu Sanyasis and Muslim Fakirs in Eastern India.
            ○ Gained support from rural impoverished communities and minor zamindars.
            ● Causes:
            ○ Religious Restrictions:
            ■ British restrictions on pilgrimage and imposition of taxes on religious donations.
            ○ Economic Hardship:
            ■ The Bengal Famine of 1770 exacerbated difficulties, leading to rebellion.
            ● Major Areas:
            ○ Purnea, Malda, Dhaka, Rangpur, Cooch Behar, and Murshidabad (Bengal Province).
            ● Notable Leaders and Activities -
            ○ Bhawani Pathak, Majnu Shah, Chirag Ali, Musa Shah.
            ○ Launched surprise raids on British outposts, treasuries and factories.
            ● Outcome:
            ○ Eventually suppressed after prolonged struggle.
            ● Significance:
            ○ Inspired literary works like "Devi Chaudhurani" and "Anandamath" by Bankim Chandra
            Chatterjee.
            Rangpur Dhing (1783)
            Area of Revolt -
            ● Rangpur District of Bengal in 1783.
            Nature of Revolt:
            ● Protest by peasants and zamindars in Bengal against excessive revenue demands.
            ● Reaction against the Ijaradari System. (Land Auctioned to the highest bidder, I hope you
            understand it)
            Leader:
            ● Kena Sarkar.
            Outcome:
            ● Highlighted flaws in the Ijardar System, leading to reforms like the Permanent Settlement.
            
            Paika Rebellion (1817)
            ● Location:
            ○ Odisha.
            ● Causes:
            ○ Resistance against British policies, particularly high land revenue.
            ● Leaders:
            ○ Bakshi Jagabandhu Bidyadhar, Mukunda Deva.
            ● Outcome:
            ○ Led to changes in British governance policies but was suppressed by 1818.
            ○ These included large remission of arrears, reduction in assessments and the introduction
            of a new settlement on fixed tenures.
            Additional Fact - In 2017, the State of Odisha urged the central Govt. to recognize the Paika rebellion as the
            First War of Independence, but the Indian Council of Historical Research (ICHR) disagreed.
            Who were Paikas ? Paikas or Foot Soldiers had been recruited since the 16th century by Kings in Odisha to
            render martial services in return for hereditary rent free lands.
            Mysore Rebellion (1830)
            ● Location:
            ○ Mysore (Nagar District)
            ● Causes:
            ○ Exploitative taxation after reinstatement of the Wodeyar dynasty and enforcement of
            subsidiary alliances, led to burdening on the lower sections of Society like Peasants.
            ● Leader:
            ○ Sardar Malla
            ● Outcome:
            ○ The British gained direct administration of the region.
            Kuka Movement (1840–1850)
            ● Nature:
            ○ Also known as the Namdhari Movement.
            
            ○ Aimed at religious purification and political resistance against British rule.
            ● Founder:
            ○ Bhagat JawaharMal.
            ● Leader:
            ○ Baba Ram Singh, emphasized military training and structured resistance.
            Kukas boycotted British goods, institutions, and Laws.
            ● Outcome:
            ○ Suppressed by the British; Baba Ram Singh deported to Rangoon.
            Peasant Movements with Religious Overtones
            Narkelberia Uprising (1830s):
            ● Led by Syed Mir Nisar Ali (Titu Mir).
            ● Muslim tenants rose against Hindu Landlords and British Indigo planters who composed a bread
            tax on Faraizi Muslims.
            ● Revolt initially focused on economic grievances but later merged into Wahabi movement, which
            had religious overtones.
            The Pagal Panthis (1825–1833):
            ● Peasant uprisings led by a religious sect in present day Bangladesh.
            ● Initiated by Karam Shah as a religious movement and later led by his son Tipu along with local
            tribes (Turning it into a (Political Movement)
            ● Revolt directed against the oppression of the Zamindars and thus refused to pay excessive rent
            and attacked the house of Zamindars.
            ● Suppressed by the British after large scale military operations.
            Faraizi Revolt (1818–1857):
            ● Led by Haji Shariatullah and later by Dudu Mian of Faraizi sect.
            ● Movement sought to cleanse Islam and supported Tenants’ rights against Zamindars.
            ● Many Faraizis ultimately joined the Wahhabi Movement.
            
            Other Major Uprisings
            
            Tribal Uprisings During British Rule
            ● Tribes residing in isolation during the 18th century were disconnected from mainstream society,
            with distinct agricultural methods, traditions and societal structures.
            ● They inhabited mainly forested land, which the British gradually encroached upon, result of
            which were recurrent Tribal uprisings happening under British rule, which can be divided under
            two heads -
            Understanding the evolution of Tribal Movements under British Rule -
            ● First Phase (1795–1860):
            ○ Rise of the British Empire.
            ○ Leadership of revolts from traditional sections whose privileges were undermined, such as
            Zamindars.
            ● Second Phase (1860–1920):
            ○ Intensive colonialism coincided with mercantilism - Impact on tribal economy, land, and
            forests.
            ○ Leadership emerged from lower tribal rung.
            ● Third Phase (1920–1947):
            ○ Tribals engaged in nationalist, agrarian, and separatist movements.
            ○ Leadership came from educated tribal leaders and outsiders.
            
            Plight of the Tribals Under Colonial Rule - Key Issues
            ● Debt Spirals and Outsider Dominance:
            ○ Traders and moneylenders offered loans for acquiring non-local goods.
            ○ Led to dependency, high-interest rates, and subsequent debt cycles.
            ● Eroded Chieftaincy: (From Leaders to Enforcers)
            ○ Tribal leaders' roles diminished to mere enforcers of British policies.
            ○ Loss of respect among their own people.
            ● The Agrarian Dilemma: (Forced Settlements and Fallow Fields)
            ○ Forced to settle and adopt farming under unsuitable conditions.
            ○ Increased hardships due to unsustainable practices.
            ● Forests Under Siege: (Land now State Property of British)
            ○ Forest laws restricted tribal access, impacting their livelihood.
            ○ Forced relocation and loss of autonomy.
            ● Labour Traps: (Plantations and Mines)
            ○ Recruitment for tea plantations and mining led to loss of mobility and freedom.
            
            Indian Forest Act
            A key source of conflict between the Tribals and British was the enactment of the Indian Forest Act and its
            amendments. In 1850’s, there was demand for timber due to 2 primary reasons -
            ● Introduction of Railways in India - Timber used as a fuel for locomotives and for railways
            sleepers to support the tracks.
            ● Shipbuilding - Importance of Indian Teak for shipbuilding was important for sustaining British
            Naval Dominance.
            Thus a clear Forest Policy was established by Lord Dalhousie in 1864, and attempt was made to establish
            British control over Forests under various legislations with the objective of -
            ● Declared forests as state property.
            ● Allowed British control over land containing trees.
            Forest Act of 1878
            Act divided Indian Forests into -
            ● Reserved Forests (For Heavy Commercial Use) (Completely Government Controlled)
            ● Protected Forests (Partly Government Controlled)
            ● Village Forests (Controlled by Village near Forests)
            Thus, Tribal communities lost their traditional rights, such as grazing and agriculture, and given only
            limited privileges in those areas.
            Forest Act of 1927
            ● Did not acknowledge community rights, Instead individuals or communities were required to
            claim their rights over specific forest land before the Forest Officer.
            ● Traditional practices like shifting agriculture, collecting forest produce were made illegal.
            
            Revolt by Mainland Indian Tribes
            Pahariyas' Rebellion (1778)
            ● Area - Rajmahal Hills (Jharkhand)
            ● Cause - Protested against British disruption of their lifestyle.
            ● Main Tactic - Engaged Britishers in Guerilla warfare.
            ● Result - British declared Pahariya inhabited region a “Daman-i-Koh” area, meaning “forbidden
            territory” and agreed not to extend their administration further into that area.
            Chuar Uprising (1798–1799)
            ● Area - Midnapore and Bankura districts of Bengal.
            ● Cause - Protested against British revenue policies.
            ● Activity -
            ○ Multiple Waves of Chaur uprising happened between 1766 and 1798.
            ○ Led by Durjan Singh, A deprived zamindars.
            ○ Also known as Revolt of Jungle Mahal.
            ○ Revolt brutally suppressed by British.
            Kol Revolt (1831)
            ● Location: Chotanagpur.
            ● Cause: Against transfer of land to non-tribal settlers and oppressive policies of British Colonial
            administration.
            ● Leader: Budho Bhagat under whose guidance rebels resorted to violence, killing about a
            thousand outsiders perceived as oppressors.
            ● Outcome: Suppressed with large-scale military operations.
            Ho and Munda Uprisings (1820–1837)
            ● Location - Singhbhum and broader region of Chotanagpur
            ● Cause - Resistance to British revenue systems and Bengali settlers.
            ● Leadership: Raja of Parahat.
            ● Activity - Ho Revolt continued until 1827, when Ho tribals were eventually compelled to
            
            surrender. However in 1831, they started another rebellion, joined by Mundas of Chotanagpur,
            again subdued by 1837.
            Koli Uprising (1822–1829)
            ● Location: Maharashtra (Particularly Akola hills)
            ● Cause: British interventions in Tribal rights and Exploitation by moneylenders and zamindars.
            ● Leader: Ramji Bhangre
            ● Activity - Rebels targeted Baniya moneylenders, attacking and looting their financial documents.
            The British responded with military force.
            Ramosi Uprisings (1820s–1830s)
            ● Location - Western Ghats
            ● Cause - Protested against loss of livelihoods after annexation of Maratha territories.
            ● Leader - Led by Chittur Singh and Umaji Naik.
            ● Activity - Resorted to armed attacks on British establishments, British brutally suppressed the
            revolts.
            Santhal Rebellion (1855–1856)
            ● Area: Santhal Pargana (Jharkhand and West Bengal).
            ● Causes:
            ○ Encroachment on land by outsiders (Dikus) leading to gradual invasion of their land rights.
            ○ Economic exploitation by zamindars.
            ○ Loss of Traditional Lifestyle.
            ● Leaders: Sidhu and Kanhu Murmu, rallied the Santhals behind them with the belief that they
            were divinely chosen to end the ongoing oppression and restore their rightful way of life.
            ● Tactics: Armed insurrection with traditional weapons.
            ● Outcome:
            ○ The British suppressed the rebellion.
            ○ Creation of Santhal Pargana as an administrative district in 1855, thus granting them
            some autonomy.
            ○ 1876, Santhal Parganas Tenancy Act further safeguarded tribal land rights by prohibiting
            
            its transfer to non-tribal individuals.
            Khond Uprisings (1837–1856)
            ● Causes:
            ○ Resentment against British control, cultural disruption, and loss of territorial rights.
            ○ Abolition of the Meriah System (human sacrifices for fertility rituals).
            ○ Economic exploitation through increased taxes and intrusion by moneylenders.
            ● Regions:
            ○ Hilly tracts of Odisha and Andhra Pradesh.
            ● Leader:
            ○ Chakra Bisoi united tribes like the Ghumusar and Kalahandi against British policies.
            ● Methods:
            ○ Used traditional weapons such as tangi (axes), bows, arrows, and swords.
            ● Outcome:
            ○ Fierce resistance ultimately suppressed due to superior British military strength.
            Koya Revolts (1803–1886)
            ● Causes:
            ○ Oppression by police and moneylenders.
            ○ Exploitation under British forest laws.
            ● Notable Leaders:
            ○ Tomma Sora (1879–1880 rebellion) and Raja Anantayyar.
            ● Methods:
            ○ Guerrilla tactics exploiting the terrain.
            ● Outcome:
            ○ Suppressed by British forces.
            
            Bhil Revolts
            ● Causes:
            ○ Famine, economic hardship, and British misgovernance with notable revolts occurring
            repeatedly in 1817,1825,1831,1846 and 1913.
            ● Regions:
            ○ Western Ghats, southern Rajasthan (Banswara district), and Dhule district (Maharashtra).
            This areas was strategically significant for its role in linking central India to western coast
            of India.
            ● Notable Events:
            ○ Sindwa Treasury Loot (1857):
            ■ Around 500 Bhils, led by Kazi Singh, plundered the treasury.
            ○ Mangarh Massacre (1913): (Adivasi Jallianwala)
            ■ Bhils, inspired by Govind Guru, peacefully protested for land and rights.
            ■ British forces killed over 1,500 people, including women and children.
            ● Methods:
            ○ Guerrilla warfare and exploitation of mountainous terrain for plundering and looting state
            treasures.
            ● Outcome:
            ○ Revolts suppressed by the British.
            Munda Rebellion (1899–1900) (Ulgulan which means Uprising is a famous word for this revolt)
            ● Region: Chotanagpur region (Jharkhand).
            ● Causes:
            ○ Loss of tribal lands under the Khuntkatti System (tribal concept of collective ownership of
            land) - Non Tribal settlers (Outsiders - Dikus) became jagirdars and zamindars, seizing
            Munda land and forcing Mundas into landless labour.
            ○ Large forest areas were declared as protected forests, depriving Mundas of their forest
            rights.
            ○ Exploitation through beth begari (forced labor) and Christian missionary interference.
            ● Leader:
            ○ Birsa Munda:
            
            ■ Declared himself a messenger of God and started a new faith called Birsait.
            ■ Advocated for Hindu-Christian elements into new Munda ideology.
            ● Methods: Transformed into a political and religious campaign known as the “Sardari Fight”,with
            the aim of establishing Munda Raj.
            ● Outcome:
            ○ Birsa's arrest and death in 1900 ended the movement.
            ○ The Chotanagpur Tenancy Act (1908) recognized tribal land rights such as Khuntkatti
            rights, Banned Begari and restricted the transfer of tribal land to non tribal individuals.
            Tribal Uprisings of North East
            Ahom Revolt (1828)
            ● Causes: British refusal to leave Assam after the 1826 Treaty of Yandaboo, contrary to the
            promise made at the end of Anglo Burmese War.
            ● Regions: Sibsagar district, Assam.
            ● Leader: Gomadhar Konwar and Dhanjay Borgohain.
            ● Outcome: British suppressed the movement and executed leaders.
            Anglo-Khasi War (1829–1833)
            ● Causes: Khasi resistance to British colonization.
            ● Leader: Tirath Singh organized an armed revolt.
            ● Outcome: Tirath Singh was captured and exiled to Dhaka.
            Singhpo Rebellions (1830, 1843)
            ● Regions:
            ○ Arunachal Pradesh.
            ● Leaders:
            ○ Bom Singhpo (1830): Attacked British armory but was captured.
            ○ Beesa Gaum (1843): Led armed resistance but was sentenced to life imprisonment.
            Tai Khamti Resistance (1839–1844)
            ● Location: Arunachal Pradesh
            
            ● Causes: Fear of British-imposed taxes.
            ● Leaders:
            ○ Chow Rang Pha Gohain.
            ● Outcome:
            ○ The British defeated the Khamtis and asserted control over Arunachal Pradesh.
            Some Important Mutinites During British Rule
            Vellore Mutiny (1806)
            ● Date & Location: 10 July 1806, Vellore Fort, Tamil Nadu.
            ● Nature: Early armed revolt by Indian sepoys against British rule
            Causes:
            ● Cultural Interference:
            ○ British military regulations banned religious symbols (e.g., caste marks) and forced sepoys
            to wear European hats.
            ● Resentment:
            ○ Sepoys angered by racial discrimination and poor treatment.
            ○ Sympathy for Tipu Sultan’s family, imprisoned in Vellore Fort.
            Vellore Mutiny (1806)
            The Revolt:
            ● Sepoys killed 14 British officers and unfurled Tipu Sultan’s flag.
            ● Around 1,500 soldiers participated in the mutiny.
            Outcome:
            ● Suppressed by British reinforcements led by Colonel Gillespie.
            ● 800 sepoys were killed; many executed or punished.
            ● Tipu Sultan’s family relocated to Calcutta.
            Significance:
            ● A precursor to the Indian Rebellion of 1857, highlighting early resistance to British cultural and
            political dominance.
            
            Peasant Movement Post 1857
            The Peasant Movements that emerged after 1857 were characterized by a greater degree of
            organizations, coordination and political consciousness. The peasants began to form organisations and
            unions to fight for their rights. They also started to participate in political movements and demand
            representation in the legislative bodies.
            Indigo Revolt (1859-60)
            Cause:
            ● Exploitation by British planters under the Nij and Ryoti systems for cultivating indigo.
            ○ Under the Nij system, planters forced workers on owned or rented lands.
            ○ Under the Ryoti system, peasants were coerced into growing indigo, leading to degraded
            soil and a vicious loan cycle.
            Key Events:
            ● 1859: Digambar Biswas and Bishnu Biswas led peasants in Bengal to refuse indigo cultivation.
            ● 1860: Publication of the play "Nil Darpan" by Dinabandhu Mitra and newspaper "Hindoo
            Patriot" highlighted peasant struggles.
            Outcome:
            ● Formation of the Indigo Commission (1860) Passage of the Indigo Act (1862), restricting planter
            exploitation.
            ● Indigo cultivation shifted to Bihar, and later declined due to synthetic dyes developed in
            Germany.
            Pabna Agrarian Leagues (1870–1880)
            Overview:
            ● Location: East Bengal region.
            ● Cause:
            ○ Zamindars raised rents beyond legally permissible limits and prevented tenant
            ownership rights, violating the 1859 Rent Act.
            ● Key Leaders:
            ○ Ishan Chandra Roy, Shambhu Nath Pal, and Khodi Mulla formed agrarian leagues to
            resist oppression.
            
            Outcome:
            ● Government suppressed the movement but initiated an inquiry.
            ● Culminated in the Bengal Tenancy Act of 1885, defining landlord-tenant rights.
            Deccan Peasant Uprisings (1860s–1870s)
            In the late 19th century, peasants in the Deccan region faced harsh taxation under the Ryotwari System.
            Causes:
            ● Economic issues worsened by:
            ○ Consecutive bad harvests.
            ○ Crash in cotton prices after the American Civil War.
            ○ 50% rise in land revenue demand (1867).
            ○ Exploitation by moneylenders.
            Tactics:
            ● Peasants adopted social boycotts against moneylenders and later turned to agrarian riots
            targeting moneylenders properties and public destruction of debt receipts and documents.
            Outcome:
            ● Passage of the Deccan Agriculturists Relief Act (1879), protecting peasants from excessive debts
            and exploitation.
            Bijolia Peasant Movement (1897–1941)
            ● Location: Bijolia, Mewar (feudal estate).
            ● Causes: Heavy tax burden on peasants by jagirdars, including Talwar Bandhi Tax (successor tax)
            and Chouthan Tax (tax on human trade).
            ● Key Leaders: Vijay Singh Pathik, Manakya Lal Verma, and Sadhu Sitaram Das.
            ● Outcome: Agreement in 1922 reduced taxes, but unrest continued until independence.
            Kisan Sabha Movement (1919 - 1922)
            ● Location - United Provinces
            ● Key Leaders: Gauri Shankar Mishra, Madan Mohan Malaviya, and Baba Ramchandra led
            peasants and organised them under Kisan Sabhas and thus founded the United Provinces Kisan
            Sabha in 1918. Later, Awadh Kisan Sabha was formed in 1920
            
            ● Objectives: Abolish forced gifts (Nazrana), unpaid labor (Begar), and unjust evictions.
            ● Outcome: Peasant demands incorporated into the Awadh Rent (Amendment) Act (1921).
            Eka Movement (1921–1922)
            ● Location: Hardoi, Bahraich, and Sitapur districts (UP).
            ● Demands: Pay only recorded rent and resist land eviction with the right to refuse forced labour.
            Moplah Uprising (1921)
            ● Region: Malabar, Kerala.
            ● Cause: In the 19th century, British legislations granted landlords, mostly upper caste Hindus,
            exclusive legal land ownership, stripping Mophlas (Malyali Muslims) of their long standing formal
            and traditional rights.
            ● Activity - 1920 NCM sparked the revolt. Driven Muslim cleric's intense speeches and anti British
            feelings, Mophlas initiated a violent uprising against British and Hindu landlords.
            Outcome:
            ● Violent communal uprising, leading to suppression by the Malabar Special Force.
            Bardoli Satyagraha (1926–1932)
            ● Leader: Vallabhbhai Patel, earned the title Sardar.
            ● Cause: 30% land revenue hike in Surat, Gujarat.
            ● Tactics: Peasants withheld taxes, formed worker camps, and published Bardoli Patrika. Social
            Boycott was imposed on those opposing the movement.
            ● Outcome: The hike was withdrawn, strengthening the non-cooperation movement.
            All India Kisan Congress/Sabha (1936)
            ● Formation: Established in Lucknow, under Swami Sahajanand Saraswati. Originated from
            Bihar Provincial Kisan Sabha.
            Released a “Kisan Manifesto” and a periodical under Indulal Yagnik advocating for -
            ● Abolish the zamindari system.
            ● Reduce land revenue.
            ● Transfer land to actual tillers.
            
            Leaders:
            ● N.G. Ranga, R.M. Lohia, and others.
            Initially adhering to Gandhian principles, All India Kisan Sabha eventually gravitated towards the Socialist
            faction within the Indian National Congress. (Logical thing - I hope you understand Why?)
            Tebhaga Movement (1946)
            Region:
            ● North Bengal
            Course of Action -
            ● In Sep 1946 Bengal Provincial Kisan Sabha initiated a mass movement. Their goal was to
            implement the Flood Commission’s Tebhaga recommendations with the objective to ensure that
            Bargardars (sharecroppers) received 2/3rd of the harvest.
            ● Movement supported by communist cadres and urban student militias.
            Outcome:
            ● Movement faded after the Bargardari Bill was introduced.
            
            Revolutionary Movement of Indian Independence
            Phases of the Revolutionary Movement
            1. Phase 1: Pre-World War I
            ○ Focused on individual acts of defiance, primarily inspired by religious and cultural symbols.
            2. Phase 2: Post-World War I
            ○ Marked by coordinated group activities and a blend of nationalism and socialism, largely
            influenced by the RussSian Revolution.
            Reasons Behind the Growth of Revolutionary Movement
            ● Failure of Existing Political Trends:
            ○ The inability of moderate approaches to make headway with colonial authorities drove
            increased support for radical revolutionary methods.
            ● Inspiration from Indian Ideologues:
            ○ Aurobindo Ghosh: Through “Bande Mataram,” he advocated militant nationalism,
            emphasizing spiritual revival, cultural renewal, and armed resistance.
            ○ Vinayak Savarkar: His book “Indian War of Independence” celebrated the concept of
            revolutionary martyrdom.
            ○ Swami Vivekananda: His teachings on spiritual resilience, cultural pride, and social
            reform indirectly inspired a revolutionary mindset among the youth.
            ● Influence of International Movements:
            ○ The Irish Nationalist Movement, Ethiopia’s defeat of Italy in 1896, and Japan’s victory
            over Russia in 1905 demonstrated that colonial powers could be resisted and defeated.
            ● Economic and Social Conditions in India:
            ○ Widespread poverty, social disparity, and economic hardship created a ripe environment
            for revolutionary sentiments to take root.
            ● Attitude of Colonial Rule:
            ○ ◦ The exploitative and repressive attitude of the British government amplified resentment
            and fuelled the revolutionary movement.
            
            Prominent Revolutionary Activities in India
            Maharashtra
            ● Vasudev Phadke: Known as the “Father of Armed Indian Rebellion,” He organised insurgent
            activities in response to the Deccan Famine (1876-77). Forming a band of 40 people, he
            conducted raids and robberies to fund a rebellion.
            ● Chapekar Brothers (Poona Conspiracy Case):
            ○ ◦ During the 1897 Bubonic Plague outbreak in Bombay and Poona, they were outraged by
            the oppressive plague control measures. In retaliation, they assassinated plague
            commissioner Rand and his military escort Lt. Ayrest, marking the first significant
            instance of aggressive nationalism post-1857.
            ● VD Savarkar and Abhinav Bharat:
            ○ Formation: Founded the Mitra Mela Secret Society, which evolved into the Abhinav
            Bharat group in 1904. They set up bomb-making centers in Nasik, Poona, and Bombay.
            ○ Activities: VD Savarkar secretly sent pistols from London, used to assassinate Nasik
            magistrate Jackson (Nasik Conspiracy Case). His book, 1857: First War of Independence,
            was influential, reprinted later by revolutionaries like Bhagat Singh, Lala Hardyal, and
            Rash Behari Bose.
            Bengal
            ● Anushilan Samiti and Yugantar Group:
            ○ The origins of militant nationalism in Bengal trace back to early 20th-century societies like
            the Anushilan Samiti founded by Satishchandra Bose, Jatindranath Banerji, and others.
            ○ Influential Publications:
            ■ Bhawani Mandir (Aurobindo Ghosh): Written during the Partition of Bengal and
            inspired by Anandmath, later used as evidence in the Alipore Bomb case.
            ■ Yugantar (Barindrakumar Ghosh): Advocated openly for sedition and aimed to
            build a revolutionary mindset.
            ● Dacca Anushilan Samiti and Yugantar Split (1906):
            ○ Anushilan Samiti split into the Dacca Anushilan Samiti in East Bengal, led by Pulin Behari
            Das, and Yugantar established itself as a subgroup of the Calcutta Anushilan under
            Barindrakumar Ghosh and Bhupendranath Dutta.
            ● Notable Activities:
            
            ○ Muzaffarpur Murders (1908): A failed attempt in Muzaffarpur to assassinate Judge
            Kingsford (Judge in the case of Yugantar trails) by bomb throwers Khudiram Bose and
            Prafulla Chaki.
            ■ This assassination led to search for illicit arms led to multiple arrests, including
            Aurobindo and Barindra Ghose, who were tried in Alipore Conspiracy case, 1908
            (Aka Maniktala Bomb Conspiracy)
            ○ Following this, the revolutionary movement in Bengal became decentralized but
            remained active. The appointment of an Indian law member in the Viceroy’s executive
            council was seen as a result of this pressure.
            Revolutionary Activities in Other Regions
            ● Tirunelveli Conspiracy Case (1911): Vanchinathan Iyer assassinated Robert Ashe, the British
            officer responsible for ordering shootings in Tirunelveli.
            ● Delhi Conspiracy Case (1912): Rash Behari Bose and Sachin Sanyal attempted to assassinate
            Viceroy Hardinge in Delhi.
            International Revolutionary Activities before WW1
            India House (London)
            ● Established by Shyamji Krishna Varma (A Great revolutionary from Maharashtra who fled to
            England escaping his punishment in India for involvement in Rand Murder Case (Poona Conspiracy
            Case)) in 1905 to propagate revolutionary ideas through the publication Indian Sociologist and
            provide scholarships to radical Indian youth for training.
            ● Madan Lal Dhingra (1909): One of the associates of Indian House, Madan Lal Dhingra
            assassinated Curzon Wyllie in London, a British official, becoming the first Indian revolutionary
            to be executed on Foreign soil.
            Ghadar Movement (USA)
            ● Based in San Francisco, the Ghadar Party published The Ghadar and advocated rebellion
            among Indian soldiers. The Party consisted mainly of Ex-soldiers and peasants who had
            migrated from Punjab to USA in search of better job opportunities. In 1913, Ghadar Party was
            formed with Baba Sohan Singh Bhakhna as elected President, Lala Hardyal as General
            Secretary.
            ● Key Events: - Komagata Maru Incident (Shore Committee)and WWI intensified Ghadar's
            
            revolutionary zeal, with leaders like GD Kumar and Taraknath Das initiating reform publications
            like Swadesh Sevak and forming United India House in Seattle.
            Berlin Committee (Germany)
            ● Revolutionaries like Virendranath Chattopadhyay and Lala Hardyal formed the Berlin
            Committee (AKA Indian Independence Committee), securing German support to fuel uprisings in
            India.
            ● Zimmerman Plan: Junagatar Group of Bengal arranged to import German Arms and ammunition
            during WWI with the help of Berlin Committee, Bagha Jatin and Rashbehari Bose took charge to
            bring about an All India insurrection in Upper India in what is known as the ‘German Plot’ or the
            Zimmerman Plan.
            ○ The Plan was to organise a guerrilla force to start an uprising in the country with the
            seizure of Fort William and a mutiny by armed forces. Unfortunately, the plan was
            exposed by an infiltrator leading to a failed campaign.(Balasore Betrayal)
            Silk Letter Movement
            ● Reshami Rumal Andolan was a collaboration between Deobandi leaders, the Ottoman Empire,
            Afghanistan, and Germany to destabilize British rule in India.
            Provisional Indian Government (Kabul)
            ● Led by Raja Mahendra Pratap Singh, Barkatullah, and others, aiming to recruit Indian soldiers
            and rally support from abroad in 1915 by setting up a Provisional Indian Government in Kabul.
            Singapore Mutiny (1915)
            ● Muslim soldiers stationed in Singapore rebelled against deployment to fight the Ottoman
            Empire, and were later executed after suppression of the mutiny.
            Revolutionary Activities After World War I
            Hindustan Republican Association (HRA)
            ● Founded by Sachindranath Sanyal, the HRA unified revolutionary groups across India.
            ● Kakori Conspiracy (1925): Train robbery aimed to fund HRA activities. After the Kakori incident,
            HSRA adopted socialism, led by Bhagat Singh and Chandrashekhar Azad, leading the
            transformation to Hindustan Socialist Republican Association (HSRA)
            ● Notable Activities: Saunders’ assassination in response to Lala Lajpat Rai’s death, and
            bomb-throwing in the Central Legislative Assembly to protest civil liberty restrictions. (Public
            
            Safety Bill & Trade Disputes Bill were being discussed when harmless bombs were thrown)
            ○ Bhagat Singh formed ‘Naujawan Bharat Sabha’ in 1926, and while in Prison penned
            down ‘Why I am an Atheist’
            Revolutionaries from Bengal
            ● Chittagong Armoury Raid (1929): Surya Sen attempted to seize arms and inspire an uprising in
            Chittagong leading to British severe suppression and crackdown on a network of revolutionaries.
            ○ Pritilata Wadedar along with Kalpana Dutt participated in Chittagong Armoury Raid along
            with Surya Sen.
            ● Bina Das: Attempted to assassinate Bengal Governor Stanley Jackson at a Calcutta University
            convocation.
            Important Writings
            ● Bandi Jeevan , A life of Captivity book written by Sachindranath Sanyal. is called as the Bible
            of revolutionaries.
            ● Pathar Dabi - Sharatchandra Chatterjee
            Reasons for Decline of Revolutionary Activities
            ● Government Repression: Stringent laws like the Defence of India Act (1915) and the Rowlatt
            Act (1919) aimed to curb revolutionary activities.
            ● Lack of Coordination and Unified Leadership: Revolutionary efforts remained fragmented
            across regions.
            ● Financial Constraints: Limited resources hindered the movement's sustainability.
            ● Shift to Alternative Strategies: The rise of mass movements, such as Gandhi’s Non Cooperation,
            provided new avenues for resistance.
            
            Communalism in British India
            Manifestation of Communalism in Significant Events During British Rule
            ● Anti-Congress Appeal (1887)
            ○ Formation of United Indian Patriotic Association: Syed Ahmed Khan founded this
            association in 1887 to attack the Indian National Congress.
            ○ Appeal to Muslims: Syed Ahmed Khan urged educated Muslims to stay away from
            Congress, although some Muslims did join the organization.
            ● Partition of Bengal (1905)
            ○ Objective: The British divided Bengal to weaken Bengali nationalism by creating separate
            Hindu and Muslim regions, intensifying communal identities.
            ● Formation of the Muslim League (1906)
            ○ British Support: The All-India Muslim League was established in 1906 under British
            patronage.
            ○ Leadership: Elite leaders like Aga Khan played a significant role in its formation.
            ○ Purpose: The League aimed to divert Muslim youth from Congress and laid the
            groundwork for Muslim separatism.
            ● Separate Electorates (1909)
            ○ Introduction by Morley-Minto Reforms: The 1909 reforms institutionalized separate
            electorates in India.
            ○ Impact: It deepened communal divisions and hampered Congress’s role as a unified
            nationalist body.
            ● The Khilafat Movement (1919-1924)
            ○ Focus on the Ottoman Caliphate: The movement aligned Muslim political interests with
            preserving the Caliphate, emphasizing religious identity over national unity.
            ○ Result: This alignment heightened communalism in India.
            ● Nehru Report (1928)
            ○ Proposal: The Nehru Report suggested a bicameral legislature without separate
            electorates.
            ○ Reaction: This alienated Jinnah from Congress, leading to his Fourteen Points that
            further advanced communal demands.
            
            ● Towards Mass Communalism (Post-1937)
            ○ Challenges for the League: Despite securing the Fourteen Points, the Muslim League
            struggled with limited support among Muslims.
            ● Dual Strategy:
            ○ Reduced membership fees and focused on socio-economic issues to attract broader
            support.
            ○ Accused Congress ministries of favoritism towards Hindus and discrimination against
            Muslims, deepening the Hindu-Muslim divide.
            ● Pakistan Resolution (1940)
            ○ Demand for Pakistan: At its Lahore session, the Muslim League passed the Pakistan
            Resolution, calling for the creation of an independent Muslim state comprising
            Muslim-majority regions.
            British Policies
            ● Support for the League:
            ○ The Cripps’ Proposals (1942) legitimized the Pakistan demand by offering provincial
            autonomy, strengthening the League’s position.
            ○ Failure of the Cabinet Mission Plan (1946), which rejected Pakistan, led to heightened
            communal tensions.
            ○ Role in Partition: British backing of the League’s demands significantly influenced the
            partition process.
            ● Direct Action Day (1946)
            ○ Trigger: The Muslim League’s rejection of the Cabinet Mission Plan.
            ○ Outcome: On 16th August 1946, communal violence erupted in Calcutta, spreading to
            other parts of India and leading to widespread riots.
            Culmination of Communalism
            ● The growth of communalism and its manifestations during British rule culminated in the
            Two-Nation Theory, eventually leading to the Partition of India.
            
            Leftist Movement in India National Struggle
            Origins and Influences
            ● Emergence: Rooted in the expansion of modern industries and inspired by socialist movements
            in Great Britain and Russia.
            ● M.N. Roy's Role: Influenced by the Bolshevik Revolution, M.N. Roy founded the Communist
            Party of India (CPI) in exile (1920) in Tashkent, Soviet Russia, with support from the Communist
            International.
            ● Military School: Established in Tashkent to train Indian frontier tribes for resistance against
            British rule.
            Key Events
            ● Peshawar Conspiracy Case (1922):
            ○ Trainees from Tashkent, upon returning to India, were arrested and tried for spreading
            communist ideas.
            ○ Leaders Mohammed Akbar and Gauhar Rahman Khan were sentenced to rigorous
            imprisonment.
            ● Formation of CPI in India (1925):
            ○ Kanpur Conference (1924): Marked a significant step toward forming an all-India
            communist party.
            ○ Key Figures: Nalini Gupta, Mujaffar Ahmed, and Singaravelu (elected president).
            ○ By 1930, CPI affiliated with the Communist International and released its first publication,
            The Communist.
            Workers’ and Peasants’ Parties (WPP)
            ● Formation and Activities (1925-1929):
            ○ Functioned within the Indian National Congress (INC) and served as CPI's front
            organizations.
            ○ Prominent regional parties included:
            ■ Labour Swaraj Party (Bengal, 1925).
            ■ Kirti-Kisan Party (Punjab, 1926).
            ■ Bombay Workers’ and Peasants’ Party (Maharashtra, 1927).
            
            ■ WPPs in the United Province (1928).
            ● Decline:
            ○ Repression: Severe Government actions
            ○ Isolation: Following Communist International's directives, CPI severed ties with INC,
            labeling it bourgeoisie, leading to WPP’s disintegration.
            Reorganization of CPI (1934-1935)
            ● Leadership of P.C. Joshi:
            ○ Reorganized the Communist Party and forged alliances with socialists and INC.
            ○ Communists operated both underground and within the Congress Socialist Party (CSP).
            Formation of Congress Socialist Party (CSP)
            ● Reason: Disillusionment with Gandhi’s methods led young Congress members to embrace
            Marxism.
            ● Establishment: Formed in Bombay (October 1934) by Jayaprakash Narayan, Acharya Narendra
            Dev, and Minoo Masani.
            ● Objectives:
            ○ Socialism in India.
            ○ Land reforms, workers’ rights, abolition of princely states, women’s emancipation, and
            national independence.
            Impact
            ● While the Leftist movement significantly influenced Indian politics, repression and isolation
            reduced its momentum, forcing Communists to align with broader nationalist efforts.
            Workers’ Movement in India
            Early Initiatives by Individuals and Groups
            ● Sasipada Banerjee (Bengal):
            ○ Established the “Working Men’s Club” (1870).
            ○ Published the monthly journal Bharat Shramjibi in Bengali (1874).
            ● Sorabji Shapoorji Bengali (Bombay):
            ○ Led the first organized labour agitation (1875) to improve working conditions for women
            
            and children.
            ○ Result: Appointment of the First Factory Commission (1875) and passing of the First
            Factories Act (1881).
            ○ Highlighted mill workers' conditions through his newspaper, Rast Goftar.
            ● N.M. Lokhande (Bombay):
            ○ Founded the Bombay Millhands’ Association for cotton mill workers.
            ○ Published the Marathi journal Dinbandhu (1898).
            ● N.M. Joshi:
            ○ Established the Social Service League (1911), providing training for volunteers in disaster
            relief and welfare programmes.
            ● Kamgar Hitvardhak Sabha:
            ○ Formed by S.K. Bole and others to assist workers.
            Factories Act of 1881 (Under Lord Ripon):
            ○ Key focus: Regulation of child labour (ages 7-12).
            ○ Provisions:
            ■ Ban on employing children under 7 years.
            ■ Limitation of children’s work hours to 9 hours/day.
            ■ Four holidays per month for children.
            ■ Fencing hazardous machinery.
            Factories Act of 1891:
            ○ Focused on women and child labour.
            ○ Provisions:
            ■ 9 hours/day work limit for children (ages 9-14).
            ■ 11 hours/day work limit for female labourers.
            ■ Half-hour meal break daily.
            ■ One compulsory rest day per week.
            
            Factories Act of 1911:
            ○ Influenced by Lancashire mill owners.
            ○ Provisions:
            ■ Limited adult males' work hours in textile factories to 12 hours/day.
            ■ Maximum 6 hours/day work limit for children.
            
            
            Development of Education during British India
            Introduction
            ● Until the late 18th century, the English East India Company, being a commercial enterprise,
            focused solely on trade and profit, avoiding involvement in educational initiatives.
            ● Efforts to promote education were primarily led by evangelists and missionaries.
            Efforts of Evangelists
            ● Missionary Objectives: Evangelical missionaries aimed to spread Christianity in India, often
            attributing social issues to Indian religious practices.
            ○ Cultural Perception: Indian culture was viewed as less developed compared to British
            culture.
            ○ Educational Approach: Promoted English education to instill moral values, focusing on
            evangelization over practical learning.
            ● Challenges and Achievements:
            ○ The East India Company resisted supporting missionary activities until 1813.
            ○ Notable missionaries like William Carey, Joshua Marshman, and William Ward:
            ■ Established a mission in Serampore (Danish territory).
            ■ Founded a printing press (1800) and Serampore College (1818), indirectly
            advancing modern education in India.
            Phase of Orientalists
            ● Emergence of Orientalism:
            ○ Towards the end of the 18th century, political and economic needs compelled the British
            to promote education among Indians.
            ○ Orientalists supported pro-native education, fearing rejection of Western education.
            ● Orientalist Contributions:
            ○ Study of Asian Cultures: Focused on ancient languages, literature, and philosophies.
            
            The Charter Act of 1813
            ● Significance:
            ○ Marked the first time the East India Company took responsibility for education.
            ○ Allowed allocation of one lakh rupees annually for educational purposes.
            ○ Permitted missionaries to engage in educational and proselytizing activities.
            ● Delays:
            ○ Educational initiatives were delayed; funds were not utilized until 1823 when the General
            Committee of Public Instruction was established.
            Committee of Public Instruction, 1823
            ● Led by: Lord Macaulay.
            ● Actions (1823–1833):
            ○ Dominated by Orientalists.
            ○ Established Sanskrit College, Calcutta (1824).
            ○ Set up additional colleges in Agra and Delhi.
            ● Orientalist vs Anglicist Debate:
            ○ Divided into two factions:
            ■ Orientalists: Focused on preserving Indian traditions and classical education.
            ■ Anglicists: Advocated Western-style education and English as the medium.
            Anglicist-Orientalist Controversy
            ● Represented differences in:
            ○ Mode and medium of education.
            ○ Broader aspects like Indian society’s future and the role of tradition vs modernity.
            ● Proponents:
            
            ○ Orientalists: Sir William Jones, Warren Hastings, James Prinsep.
            ○ Anglicists: Thomas Macaulay, Charles Grant, William Wilberforce.
            ● Focus:
            ○ Orientalists: Emphasized Indian scriptures and languages like Sanskrit, Persian.
            ○ Anglicists: Prioritized science, mathematics, and contemporary education in English.
            Macaulay’s Minute (1835)
            ● Declared English education as the government’s objective.
            ● Impacts:
            ○ Abolished Persian as the court language; English replaced it.
            ○ Emphasis on Western education led to the decline of traditional learning.
            ○ Led to Downward Filtration Theory, aiming to educate higher classes for indirect benefits
            to lower classes.
            Wood’s Dispatch (1854)
            ● Referred to as the Magna Carta of Indian Education.
            ● Key Provisions:
            ○ Advocated for vernacular languages in primary schools and English for higher education.
            ○ Stressed secular education, vocational training, and women’s education.
            ○ Introduced grants-in-aid to private enterprises.
            ● Results:
            ○ Department of Public Instruction (1855).
            ○ Establishment of universities in Calcutta, Madras, Bombay (1857).
            Development of Education under Crown Rule
            Post-1858 Changes
            ● Transitioned from Company rule to British Crown, leading to centralization of education.
            ● 1870 Policy by Lord Mayo:
            ○ Provincial governments controlled education with constraints:
            ■ University legislation was under the Government of India.
            ■ Education expenditure was drawn from assigned revenues.
            
            ○ Result: Limited resources at the provincial level hindered primary and secondary
            education.
            Hunter Commission (1882–83)
            ● Purpose:
            ○ Assess implementation of the 1854 Wood’s Dispatch.
            ○ Evaluate state-sponsored education, focusing on primary and secondary levels.
            ● Recommendations:
            ○ Strengthen primary education in vernacular languages.
            ○ Transfer control of primary education to district and municipal boards.
            ○ Divide secondary education into:
            ■ Literary stream: Prepares for university.
            ■ Vocational stream: For commercial careers.
            ○ Emphasize female education and private engagement in education.
            ● Outcome:
            ○ Accepted in 1884, delegating primary education to local authorities.
            ○ Promoted private schools and established universities like Punjab (1882) and Allahabad
            (1887).
            Raleigh Commission (1902)
            ● Established to regulate universities, addressing concerns of nationalist propaganda.
            ● Focused on:
            ○ Current state of universities.
            ○ Proposals for future reforms.
            ● Outcome: Recommendations led to the Indian Universities Act (1904).
            Indian Universities Act (1904)
            ● Reforms:
            ○ Enhanced focus on study and research.
            ○ Reduced tenure for university governing members.
            ○ Instituted stricter inspections for affiliated colleges.
            
            ○ Introduced government veto power over university regulations.
            Saddler University Commission (1917–19)
            ● Comprehensive review from school to university education.
            ● Recommendations:
            ○ 12-year school course, with a 3-year university degree system.
            ○ Establish separate boards for secondary and intermediate education.
            ○ Encourage female education, vocational training, and centralization of universities.
            Education under Dyarchy (1921–1937)
            ● 1921 Reforms:
            ○ Provincial governments are given more autonomy in education.
            ○ Primary Education Acts passed in Bombay (1923), Assam (1926), U.P. (1926), and Bengal
            (1930).
            ● Secondary Education: Rapid expansion, doubling school numbers by 1937.
            ● Promoted modern vernaculars as the medium of instruction.
            Wardha Scheme of Basic Education (1937)
            ● Proposed by Mahatma Gandhi:
            ○ Focused on:
            ■ Craft-based, self-supporting education.
            ■ Use of mother tongue as the medium of instruction.
            ● Outcome:
            ○ Supported by a national conference but halted due to World War II.
            Sargent Plan of Education (1944)
            ● Aimed for post-war reforms:
            ○ Pre-primary (3–6 years) and compulsory elementary education (6–11 years).
            ○ High school categorized into academic and vocational streams.
            ○ 20-year plan to eradicate adult illiteracy.
            ○ Emphasis on teacher training and specialized education.
            ○ Impact: Became a guideline for future reforms despite political constraints.
